#10574. Playing GTA 6 this weekend? Here's 16 spoiler-free tips for your first playthrough

It's launch weekend for GTA 6 - in case you hadn't noticed - and since I've played the best part of a hundred hours of this thing, I thought it might be useful to share a few pieces of advice from my time with it.

Many of these are most applicable for the early game, but there are a few that'll be worth bearing in mind throughout. After last night's driving around I would also maybe add in a quick bonus one, come to think of it: watch out for cyclists making last-minute left turns. Or don't, actually. Half of this lot deserve it…

The below list is spoiler-free, so you can relax if you're anxious about going through the story entirely fresh. Though naturally I do explain the way some parts of this game work, taking away some of the sense of discovery. You can take my word for it that there's plenty more to discover beyond this though, so worry not.

If you'd like even more help navigating Leonida, then check out our wonderful guide team's GTA 6 walkthrough.

The number one thing I'd tell people to do in GTA 6 is to create lots of save files and, as you progress and start needing to overwrite them, to try to keep a roughly even spread of files through each chapter. There are various reasons for this - for one, while the game autosaves, it doesn't seem to do that successfully if you suddenly lose power to the console or witness a rare game crash, so it'll preserve your progress. More than that: GTA's story and missions are surprisingly, perhaps mysteriously reactive. Quite a lot can differ, and so being able to go back and make different choices, or play the game differently, will be something 'future you' likely appreciates.

In the very early game you won't have access to a bank account, but once you do: you should put every bit of cash you have in there as soon as you can. Cash is lost on death or arrest - which can happen quite often in GTA 6, funnily enough - and there's no real benefit to carrying it around. Just about everything, besides in-game strippers, can be paid for via your BuckMe account directly. And there's a relatively low $20,000 limit you really don't want to hit in the middle of raiding a gang hideout, either.

GTA 6 has a luxurious range of customisation settings, from hiding notifications about your relationship to reinstating the old 'tap to sprint' control scheme. The accessibility settings - while crucially I'm not an expert - seem considerable. And there are somewhat disguised difficult settings here around enemy damage taken and encounters with the police.

Fairly early in the game you'll gain access to the Scooter Bros. App, which lets you repair or summon your owned vehicles at will. Crucially, there's also an option to pay a one-off fee to unlock three free uses of the app per day. At $12,000, in the early game this is a lot of dosh, but once you rack up that kind of money after advancing through the game a little, it's well worth the initial cost. The sooner you unlock it, the more value it provides over time on a cost-per-use basis. And trust me when I say: as the game's intensity ramps up, you will probably find yourself needing to repair and recover your vehicles quite often. (Also, in Chapter 1, before you get access to the app, you can access the same service by going to your phone's contacts and calling Joey - handy!)

Rockstar has somewhat disincentivised it by adding a cost and putting it in your phone's apps rather than the main map screen, but you can fast travel around Leonida if you ever want or need to. This is done via the RideMe Uber-like app. It's not cheap - you'll need to pay both the fee for the journey and an additional $100 to skip that journey and instantly arrive at your destination, but in certain scenarios it might prove useful. To skip the journey, hold L2/LT once in the cab, and then hold down Triangle/Y when the option is shown. However…

While it's possible to journey-skip your way around this world, having tested that first hand I'd advise against it. It might be obvious, but GTA 6 is genuinely designed around you manually travelling around, typically in the car. If you skip all your commutes between missions or locations, you'll miss out on the game's dozens and dozens of emergent events, which lead to anything from one-off jokes to entire mission chains. The car is also, in a way, kind of the heart of the entire game. Relish the intentional slowness!

Again, avoiding spoilers here, but in the mid game the 'true' loop of GTA 6 gameplay will properly reveal itself, in all the many methods of earning money and the increased emphasis on doing so. You can of course play the game however you like, but having reached the end and looked back from that point, I found that if there was a particular moment to set aside as the time for aimlessly exploring the world, it was from around Chapter 3 onwards. You'll have enough tools to be able to see it at more or less its fullest then, but also won't have missed anything essential by waiting until that point to take your foot off the pedal either.

A handy early-game tip here that even I missed when I was playing, but have since discovered: two islands in the small cluster of them to the south-west of Jason's early game home each house a really useful weapon, in a silenced pistol and a handy shotgun. You'll own these permanently once found, and so they can be incredibly helpful for navigating missions with maximum or minimal violence. Look for shipwrecks on those islands to find them. The pistol is hidden under a little hatch on the deck of a shipwreck on the southernmost point of the south-easternmost island. The shotgun is next to a small wooden wrecked boat around the middle of the northernmost island.

A mechanic I slightly underused at first was the ability to 'save' a given outfit to your car. You can do this either from your car's trunk or from the wardrobe, and it's hugely useful as you start to get into more elaborate scrapes with the police (and also if you just like to role-play a bit and don the right outfit for the right occasion).

There are lots of reasons for this, from the moments of intimacy it affords you with your partner, to the extra buffs you can get from resting. Sleep at home, for instance, and you can then immediately pet your snake (ahem), down a coffee or drink from the fridge, grab an apple and take a shower for a range of buffs across health, stamina, and focus. Each of these will last quite some time, so it's worth the few minutes of time to do them. Eating and drinking regularly also helps you maximise gains in the gym.

Rockstar seems to enjoy the concept of a game within a game, and that's exemplified well by the in-game phone. You can find missions via scrolling Snapmatic, it's in-game social media, or from texting various contacts you pick up as the game progresses. It's worth checking it every now and then just to see if anything interesting - or just funny or absurd - might pop up.

Car trunks, both those belonging to the public and the police, are often well worth a look. Even if you're quickly nabbing a car for a short-term dash, take a moment to check the boot for anything worthwhile. We've found everything from sellable narcotics to very useful weapons in there. In cop cars, that goes up several notches, too.

A small thing, but in-mission checkpoints are relatively generous in GTA 6. If you really wanted to complete a mission a certain way - undetected or non-violent, say - then don't be afraid to quickly re-load rather than feeling you need to manually load up an earlier save, or give up on your goal entirely. It's also helped me out with a few minor progress-jamming bugs along the way too.

This might seem obvious, but it's worth emphasising for early-game players: it's very, very rare that you actually need to kill anybody in GTA 6. Enemies are fully incapacitated when you see a yellow hit marker over your crosshairs, even if that just comes from blasting away at their toes. If you make a habit of navigating combat this way then that also makes it much easier to recover your 'Professional' criminal status if you ever do decide to go full rampage. Though you can, of course, ultimately play this game however you like. Just be aware there may be consequences, known and unknown, as you go. Also: don't forget to click the right analog stick to aim down sights - it's an easy thing to forget when playing a third-person shooter like this, and not always the best way to operate, but incredibly helpful in select circumstances.

Throughout the game you'll have a 'family savings' goal to hit. As you progress you'll find more ways to earn money much faster, and so it's easy to make up for lost time here if, later on, you decide that actually you do want to think about the future after all. But that grind will be a fair bit less painful if you chip away at the target as you go. As long as you save yourself some fun money - a few hundred in the early game, few thousand in the mid game, and a few tens of thousands late on - then you'll have enough to do key things like buying new garages to register vehicles, or paying to register them themselves.

Throughout your time with GTA 6 you'll encounter moments when you need to make a choice. Interestingly, I noticed at least a few occasions where a scenario changed when I just waited, doing nothing at all. Sometimes that might mean a choice is made for you - so waiting is a risk - but on other occasions new options become available. Sometimes they might mean a genuinely different consequence, others they might just lead to characters reacting in a certain kind of way. But it's worth experimenting - your first options aren't always your only ones.

#10572. Influencer in tears as burglars steal “absolutely everything” days after winning 4-way boxing match

Colombian influencer Yaya Muñoz broke down in tears after discovering burglars had ransacked her home, stealing her jewelry and savings just days after she won a four-way influencer boxing match.

The 33-year-old content creator, whose real name is Dahian Lorena Muñoz, is best known for appearing on Colombia’s version of Celebrity Big Brother, La Casa de los Famosos.

On November 22, Muñoz shared a series of emotional Instagram Stories showing the aftermath of a burglary at her home in Medellín, Colombia.

“How is it possible that I come home, where there is supposed to be so much security, and it turns out I’ve been robbed?” she questioned, according to El Tiempo.

“They took absolutely everything,” she added.

In the footage, Muñoz showed empty jewelry boxes and revealed that the thieves had broken into her safe, stealing cash she had been saving.

“I can’t believe this. They took absolutely all my jewelry. My God, how can people be so evil?” she said through tears.

Muñoz also showed police around her home, suggesting the burglars may have entered through a window after noticing that a curtain had been moved while the door remained locked.

She later confirmed that authorities were investigating, adding that it still hurt to lose possessions she had worked so hard to acquire.

#INSEGURIDAD 🚨 La creadora de contenido Yaya Muñoz denunció a través de sus redes sociales que delincuentes ingresaron a su vivienda en la ciudad de Medellín y le vaciaron su caja fuerte, llevándose joyas y dinero en efectivo pese a las estrictas medidas de seguridad del lugar. pic.twitter.com/Ook9mOBDaq

The burglary comes just over a week after Muñoz emerged victorious at Stream Fighters 5, a major influencer boxing event organized by popular Kick streamer WestCOL on November 14.

Held at Bogotá’s Coliseo MedPlus, the event featured an unusual four-person Royal Rumble-style boxing match involving Muñoz, Karola Alcendra, Beba de la Cruz, and Alexa Torrex.

Rather than having all four women fight simultaneously, the contest featured a series of rotating one-on-one rounds, with the winner ultimately determined by the judges.

The fight produced a viral moment when Beba appeared to dislocate her shoulder and attempted to put it back into place in the ring before eventually withdrawing.

Following her victory, Muñoz embraced Karola, putting an end to a feud between the two influencers that had lasted for months.

The event was the latest installment of WestCOL’s influencer boxing series, which has previously broken Kick viewership records.

Meanwhile, the investigation into Muñoz’s burglary remains ongoing. The influencer has not disclosed the total value of the stolen belongings, and no suspects have been publicly identified.

#10570. I completed GTA 6's indie game darling pastiche, Tenebrae Lux, and all I got was this lousy sense of accomplishment

"We are but Promethean wanderers on the fringes of oblivion, clinging to a semblance of light amidst the vast abyss of our own ignorance. Voyaging uncharted waters may appear foolish to some, yet folly and heroism can often appear the same to the undiscerning eye," so says the voice of Johannes Zuch - creator, writer, director, composer, and 'perceptual architect' behind Leonida's latest favourite indie darling art game, Tenebrae Lux. It's pretentious, it's deeply up its own arse, and it's awkward and off-putting to play. A must-review for Eurogamer.

Developed, seemingly single-handedly, by an eccentric developer called Johannes Zuch - auteur, pseud, charlatan - you first get introduced to this curious little experiment in one of GTA 6's earliest missions, where protagonist Jason is forced to play it during a 'lad's night in' with buddy Cal. Jason is quick to dismiss Tenebrae Lux in company, but once Cal slinks off home - hungover and dreary - I slide the disc back into my JoyStation console and sink my teeth back into it. Jason loves this stuff, really. He only keeps Time Jackers: Rewind installed so his mates don't judge him.

The first thing I notice about Tenebrae Lux is the name. It's Latin for 'darkness and light', or something similar. Is this riffing on Clair Obscur, which is French for 'light dark'? No, it's too cerebral for that - too plodding and navel-gazey. Sandfall Interactive wouldn't be this pretentious. Maybe it's a pastiche of Dear Esther, then, drawing on the tenets and schema of the 'walking simulator' genre and using that washed-out film noir aesthetic. But then, it's too wordy for that, with too many interactive and puzzle-adjacent elements. Ah, it must be mocking The Witness, instead - there's the telltale lighthouse, the 'I'm definitely not telling you where to go and what to do' internal narration, and of course the fingerprints of Johannes Zuch are all over it. Johannes Zuch, Jonathan Blow - see how they braided them together? (Sorry).

Whilst this has been created by Rockstar as a pastiche - as much loving send-up as it is withering take down - and designed to make a quick joke in the early 'Feng Shui' mission, the game is playable from start to finish if you so desire. You boot it up by inserting a physical disc, too, which is funny given the context. And it's good! Well, not good good, but it's a proper brainteaser, and ribs 2016's The Witness with such knowledge and (I think?) respect that it's hard not to be charmed by Rockstar's game-within-a-game. There are collectibles to be found that riff on Mark Z. Danielewski's House of Leaves, too, a knowing nod to the most try-hard of all try-hard literature.

Within seconds of the title screen, too, you're presented with a game-within-a-game-within-a-game. You must clear four boards filled with puzzle pieces as the protagonist mutters something mysterious and weird in his posh voice. Do so, and you unlock the door to the next location. Head up to a bunker, and you'll see a room filled with various clues - maybe it's a flashing lamp flickering in morse code, maybe it's a malfunctioning clock presenting a maths puzzle, maybe it's a pricetag on a record that changes on every visit, maybe it's a photograph with anomalous elements, maybe it's a "Developed Using Zuchworks Engine" easter egg hidden in the environment. You need to keep your wits about you, because one of these clues changes every time you enter - and the clue corresponds to a date that changes on a calendar on the wall.

You can look up a guide if you need to, or you can spend far too long figuring out how all this relates to the telephone you can find in the room on your own, like muggins here, before opening up the path to the next puzzle. Look at the image below to see the level of record-keeping and brainteasing this bizarre little game prompted from me. I will say, the sense of achievement you get from playing around with this stuff is pretty remarkable, given - let's not forget I'm actually playing Grand Theft Auto 6 whilst I'm wandering around in the black and white murk trying to complete a puzzle game for no actual reward.

The bunker room is, by far, the hardest part of Tenebrae Lux. From here, things ease up: you need to figure out the right sequence of glyphs on a bridge, and then put some distance between yourself and a curious and rather creepy suited man that's seemingly figuring things out ahead of you in a location inspired by, I think, The Backrooms (or Portal, or Superliminal, or maybe something else entirely. Who knows what Zuch was thinking?)

"With the last enigma finally mastered," the narrator drones in his bumbling monotone, "I step forward into the void, the shadows swallow, consuming all, and then finally the sweet embrace of darkness of which there is no end." 'You have just experienced a Johannes Zuch experience', Tenebrae Lux tells me, before cutting to the best joke of the whole thing: the credits roll much akin to something Hideo Kojima would produce.

So, what do you get out of all of this? Well - just like real puzzle games in the real world - nothing, really. If you've not completed one of the other three fully playable games in GTA 6, you will get a trophy/achievement. But given the main game is all about Jason and Lucia's desperate scramble to escape the poverty trap, to try and break free of the cycle of debt, violence, and consequence that keeps them clinging to Leonida's seedy underbelly, there's no monetary reward. There's no 'progress' in the sense of the protagonist's shared story.

Even in terms of your own cold, hard, mathematical 'completion' you only need to boot each of the four games up once in order to work towards GTA 6's 100% completion. So what's the point of all this? Why did I spend the better part of 90 minutes trying to figure out how to find a sliver of light in the dark in Tenebrae Lux? The game is a wonderful pastiche in that, essentially, it's not really saying anything. You can probably project some vague meaning onto it if you wish - I'm pretty certain the protagonist literally 'finds himself' at the end - but glib little readings like that are ten-a-penny in this genre.

I think the most impressive thing about Tenebrae Lux is that it exists. You're teased with it in an early mission - Rockstar may as well say 'look, we put a game in a game, lol' - but it's more than that. This is what happens when Rockstar's 4700-plus developers are given the room to… develop. Tenebrae Lux could very easily have been a non-interactive experience, a quick, cheap joke shown over-the-shoulder taking a drive-by pop shot at Johnathan Blow, Hideo Kojima, Dan Pinchbeck, or any and all of the above. Or maybe it could be a 'vertical slice', an incomplete demo, a teaser. But it's not, because nothing about GTA 6 is quick or cheap.

GTA 6, in every single conceivable way, is a premium experience - part-sandbox, part-simulation, part-otherworld. To create not just one, but four fully playable and completable games within that framework is showboating, sure, but to make that game actually good and - perhaps controversially - even better than some of the games it's pastiching… Well, it's just rubbing salt in the wound, really.

A code for the game was provided by Cal Hampton.

#10569. Running an AI Agent on a Local LLM: What I Measured on One GPU

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Running an AI agent on your own GPU sounds simple: point it at Ollama and stop paying per token. I set up OpenClaw, an open-source AI assistant, with a 9B model in Ollama on one RTX 3090 and connected it to Discord. Then I read the Ollama log for every request.

The agent worked. The numbers were not what I expected, though. A one-line question turned into a prompt of more than 18,000 tokens, the first reply took over a minute, and the small model sometimes guessed instead of using its tools.

This article covers what I measured and what each number means if you plan to run an agent on local hardware.

OpenClaw ran in Docker and reached Ollama on the host at http://host.docker.internal:11434. Discord was the chat interface, and OpenClaw's web panel showed the sessions and context use.

This article is about the results, not the installation. The full step-by-step setup is in a separate guide to running OpenClaw in Docker with Ollama and Discord. That guide covers the Compose file, the Discord bot and how to keep the ports off your network.

All timings below come from the Ollama log on the test machine. Ollama loaded the model with a 32,768-token context.

My first message in Discord was short: "Which model are you running on, and what is 17 * 23?" The bot answered correctly: it ran on ollama/qwen3.5:9b, and 17 × 23 is 391.

The Ollama log showed what that question cost. The model processed 18,415 prompt tokens and generated 33 tokens.

The extra tokens come from the agent itself. With every request, OpenClaw sends:

On the RTX 3090, reading that prompt took 10 seconds at 1,838 tokens per second. A slower GPU, or a CPU, needs much longer to read the same prompt.

Prompt tokens per model call, from the Ollama log. Only the first message had to process the whole prompt.

OpenClaw counts tokens with its own estimate, which was higher than the Ollama count. The gateway log estimated the prompt at 24,608 tokens, against a prompt budget of 24,576 tokens for this model. By OpenClaw's own count, the base prompt alone used up the budget before any conversation history.

The web panel showed a similar figure: 68 to 69 percent of the context window was in use for the Discord sessions after only a few messages.

The first message arrived while the model was not loaded in GPU memory. That reply took 1 min 10 s:

Reply times on the test machine. Loading the model took most of the first reply.

The speed-up comes from prompt caching. The system prompt, tool definitions and workspace files at the start of the prompt stay the same between requests. Ollama kept that part cached and processed only the new tokens. The model wrote at 91 to 96 tokens per second in every test.

Ollama's documentation says models stay in memory for 5 minutes by default before they are unloaded. After that, the next message pays the load time again. You can change this with the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE environment variable on the Ollama server, or with the keep_alive parameter per request. A negative value, such as -1, keeps the model loaded. I did not change this setting in the test.

If you run Ollama in Docker, pass the variable with -e OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1 or in the environment: section of your Compose file.

Two things used the GPU besides my chat messages.

A heartbeat. OpenClaw's web panel listed one scheduled automation, heartbeat-main, which runs every 30 minutes. The agent wakes on that schedule, so the model gets called even when nobody chats. With Ollama's 5-minute default, the model is unloaded between heartbeats unless someone chatted recently, so a heartbeat most likely starts with a model load. I did not measure this.

An embedding model. During the first message, OpenClaw also called Ollama's /api/embed endpoint twice to index its memory. It used qwen3-embedding:4b, which was already installed on the Ollama server. I did not set OpenClaw to use that model; it picked it on its own.

The two models took this much GPU memory:

That is 11.4 GB for the models alone, almost half of the RTX 3090's 24 GB.

What this means for you: plan GPU memory for the chat model, the embedding model and the context, not the chat model alone. If the GPU also runs other models, the heartbeat can compete with them.

The first two tests in Discord: a question, then a file written with a tool. The user name is blurred.

The mistakes showed up when the model had to check something:

After the restart, the bot claimed it had lost the earlier session. When asked to use its read tool, it read the file correctly.

The fix for the first problem was simple. When I wrote "use your read tool to open homelab-test.md", the model opened the file and listed all three points correctly. Naming the tool made it check instead of guess.

The model listed four files and missed the test file and a folder, because it answered from its prompt.

Based on this test, a local 9B model on one consumer GPU works for:

If you need better answers, OpenClaw also works with hosted models, but then your prompts leave your machine.

In this test, qwen3.5:9b used 6.3 GB and the qwen3-embedding:4b memory model used 5.1 GB. Together the two models needed 11.4 GB.

The model had to load into GPU memory first, which took 59 seconds of the 70-second reply. Ollama keeps a model loaded for 5 minutes by default, and the OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE setting changes that.

Every request includes OpenClaw's system prompt, its tool definitions and its workspace files. A one-line question became an 18,415-token prompt in this test.

Yes. A heartbeat automation runs every 30 minutes and calls the model even when nobody sends a message.

It handled questions, arithmetic and file creation correctly in this test. It sometimes answered from its prompt instead of using its tools. When it wrongly said its memory was lost, asking it to use its read tool made it check the file and answer correctly.

#10568. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

Materials provided by RIKEN. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10566. It took me less than an hour to find my first incredibly minor Rockstar easter egg in GTA 6 - but it has some big implications for Leonida

Are you wondering how connected the worlds of GTA 5 and GTA 6 are? Well, I've got some news for you. Keep on reading below if you'd like some very minor spoilers for the early game of GTA 6, spoilers for GTA 5, as well as some information about how the new game links to GTA 5 and other Rockstar games.

Right, if you're reading from this point onwards, you're clearly here for the tea. In the first hour or so of GTA 6, when you finally get control of Jason and head to your first property (the green stilted house) in the Leonida Keys, you will be able to explore a little. On my way to do a workout - you need to keep Jason a peak physical instrument for his work's sake, right? Right? - I happened upon a fitness magazine resting on a table on the decking out front.

Called 'Body Copy' - a joke I appreciate as a former magazine editor, naturally - this publication features a few different articles. There's Morning Glory, where Nolan Crockett jacks you with his early workout; Skindecent Exposure, a photo spread about why beaches are 'crazy' places to work out; Shrediquette, a piece dedicated to proper in-gym decorum; and Twilight Tantra, an interview feature and yoga masterclass with 'gerontophile' Fabien LaRouche.

Hang on, Fabien LaRouche… isn't that the French wellness guru and shyster that was a "close friend" to Amanda De Santa in Grand Theft Auto 5? Yes, yes it is. In fact, Fabien played quite a large role in that game, acting as the antagonistic force that eventually fractured Michael and Amanda's relationship. After conducting an affair under the guise of 'yoga lessons', Amanda left our protagonist, and moved in with Fabien, taking Michael's kids - Jimmy and Tracy - with her. In a totally healthy and normal way, you could follow their new life via updates posted to GTA's Facebook surrogate, LifeInvader. It's clear the kids never liked the pretentious, annoying man - but for Amanda, the penny is a little slower to drop. Eventually, Amanda and Michael reconcile their differences - with the latter whalloping Fabien in the process. The married couple pledge to resolve their differences, and Fabien is never seen again.

Until GTA 6, at least. Here, in what we assume is supposed to be 2025 or 2026, it's been about 13 years since we last saw the character (it is widely agreed that GTA 5 is set in 2013 - the game's original launch year). And it seems that the events surrounding Michael and his family really did a number on Fabien. Which - of course - he blamed on the universe, rather than taking any personal responsibility. Naturally.

"Ever since the karmic decay of Los Santos was made perfectly clear to him in the early 2010s, yoga instructor Fabien LaRouche has been on an epic pilgrimage of enlightenment through the remotest ashrams and finest 5-star resorts and plastic surgery retreats of The East," reads the Body Copy article. "Having completed his chakric education and returned to the United States, we meet with him on the final week of his six-month residency at Dwindle Wood Retirement Community."

Here, the article goes on to explore Fabien's penchant for older women - he seems to like their nature, their desire to learn about self-improvement and wellness, and (perhaps most importantly) their bank accounts. "Being over the hill doesn't necessarily mean you have nothing left to live for," he opines in the article. Thanks for that, Fabien.

It's interesting to see - very early in the game and quite plainly telegraphed - that Rockstar is willing to reintroduce us to people from previous games. This isn't a megaton addition by any means - Fabien was never a 'main character' - but I love that a nod to him, in a feature tucked away at the back of a magazine, sets the scene for more cameos, perhaps, later in the game. It shows us, nice and early, that GTA 5 and GTA 6 are indeed connected. By inference, then, we know that GTA 4 is included in that same picture; there were recurring characters in GTA 5 that appeared in GTA 4, after all - Patrick McReary, Johnny Klebitz, Rocco Pelosi and more come to mind.

It may seem like a small thing, but it's just another example of Rockstar using its expansive toolset, ridiculous budget, and well-established legacy to inject a sense of scale into the world of Grand Theft Auto 6. There are other nods to the universe out there, too. I won't spoil them here, but I've been a little bit dewy-eyed to hear rumours of some of the other nods to Rockstar titles hidden in the world of Leonida: it makes all the experiences I've had in past games, the emotional response I've had to previous Rockstar worlds, feel in some way valid and meaningful here, too. I'm looking forward to seeing what other little secrets await me across the keys and into the glowing neon promise of Vice City proper.

#10565. What we've been playing - "It's slow, it's awkward, it's weird, and it's hard"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Did anything notable happen this week? Not that I can remember. The weather was quite rainy - will that do? I'm just being silly, of course. We've had - and I suppose are still having - an historic week in video games, as Grand Theft Auto 6 arrives. It's dominated everything and I fear it's going to dominate this article too. But a reminder: next week is our "Not-GTA Week", so we'll be making a concerted effort to talk about games, probably smaller games, that aren't Godzilla blockbusters.

As you know, this is our regular feature where we say a bit about the games we've been playing this week. This week, rather a lot of us have been playing GTA 6, as you can imagine, and discovering various things about it. But not all of us. Connor, ever the contrarian, has been continuing his race to level 60 in World of Warcraft: Forever, and he's agonisingly close to getting there.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago.

It probably comes as no surprise that I'm - much like the rest of the world - playing GTA 6 right now. While I found Chapter 1 to be quite a slow start, and where I felt somewhat restricted, I've now started Chapter 2 and found myself enjoying stealing cars, jamming along to the radio, and simply exploring Leonida - and all the strange happenings that come with it. There's so much to see and do!

I'm looking forward to deviating from the main missions for a while to do a spot of bird-watching (I like birds a lot, alright) and seeing what other side activities Leonida has to offer outside of the criminal lifestyle at the core of things. I'm also trying to be the best partner possible to both Jason and Lucia, but I'm itching to cause a bit of trouble soon... Hurting NPCs comes with so much guilt this time around though!

It's slow, it's awkward, it's weird, and it's hard. It's a perfect Eurogamer game, right? Right? GTA 6's in-game lampoon of Jonathan Blow, Hideo Kojima, Dan Pinchbeck and - weirdly - Mark Z. Danielewski is a wonderful bit of top-shelf pastiche. It goes to show just how much fun Rockstar had making GTA 6, and the freedom its dev team had to install as much depth and satire in just about every nook and cranny conceivable. Don't sleep on Tenebrae Lux, it's genuinely satisfying to complete.

I'm not, historically, the biggest GTA fan - although I've absolutely got a soft spot for Vice City's sublime mood - so I wasn't quite as excited to play GTA 6 as some. But it took, oh I dunno, about 30 seconds before I was sold. This thing has style. And everything else a billion dollars and some incredible development talent gets you. And it's those because-we-can details that have, in my first few hours, left the biggest impression. I spent a good five minutes just marveling at Jason dance - specifically the way his little stationary wiggle (perfectly timed to the radio beat!) shifts seamlessly into a wiggle-strut when you hit the forward button.

I love the way the setting sun seems to shine just right through the clouds when the moment calls, and those perfect needle drops. And careening through the night - amid the driving rain and smear of taillights, Kylie classic Spinning Around booming on the radio and Jason rocking a recently acquired, slightly improbable hat - this extravagant, ridiculous thing had me completely transfixed. I'm genuinely, unexpectedly excited to see where it takes me next.

Assuming there's lots of Grand Theft Auto 6 in this article, I'll save you from my own early impressions of this week's blockbuster release. Instead, you get more tales from an MMO. It is what it is!

I'm level 58 right now, pushing my way into the endgame dungeons. I've almost maxed all my professions, and am gradually getting gear that should serve me well when the raids are added next month.

But forget all that. I got a salt shaker! As a leatherworker, you can buy a salt shaker from engineers for a little bit of gold on the auction house. These are more important than you'd assume. With them you can make one refined deeprock salt every three days - a high-end crafting material. Rather than hoard these for fancy gear, I just sold 'em back to the auction house. They're current going for 50 gold a pop on my server, which is fantastic passive income.

This is important because I'm currently saving for an epic mount: a process that hit a major roadbump after I unlocked my secondary class specialisation for 50 gold, and then blew way more than that on unlocking all the available skills (that I likely won't use) from my druid trainer (for no good reason). I'm currently sitting on 232 gold as of writing. There's a long way to go to 1000, but hey, that's the game.

I can't imagine anyone changing the radio station when Drive by The Cars begins during that first moment of proper uninterrupted freedom. The buttons to do that might as well not even be there. If you did, we're completely different people.

I'm not very far in but my major impressions of GTA 6 so far are: the performances and their presentation are wonderful, the game controls a bit sludgily and feels like it's not quite in focus on PS5, and I really like DIY.

Let's take that middle one, that criticism: I'm sure this will lift as I get used to the game and how it moves, and how the characters work. It's also just a fact of life, so to speak, that it's going to struggle a bit on PS5 rather than PS5 Pro. And I'm nitpicking; on the whole, this thing is staggeringly technically impressive.

The latter point, DIY: it's absurd how much detail Rockstar has put into you doing up your shack. I especially like how it's not boring. A deeper thought related to this, and to the rest of the game, is the fantasy of self-improvement GTA 6 delivers. In GTA 6, as in all GTA games I think, you can drastically alter your life's fortunes in mere moments. I set about doing up my shack in a way I could never do in real life given a week or a month, but here, I did it in moments. That appeals to me greatly.

Honourable mention to Star Wars: Galactic Racer which I finally played this week. Love the speed, love Biddy Blas, and also really gel with the Roguelike approach, whereby you take the game in 'runs' to see how far you can progress each time. Cracking game.

#10563. What if Vampire Survivors met Grand Theft Auto 2? Maniac is a brilliant mash-up that dares to ask that question

"We all have a pathetic hot-dog-suit guy inside of us."

What would you get if you combined the chaotic top-down action of old-school Grand Theft Auto 2 with the chaotic top-down action of Vampire Survivors? That's the question Soldat and King Arthur's Gold developer Transhuman asked when it started working on 2024's roguelite shooter Maniac. The result is a delight, yet Maniac remains frustratingly underappreciated.

Your goal is simple: survive for as long as you can or until the 20-minute clock hits zero. Throughout that time, your secondary objective is to create as much mayhem as possible - painting the road with the blood of your foes, slamming cars into buildings, performing drive-bys as you rampage across the procedurally-generated city. The more carnage you cause, the higher your Wanted level, until you're fending off police and the military.

Maniac's debt to GTA is clear, and its chaotic action is brilliantly realised as it channels the compelling rhythms of a Survivors-style roguelite, where randomness in items and level layout makes each run fresh as you strive to unlock new characters, progressing a little further each time. It's a relatively slight experience, as its £4 price tag reflects, featuring a single compact map, just six characters, and barely any permanent progression. But Maniac makes a spirited argument for a return to a simpler time when GTA was all about shooting cops, stealing cars, and indiscriminately murdering pedestrians rather than the 'everything' game it is today.

Maniac is also a game that feels like it's trying to make good on an idea many of us had when we first played GTA 1 & 2 all those years ago: what would a better-looking, more advanced version of that look like? And for developer Michał Marcinkowski, Maniac's genesis also harkens back to an earlier time. "When I was 12, I used to come back from school and play the demo of GTA 1," he tells me over email. "It was top-down and time-limited, and all I'd do was try to cause as much chaos as possible within the 20 minutes of the demo."

And there's perhaps a hint of a 12-year-old's exuberance to Maniac. There's the bright colour palette and gleeful chaos of its physics-driven mechanics, the adrenaline rush its aggressive enemy AI creates, and its absurd roster of playable characters, which range from Little Girl to Hotdog Guy. "Each [character] is a bit of me and a bit of you," Marcinkowski explains. "We all have a drunk Santa in us. We all have a pathetic hot-dog-suit guy inside of us. Each character failed at something and is lashing out."

As each new Grand Theft Auto game pushes further into realism, the idea of recreating the rampages seen in classic GTA feels increasingly unpalatable, but Maniac's colourful cartoon aesthetic means it can get away with more. One Achievement, with a flourish of old-school bad-taste humour, challenges players to destroy five school buses using an airstrike ability called Santa's Little Helpers. And that level of irreverent, chaotic lunacy escalates the deeper into each session you get, particularly as your Wanted level climbs: if you antagonise law enforcement enough here, they eventually break out a game-ending nuke.

"This comes back to being 12," Marcinkowski says. "Nukes are just cool. I wanted the game to end with a bang, and what's more maniacal and exaggerated than a nuke? So that idea was with me for more than 20 years."

Core to Maniac's design is the ability to change from a Survivors-like auto-shooter to a standard shooter where you're able to control your aim. The latter option is, I think, the more fitting one, particularly given how much driving you do. If you squint, it almost feels like a GTA 2 clone. And while the Vampire Survivors inspirations are plentiful - including its now-standard three-option upgrade system - the GTA elements are clearly the more prevalent.

It might not play exactly like a GTA game, but it certainly captures the series' early days spirit. And Marcinkowski says he's surprised more developers haven't tried to riff on GTA: "You'd expect the most popular game in the world to have hundreds of clones, but that's not the case." There may be a reason for that.

As big as modern-day GTA is, clones are apparently much less popular. "When you look at the sales data, particularly of top-down GTA clones, they don't sell well", explains Marcinkowski. "I found that out way too late, and I thought Maniac wouldn't be a hit." His publisher was initially equally buoyant too. "But then they studied the market and stopped being so hyped. That's how it felt as we got close to release. The wishlist count wasn't very high."

"I was expecting 10,000 [copies sold]," Marcinkowski tells me. "I actually started making another game two months before Maniac came out because I thought I'd go broke." But then the game launched, and early worries proved unfounded. It sold more than 100K copies, comfortably blowing past its maker's modest expectations, putting it in the top one percent of games released on Steam.

It's a surprise success story built on the foundations of Marcinkowski's youth, and as we chat about GTA, he reveals he's less keen on Rockstar's modern games, which lack that classic gameplay drive. "I always liked the counter-culture side of GTA, which is why I put so much of it into Maniac," he elaborates. "GTA 6 seems to have become pop culture, so it's lost its appeal for me." Marcinkowski's heart, it seems, is old-school.

Nintendo Switch, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS4, PS5, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S

#10561. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

#10559. I've never known a moment like this in video games

As gaming captivates the world, it seems fitting that Grand Theft Auto - its most contradictory, chaotic, and all-encompassing product - is to act as its ambassador.

It was hard not to spend the first hour or so of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch day in a kind of stun-lock. As far as video games editorial goes - which is not that far, in fairness; prepare for an over-flattering analogy - the effect was roughly the equivalent of Tom Hanks' experience, as his John H. Miller first set foot on Omaha beach. Tweets and reels whistled overhead, news leads stung the ears, exploding search terms blew apart some poor guides team ally on one side, while new discoveries in the early game - made by the millions and millions of launch-day players all picking it apart at once - appeared by surprise like some flanking ambush on the other.

The analogy falls apart pretty quickly when you think about it: the heroism of writing a little article about a video game aside, the frenzy of this week has been a joy, not a horror. But the point is, I've been doing this job for over a decade now, paying attention to video games for another two beyond that, and I've truly never seen anything like this before. It has been totally overwhelming. And then I thought about that for a moment before realising that - duh - nobody has ever seen anything like this before. GTA 6 is the biggest entertainment release of all time, and it's not even remotely close.

It's worth taking a moment just to actually, properly take stock of all the headlines from the past couple of days in terms of the sheer breadth and scale of its launch. Parent company Take Two announced yesterday that GTA 6 sold 32.5m copies before it came out. By the end of its first day on sale, it had made a record-obliterating $3.79bn. By contrast, the once record-breaking GTA 5 made a once astounding $800m in the same period; the "opening" period of the largest film of all time, Avengers: Endgame, made $1.22bn, and that's totalling box office receipts across its release day until the end of the first Sunday after it.

It has set multiple records for streaming, per StreamCharts, with 214.63m hours watched in its first two days - significantly more than the previous biggest game, Valorant, achieved with 150m hours in its entire launch week. It's garnered more watch time in those two days than Elden Ring, Hogwarts Legacy, and Cyberpunk 2077 achieved with their entire launch weeks combined. Its concurrent viewers peak so far - 6.38 million people - is nearly four times the previous record of 1.74m, again formerly held by Valorant.

It crashed Reddit. More than 2 million UK and US Xbox players used the 'New Zealand trick' to play the game early, which is more than a third of that country's entire population. Internet exchange operator DE-CIX claimed its launch led to the highest amount of concurrent internet usage in history, at 33.03 terabits per second.

On top of all those raw numbers, it's also just felt utterly inescapable. I can't open Instagram, TikTok, BlueSky or X without being blasted with memes, viral clips of early discoveries, reactions, hot takes, feeble attempts at brand piggybacks, slightly wayward early-game tips from opportunist streamers who haven't escaped the first chapter, or makeup tutorials for looking like Lucia. I can't open my inbox without scrolling through reams of tenuous PR pitches for stories with barely the faintest connection to the game. I can't open my phone without messages from parents, friends or peers. Even the mainstream news - heaven forbid! - has gradually clocked onto this whole video games thing, this unknown artifact from the imaginary world of "online", maybe being a bit important. Dozens of my colleagues have been inundated with last-minute call-ups to provide an expert soundbite (they're so desperate they even got me on at one point).

We'll have a lot more to say about this game in the coming days and weeks (with apologies to regulars who are already sick of it - counterprogramming starts this Monday!). It's the richest of texts, a near endless comedy clip generator, a total technical marvel and all the rest - you already know. But it felt worth it to just sit still for a moment, and take stock of what's happening here.

This is the single biggest moment any piece of entertainment media has ever had. But more than that it's felt like a kind of party. The vibes in some corners are orgiastic, as streamers uncover spooky twists, outlandish reactivity, and easter eggs I'd never got close to in a hundred-odd hours with the thing - all of this pure dynamite for the audience. Given the budgets involved and the particularly unique blend of circumstances around it - the changing shape of video game funding; of how attention is monopolised; of how development may happen in the future and the sheer amount of time and subsequent anticipation built up since the previous entry - we will likely be waiting decades for another moment like it. If there's ever a moment like it again. Part of the sense of ecstasy going around comes, perhaps subconsciously, from the acknowledgment that in pure hype and attention terms, this might be as good as it ever gets.

For the briefest time, the world belongs to video games. Rockstar and Take-Two's executives and shareholders will no doubt reap the greatest rewards from all this - all part of the great irony that makes this game's central premise all the more interesting. But I sincerely hope its developers - especially the 34 fired by the company last year, now awaiting the outcome of their tribunal over alleged union-busting, and those who hope to form a union from the currently open vote - are able to feel a particular sense of pride.

Just as this launch has been unprecedented in its success, the last two or three years have been unprecedented in their grimness for video game workers across most of the world, certainly the once-dominat western part. If we're very lucky - and frankly, I have my doubts - this burst of attention and visible profitability might go some way to helping the industry claw its way back, perhaps via attracting, as a knock-on from all the new-found attention, some kind of more sustainable investment. If we're not, and this is really the last behemoth, the last video game monument of its kind, the last rave before the police come raiding at dawn, at least we've gone out with one hell of a bang.

GTA 6 - again, it's been said before now - is such a curious thing. A game of goofiness, self-awareness, prestige, pretentiousness, childish humour, astute commentary, ironic cultural criticism, surprising player expression and - above all of that - the most mind-boggling, brain-scrambling level of profoundly human craft. There's something kind of perfect about this.

It's a good thing, actually, that video games' greatest export is also its most contradictory. Video games are contradictory things in themselves. If the non-specialist media might still occasionally slip into treating video games like some kind of object from outer space, then we might as well put forward the best possible candidate for a capsule. It's the same way you'd think about it if you had to stick one thing in a vacuum-sealed box, to send out into the universe in an attempt to symbolise humanity: something that summarises close to all of it; that we're proud of; and crucially, that captures the spirit of it too.

There are many more that might've put a more respectable foot forward - certainly that have a less chequered history, or maybe a more sophisticated, nuanced, or inventive use of the form. But there's really no better video game for exemplifying the full, chaotic range of the medium. It seems right, when you think about it, for GTA to be the one out there sitting under the spotlight, in the biggest moment this silly, serious artform might ever have.

#10557. "It stayed alive for so long" - Behind the wheel with the Last Riders saying goodbye to GTA Online, amidst GTA 6's world-changing launch

In the decade or so since Grand Theft Auto Online's arrival, its roads have become a kind of home; well-loved by its racers, its gear heads, and speed freaks. It's a game that allows players to lose themselves in the roleplay, the heists, and the crime sprees. But for me, an occasional dipper into its major updates, the car folk have always been the engine driving its appeal.

So when the GTA Online community decided to host Last Ride events this week - a sort of mass in-game road trip and a communal farewell before attention inevitably turned to GTA 6 - I was keen to join in. The Last Drive was scheduled to begin an hour before GTA 6's launch, and would see numerous groups embarking on their own journey across GTA Online's servers, all taking the same route: starting at Los Santos' Del Perro Pier and following the highway around Los Santos' entire perimeter before finally rolling up to its airport. Players would then fly off into the sunset, close the game, and move on. Some just for a while, others perhaps forever.

Kirsty is one of the many long-time players who chose to embark on their own last drive as part of the event. She runs the Fast Crew racing community in GTA Online and has been playing since the beginning in 2013, even meeting her fiance through the game. Unlike some, however, she plans to stick around in GTA Online after GTA 6's release, at least until the new game's equivalent comes along.

It's the social dynamic of GTA Online that makes it special, Kirsty tells me when we speak online, and she calls it a community of players "that thrives from supporting each other, racing together and just having fun". She points to GTA Online's 30-player sessions as one reason for its enduring appeal "That's important for people in communities like ours," she explains. "Gaming is an escape to many people, a distraction or a coping method. GTA is special because it stayed alive for so long, giving people that escape."

Then there's Brittany, who leads the GTA Online Ascent racing community. Brittany has a real-life kart racing background, and built race tracks across various Xbox 360 games before arriving in GTA Online. As with many of the players I spoke with, the in-game connections Brittany made have blossomed into real-life friendships, all built around racing.

"GTA Online introduced me to many lifelong friends," they explain. "Hell, I even met my girlfriend Ari through the GTA Racing community. Every year, five-to-six of us go to the Indy 500 and all of us met through my [GTA Online] PC racing community BRITCAR." Brittany was also planning their own Last Drive, using old cars from the game's earlier years.

It's clear GTA Online has become more than just a game to many people, and I was eager to see how players might choose to say goodbye. So as GTA 6 loomed, I booted up GTA Online and prepared for my own Last Drive, waiting near the pier in a random online lobby. Slowly, other people started to arrive - all seemingly strangers - and at the allotted time, we slowly began making our way around the map as one.

What I saw was brilliant. A snaking line of customised cars, custom paint jobs, and underlighting, slowly navigating the island; a peaceful, reverential convoy of players who weren't blasting away at each other with weapons for once. The chaos of the typical GTA Online lobby never broke out. Instead, there was a sort of unspoken armistice between those eager to enjoy one last moment in a game they'd spent so much time in. Gradually, more people joined our conga line.

Besides being a lovely way to close a chapter for many, it allowed for a cheeky bit of peacocking too. Many players pulled up in truly exceptional rides: there were supercars, bulky muscle cars, and street-racer lightshows, all as much an expression of their owners' personalities and tastes as any human avatar.

At one point, the leader of this snaking pack made a wrong turn, taking us into a dockyard and an unfortunate dead-end. We then all had to slowly reverse out - a time-consuming, if amusing, process which allowed one opportunistic spectator to pull up and take a few of us out with a well-placed RPG. But after we'd all reconvened nearby, the respectful atmosphere resumed. No flying Oppressor Mk II rained missiles on us, and there weren't any kamikaze crashes interrupting our final drive.

When we did finally reach the airport, people left their vehicles and things took a more knockabout turn. Together we huddled for a few moments, letting off gunfire and fireworks into the night. Then the cops came, and the group rushed to steal planes and helicopters before things got wilder still.

Slowly, eventually our group began to disperse. Players either went on to cause some final havoc before GTA 6's arrival, or they did as I did, flying over the ocean into the sunset before leaving the server once and for all. It sounds sappy but it was moving. Here, a game perhaps best known for its player-versus-player hijinks had - thanks to a close-knit community consisting of people like Kirsty, Brittany, and all their friends - became a wholesome drive along. A send off to the game as it was, before ushering in a new dawn.

It speaks to what is special about GTA Online, really. A playground people have spent years in, clearly still important to them, and a chance to say a farewell of sorts from the place they feel most comfortable: behind the wheel.

PC, PS3, PS4, PS5, Xbox 360, Xbox One, Xbox Series X/S
