#10555. Queen's Domain review

Where to start with Queen's Domain? It's tempting to begin with King's Field; after all, FromSoftware's seminal first-person action-RPG is an explicit inspiration for developer Freshly Baked Games. But 40 hours later, it feels like so much more. It's equally tempting to invoke another FromSoftware game: Dark Souls. Queen's Domain owes an obvious debt to that game's celebrated level design: the dazzling interconnectedness of its vertiginous world. But that's not quite right either. The deeper you delve, the more there's something of the spirit of The Legend of Zelda, a touch of Metroidvania too. Until finally you concede that Queen's Domain, with its remarkable world of densely packed secrets, is very much its own.

All this begins with an island. You, Prince Beruim, are a castaway, washed ashore on Neasied. Your father, the king, has disappeared, and your search has brought you, somewhat circuitously, here. As you clamber to your feet and survey the island, it makes an immediately striking impression: all strange peaks and crooked spires; a cramped, chaotic mass of landmarks lost amid the dense undergrowth. Pyramids perch atop steep plateaus; a vast, moss-tinged mausoleum sprawls across the land to one side while the other is dominated by an imposing tower. It's a world made all the stranger by Queen's Domain's retro aesthetic. Here, shimmering low-res textures and low-poly shapes converge to create elaborate spaces and structures that feel slightly at odds with the lo-fi vibe. It's not quite old, not quite new; instead, something of a world lost outside of time. It makes for an enigmatic, disorientating landscape. And when paired with a soundtrack that delights in discordance, as it swings from gentle ambience to pulsing synths, it's a mood that refuses to resolve.

There is so much to say about Neasied. And so much I absolutely will not spoil. But very early impressions don't do it justice at all. As you take your first tentative steps onto the island, presented with a forking path that eventually winds back in on itself, it's easy to mistake Queen's Domain for a first-person Dark Souls - especially if, like me, you're only passingly familiar with King's Field. As in most Soulslikes, early progress is defined by a punishing inch-by-inch crawl from one brutal encounter to the next; where death - via chicken-cricket hybrids, ladybird-rats, and other wonderfully odd things - comes fast and frequently, respawning you back at the last Crystal Monument you visited. And because its wilfully old-school design eschews modern-day niceties such as a combat lock-on or dedicated roll button, there's a looseness to fighting that takes a while to click. But it does, slowly, click.

This is a game that demands you lean into its limitations, encourages you to find ways to make them work. At first, those Souls-honed instincts are likely to be your undoing as you naturally scramble for a dodge button that isn't there. But soon a different sort of pattern emerges: you realise that to avoid a ladybird attack, you simply need to step back - but not so far you don't have time to nip in and strike while your opponent takes a pause. A charging enemy might require a different approach - a well-timed backstep and swivelling sideways strafe to put you in the right place for a counter. And in this way, combat becomes a compelling sort of puzzle as you learn each of the 100+ enemies' little ticks - including rock, paper, scissors-style weaknesses to different weapon types - and figure out how to respond.

There's more to consider. Your melee damage output, for instance, is directly tied to your stamina bar: the more stamina you have, the harder you'll hit; too low and it's a waste of a swing. That forces you to choose your moments carefully, moving out to safety while your bar refills. And there's a charge attack, also linked to your stamina, that does more damage still. Already combat has a distinctively deliberate rhythm, but then come your first throwing knives. These are most immediately useful against enemies ordinarily out of reach - like the laser-spewing foliage dogging your journey early on - but they're equally valuable for luring individual enemies away from the throng. Throwing weapons take a while to return to your pouch, meaning they too need to be deployed with purpose. And while knives are just the start - you'll acquire an increasingly exotic selection of throwables including poison-tipped darts and homing blades - they all fundamentally work the same way.

And the interplay of these various warm-ups and cooldowns creates an increasingly engaging rhythm as you learn to juggle between your variously equipped weapons to maximise their damage potential. You might, for instance, fire off a one-two-three volley of homing darts, then use a magic lantern to toss out a massive fireball that bounces destructively around the room. And it's there, in the ensuing chaos, you'll charge up your blade, letting rip then returning to your darts again. Combat might encourage a methodical pace, but that doesn't mean you can't fight fast, and there are some fascinating synergies among the various weapons, many having their own wonderfully distinct mechanisms. There's a brawling gauntlet that lifts you clean off the ground and a vampiric blade capable of stealing an enemy's life force. There are hammers, creatures, and an unexpected mid-game weapon class, utilising a third rechargeable power source, that I am definitely not spoiling. Queen's Domain's 100 or so weapons create ample space for experimentation and customisation, and the further from its cautious beginnings you stray, the brisker, the more exhilarating its combat becomes. In my current, late-game form, I'm a whirl of blades and blasts and up-close attacks that feels anything but Soulslike.

Queen's Domain isn't just a nippier sort of game, though. It quickly reveals itself to be friendlier, more accommodating. Levelling, for instance, happens passively as you play, meaning every swing feels like it's contributing to your progress. Weapons level independently too, encouraging specialisation. There are no flasks, no accumulated currency you'll need to worry about dropping on death, no subsequent corpse runs. And progress can be saved at any time using the nearest Crystal Monument, meaning that when you respawn, you won't have to grind through the same opponents again. Enemies do eventually reappear of their own accord, so you can't exploit the save system indefinitely. But unlike Souls-style bonfires, Crystal Monuments only repopulate the world when you explicitly tell them to - when you Rest to clear status effects and restore health - giving you considerably more agency. You can decide when to push forward, when to retreat and lock in your progress, and when to reap the benefits of a rest and reset. Together, it makes for a breezier, less punishing, more consistently rewarding riff on the traditional Souslike, while never losing that I-can-do-this just-one-more-turn allure.

But as immensely enjoyable as Queen's Domain's combat is, the real draw here, unquestionably, is the world. Neasied is extraordinary. Right from the start, as you pick yourself off the sand and survey the landscape, its enigmatic huddle of peculiar landmarks isn't just intriguing, it's inviting. These odd pyramids, these curious plateaus practically demand to be explored. But how? The answer comes perhaps an hour in when, fancily clad skeleton boss successfully slain, you get your hands on the Holy Sword Endu. It's an immediate boon, far more lethal than the rubbish rusty blade you've been wielding so far. And its secondary attack - a long-range jab that propels you forward - is handy too. It's also your key to unlocking Neasied.

Endu's violent forward momentum can, you see, fling you across previously uncrossable gaps, gradually carrying you further and further as more upgrades reveal themselves. Suddenly, Queen's Domain takes flight, quite literally, becoming something closer to an exploratory sandbox as it adopts the spirited acrobatics of a platform game. And as you experiment with your newfound freedom, the more paths you explore, the island begins to take on a dizzying new form. Neasied, when viewed from up high, reveals itself to be a vastly intricate traversal puzzle. That peculiar structure in the distance? If you can find an appropriate ledge to fling yourself from, it's now yours for the investigating - and from there, one, two, three more tantalising branches are revealed. Neasied is an astonishing piece of design: a ludicrous, elaborate tangle of hidden pathways and secrets, forever folding in and around itself, that's crafted to invite curiosity and then constantly reward.

Freshly Baked Games always feels one step ahead. I can't recall a single moment where my exploratory What-ifs - What if I throw myself this way? What if I do a falling mid-air turn? What if I toss myself down this hole? - didn't bear fruit. Sometimes your prize is a steaming platter of boon-granting food, sometimes it's another bit of increasingly exotic weaponry. Perhaps you'll find a strange NPC with a favour to ask; fish eggs and mysterious skulls; a shopkeeper with a thing for ducks (who, in a typically lovely touch, festoons his store with the goods you're willing to sell), and so many mysterious new puzzle threads to pull. As you fling and probe and push on forward, that thing you've been carrying around for hours - or, conversely, that thing you saw hours ago - suddenly finds new purpose. There's a definite touch of Metroidvania as old areas reveal new truths the further you go. And because there's so little handholding, so little explicit direction, every discovery feels like your own.

But just as you think you're starting to get a handle on Queen's Domain's shape, along comes another surprise: dungeons as vastly intricate as the world outside. You'll navigate labyrinthine waterlogged caverns of angry fish where progress involves puzzling past ancient mechanisms. Later, an arcane tower where gargoyles prowl. Here, the convoluted upward route takes circuitous laps among the endless bookshelves, spilling out the windows onto teetering precipices with alarming sea views. Then down, however briefly, to eerie chambers below. Suddenly, the mood is less Soulslike, more Zelda, and your expectations shift again. And still new discoveries lead to new paths and fresh mysteries.

And it keeps on going. As Freshly Baked Games gradually peels back Neasied's layers, it constantly finds new ways to awe: yet more surprises, yet more secrets, your exploratory digressions earning still more intriguing rewards. There comes a point where you think surely you must have seen everything, explored its deepest caverns and most mysterious tombs, but down you go again. And somewhere around the midpoint, Queen's Domain takes a truly audacious turn. There is a - let's call it a tonal lurch for fear of spoilers - that signals another shift. Suddenly, there are new toys to play with, an autumnal landscape so complex in its demands, it genuinely made my brain pulse. And then it's over. Until it's not.

Retro resolution toggle (reduces visual shimmering). Individual volume sliders for master, volume, and effects; headphones mode audio toggle. Screen shake toggle; tap to run toggle. Mouse look sensitivity and acceleration sliders; invert mouse horizontal and vertical look toggles; configurable keyboard and mouse bindings; invert gamepad horizontal and vertical look toggles; vibration intensity slider; stick swap toggle; stick deadzone adjustment slider; configurable gamepad bindings.

There are, it turns out, secrets beneath the secrets; a still more intricate web of interconnected threads spun throughout this already fascinating, impossibly complex world. The Neasied that welcomes you really is just the tip of it all. Exactly when you'll come to this realisation depends how thorough you are in the earlier hours - how willing you are to explore its darker corners and brave its more punishing realms - but for me the before-and-after managed to divide my 40-ish hour playthrough neatly in two. You reawaken with a sense of a quest unfulfilled, a hint that more is required. And so, in lieu of more explicit direction, you set out again, probe deeper, explore further, and slowly the vaguest shape of a new objective - a succession of tiny threads seemingly all leading in the same direction - begins to emerge. Tucked along uncharted paths, perhaps, or in suspicious corners, or in item descriptions that suddenly feels like clues.

It's here in this second half - something of a dark mirror to the first, in classic Zelda style - that Queen's Domain takes on a more esoteric air; its web of distantly scattered enigmas requiring real dedication to unfurl. Why this pot on a stick? Why this book? How do these unlit lanterns relate to this thing, and that thing, and this other thing on the other side of the world?

It's clever stuff, even if navigating its late-game threads can occasionally feel just a little too much, a little too abstruse, and you'll potentially face a lot of needle-in-a-haystack backtracking if you haven't been taking judicious notes as you go. Do I have any other quibbles? There are perhaps too few boss encounters that feel like genuine events. And while combat generally feels fair and balanced, serving as a propulsive drumbeat to the broader exploratory rhythms, there are a few late-game all-the-enemies-at-once-style difficulty spikes that go just a bit too far. But then another 'What if?' and the world responds, and those frustrations melt away.

I remember a time in my youth - back before dataminers and FAQs - when video games felt unknowable. Endless enigmas built from creativity and code. It felt like anything could be hiding in there if only you knew where to look, and it was easy to let your imagination run wild. Queen's Domain is so full of secrets, so full of mystery and wonder, it feels like the sort of game my enthusiastic child-brain might have dreamed up all those years ago. 40 hours in, I've seen an ending, but it feels like I'm still far from through. So many mysteries still linger, so many things remain on my checklist of speculative to-dos. Out in the real-world, I've got a Zelda and a GTA awaiting. But it says everything about Queen's Domain - this brilliant, vast, deep enigma; this absurdly audacious thing - that I'm still not yet ready to let go.

A copy of Queen's Domain was provided for this review by Future Friends Games.

#10553. A tiny supercurrent could unlock quantum information without destroying it

One of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing is surprisingly simple to describe: how do you read information without changing or destroying it? Unlike the bits used in ordinary computers, quantum bits, or qubits, are extraordinarily sensitive to measurement. Trying to determine their state can cause that state to collapse, potentially erasing the very information scientists want to retrieve.

Researchers have now proposed a way to overcome much of this difficulty by using an unusual electrical signal produced by the qubit itself. Their approach focuses on a special type known as an "Andreev qubit," which can generate an oscillating electric supercurrent through its own quantum behavior. By monitoring this current, scientists may be able to determine the qubit's state without directly measuring it or significantly disturbing the information it contains.

The research, supported by FAPESP, was published in Physical Review B Letters. It was led by José Carlos Egues, a full professor at the São Carlos Institute of Physics at the University of São Paulo (IFSC-USP) in Brazil, and Yugui Yao of the School of Physics at the Beijing Institute of Technology in China. If experimentally confirmed, their method could simplify quantum computing by removing the need for extra qubits used in certain measurement techniques.

Why Measuring Quantum Information Is So Difficult

Conventional computers store information in bits that have one of two values, 0 or 1. These values can be checked repeatedly without changing the information. Quantum computers work differently because their qubits can exist in superpositions, meaning they can combine the states 0 and 1 in ways that are not possible with conventional bits.

This unusual ability is central to the potential power of quantum computing. However, it also creates a major vulnerability. Quantum states can be disrupted by interactions with their surroundings, including the instruments scientists use to examine them.

"In the conventional formulation of quantum mechanics, a projective measurement causes the system to assume one of the states associated with the measured quantity. Thus, if a qubit has been prepared in a certain superposition of 0 and 1, its direct measurement tends to destroy that superposition and, consequently, the information it contained," says Egues.

To minimize this problem, physicists developed techniques known as "quantum nondemolition measurements," or QND. Many of these approaches use an additional quantum system called an ancilla. Rather than measuring the original qubit directly, researchers transfer information to the ancilla and measure that auxiliary system instead.

The term "ancilla" comes from a Latin word meaning "slave" or "servant." In modern scientific usage, it describes something that performs a supporting function. Here, the ancilla acts as an intermediary, allowing researchers to obtain information while preserving the original qubit as much as possible.

Although effective, these procedures come with practical disadvantages. Scientists must prepare the auxiliary qubits, connect them to the qubits being studied, transfer information between them, and perform repeated measurements. These additional steps require time and equipment, and the complexity becomes increasingly significant as the number of qubits grows.

"Our strategy is different. Instead of transferring the information to an auxiliary qubit, we seek to obtain it from a signal produced by the qubit under study itself," says the researcher.

A Special Quantum Bit That Produces Its Own Signal

The proposed technique relies on the unusual properties of an Andreev qubit. Its name honors Soviet physicist Alexander Andreev (1939-2023), who identified a distinctive quantum process in 1964 that occurs where normal materials meet superconductors.

Superconductors are materials that can conduct electricity without electrical resistance under appropriate conditions. At the boundaries involving these materials, special quantum states known as "Andreev states" can form. In the system studied by the researchers, these states are confined to an extremely small region between two superconducting materials.

These quantum states can represent the basic values 0 and 1, along with superpositions of those values, making them suitable for storing quantum information.

"In the system under consideration, this region is a quantum dot - that is, a nanoscale structure in which electrons are spatially confined - formed in an indium arsenide [InAs] nanowire connected to superconducting materials on both sides. It's in this environment that the states characterizing the Andreev qubit are formed," Egues explains.

The key to the new approach is an electrical phenomenon called a "supercurrent." Unlike the current flowing through ordinary electrical conductors, a supercurrent moves without resistance.

The researchers found that when the Andreev qubit exists in a superposition, the supercurrent develops a component that oscillates over time. These fluctuations arise from quantum interference between the different states making up the superposition.

Crucially, the oscillations are not random. Their behavior contains information about the qubit's underlying quantum state, potentially allowing scientists to determine what that state is without directly measuring the qubit.

"It's precisely that property that we use to read the qubit. It's that supercurrent that lies at the heart of our proposal," the researcher points out.

How a Tiny Electrical Current Reveals Quantum Information

To understand how the technique works, it helps to consider how physicists describe a qubit's state mathematically.

One common representation is the "Bloch sphere," a geometric model in which every possible pure state of a single qubit corresponds to a point on the surface of a sphere. Two angles determine the location of that point.

The first angle describes how much each of the two basic states contributes to the superposition. The second describes their relative phase, which specifies how the quantum components are aligned with one another. Together, these two measurements provide the information needed to identify the qubit's state.

According to the new research, both pieces of information can be extracted from the supercurrent. Its steady component and the size of its oscillations reveal the relative contributions of the two basic states. Meanwhile, the timing of the oscillations provides information about their phase relationship.

"Our study showed that this information is encoded in the supercurrent itself. The supercurrent has a constant component and a fluctuating component. The value of the constant component and the amplitude of the oscillation reveal the proportion of each state (0 and 1) in the superposition, and the moment when that oscillation begins reveals the phase relationship between them. Thus, by measuring how the current varies over time, it's possible to reconstruct the initial state of the qubit," Egues explains.

This means the electrical current effectively carries a signature of the quantum information stored in the system. By observing how that signature changes over time, researchers could reconstruct the qubit's original state.

A Supercurrent That Oscillates Without Voltage

Another surprising feature of the proposed system is that the electrical current can oscillate without an externally applied voltage.

Ordinarily, producing an oscillating supercurrent in this type of superconducting system requires a voltage. This familiar phenomenon is called the "AC Josephson effect."

The researchers predict something different in their Andreev qubit. Here, the current's oscillations arise naturally from the qubit's own quantum evolution. No outside voltage is required to generate them.

"Our system generates a supercurrent that varies over time like an alternating current but without voltage. It's the very evolution of the qubit's state that produces that oscillation," says Egues.

This behavior is important because it transforms quantum activity occurring on an extraordinarily small scale into a detectable electrical signal. The current provides a connection between the microscopic world of quantum states and the larger physical systems scientists can measure.

That connection is what makes the proposed reading technique possible.

Detecting Quantum States With an Extremely Sensitive Sensor

The researchers envision placing the Andreev qubit inside a closed superconducting circuit, allowing its supercurrent to circulate continuously.

Next to the circuit would sit a SQUID (superconducting quantum interference device), a highly sensitive instrument capable of detecting extremely weak magnetic fields.

Whenever an electric current flows, it produces a magnetic field. Because the supercurrent in this system oscillates, the magnetic field it generates also changes over time.

The nearby SQUID could detect these tiny magnetic variations and use them to determine the behavior of the circulating current. Since the current contains information about the qubit's quantum state, the researchers could then reconstruct that state without measuring the qubit directly.

Egues compares the arrangement to two compasses that interact through a magnetic field.

"We can think of the qubit as one compass and the SQUID as another compass. The current in the circuit produces a magnetic field, and the SQUID 'senses' that field. Since the coupling between the two is very weak, the effect of the measuring instrument on the qubit tends to be very small."

The weak interaction between the sensor and the qubit is especially important. It offers a way to gather information while minimizing the disturbance that normally accompanies quantum measurements.

Reading Quantum Information With Almost No Disturbance

In quantum physics, observing a system is not necessarily a harmless process. A measuring instrument interacts with whatever it is attempting to measure, and that interaction can change the system's behavior.

This fundamental difficulty puzzled the earliest researchers studying quantum mechanics. It remains an unavoidable feature of quantum measurements today.

The proposed technique does not eliminate this effect entirely. The SQUID still interacts with the circuit, creating a small amount of feedback that can influence the qubit. However, the team's simulations suggest that this disturbance can be reduced to a level so small that it becomes effectively negligible.

"Our method doesn't eliminate the fundamental problem of quantum measurement. But it can approach the limit of a non-destructive readout and, at the same time, avoid the use of auxiliary qubits - the ancillas - employed in other non-destructive quantum measurement protocols," Egues notes.

The approach could also remove another complication found in some existing techniques. Because researchers would not need to restore the qubit to its original state after every measurement, they could make repeated observations as the state changes.

This would allow scientists to follow the qubit's quantum evolution over time, rather than obtaining information only through separate measurement and reset cycles.

Simulations Achieve Up to 99.85% Accuracy

To investigate whether the method could work outside ideal theoretical conditions, the researchers performed computer simulations designed to account for imperfections that would arise in real experiments.

These calculations included measurement noise, as well as the small amount of feedback introduced by the detection coil. The goal was to determine whether the qubit's state could still be reconstructed accurately despite these disturbances.

The results were encouraging. Depending on the number of measurements used, the simulations produced accuracies of 99.52%, 99.81%, and 99.85%.

According to the researchers, these values fall within the standard thresholds used in quantum error correction schemes, which are designed to manage errors in quantum information processing.

The findings suggest that the method could provide highly accurate quantum state reconstruction without the additional complexity of auxiliary qubits.

However, the technique has not yet been demonstrated experimentally. The results come entirely from theoretical analysis and computer simulations, so researchers still need to establish whether a physical device can achieve comparable performance.

Could This Make Quantum Computers Simpler?

Despite the lack of experimental confirmation, the researchers emphasize that their proposal does not depend on technologies that have yet to be invented.

The calculations use realistic properties drawn from existing quantum systems, and SQUID sensors are already established tools for detecting exceptionally weak magnetic fields and electric currents.

What remains to be built is the specific arrangement of components required to implement the proposed measurement technique.

"All the numbers we used are realistic. In principle, this can be achieved. In fact, we used parameters from existing systems in our calculations. What doesn't yet exist is that geometry, that setup that we proposed," he argues.

This makes the approach different from theoretical concepts that cannot be realized until scientists develop entirely new materials or manufacturing techniques. In principle, the necessary components already exist, although assembling them into the proposed configuration and demonstrating the predicted behavior remain important challenges.

The research offers two potentially significant advances.

From the standpoint of fundamental physics, it predicts that a qubit can produce an oscillating supercurrent resembling the AC Josephson effect, but without requiring an external voltage. Instead, the oscillations emerge from the qubit's own coherent quantum evolution.

From a technological perspective, the study shows how those oscillations could serve as a measurable signature of the qubit's internal state. By analyzing the electrical current, researchers could recover quantum information while causing very little disturbance to the system.

If future experiments confirm these predictions, the method could make reading Andreev qubits substantially simpler. Eliminating auxiliary qubits and repeated resets could reduce the equipment and procedures needed to retrieve quantum information.

More fundamentally, the work points toward an intriguing possibility: information hidden in the microscopic quantum world may be accessible through an electrical current produced by the quantum system itself, offering a promising new way to observe delicate quantum states without destroying them.

#10550. Best Ecommerce Platforms for Developers: Six Options and the Layers Each Leaves You

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picking an ecommerce platform is really a decision about which layers you want to own: the storefront, the catalog and order logic, the payment flow, and the hosting underneath. Every platform below draws that line in a different place. If you have already settled the front-end question with a comparison like SitePoint's Astro vs Next.js guide, the next question is what sits behind it. Prices are from each vendor's pricing page as of November 2026.

Shopify is the fully managed option. Basic costs $29 a month with card rates from 2.9% plus 30 cents, and a 2% fee applies to orders processed through a third-party gateway on that plan. Developers get two routes: Liquid themes, or Hydrogen, Shopify's headless toolkit, where your framework owns routing and rendering and Hydrogen supplies the Storefront API client and cart. Templates exist for React Router on Oxygen and for Next.js on Vercel. Plus starts at $2,300 a month.

Whop is a business platform for taking payments and sending payouts, and it sits under a storefront you have already built rather than replacing it. The Checkout element runs in the browser with no server code and no API key on the page. You pass your own order ID as metadata, Whop copies it onto the payment, and your server fulfills from a signed webhook that follows the Standard Webhooks spec. Domestic cards cost 2.7% plus 30 cents with no monthly fee, buyers see payment methods chosen by their location across 195 countries, and the same balance pays out in over 200 countries, including to affiliates or sellers you onboard. Whop's own guide shows how to integrate a checkout API into a Next.js storefront end to end. The trade-off is that there is no catalog or shipping engine; you collect address fields at checkout and trigger fulfillment from the webhook.

WooCommerce is a free plugin on WordPress you host yourself, so you own updates, security, and performance. Its payment gateway, WooPayments, charges US merchants 2.9% plus 30 cents per card transaction, 1.5% more for international cards, 2% for currency conversion, and $25 per dispute. Everything is PHP hooks and filters, which makes it the natural pick for agencies already living in WordPress and the wrong pick for anyone who does not want to run a server.

BigCommerce hosts the back end and lets you bring any front end. Core is $29 a month billed annually, capped at $30,000 in trailing annual sales, and plans auto-upgrade as volume grows. There are no platform fees when you use an embedded payment provider, and a 2% fee on Core if you use another one. Catalyst, its reference storefront, is built on Next.js, React Server Components, and the Storefront GraphQL API, and deploys to Vercel.

Medusa is an open-source commerce framework in Node.js and TypeScript, with an MIT-licensed core and a commercial enterprise edition. SitePoint covered it when it was still a young Shopify alternative; it now has over 37,000 GitHub stars. Self-hosting is free. Medusa Cloud starts at $29 a month for development, $99 for launch, and $299 for scale, with no percentage of sales, and the company says you can export your data and self-host at any point.

Saleor is a GraphQL-native, API-only commerce platform written in Python and released under the BSD-3 license. Self-hosting is free. Saleor Cloud starts at $1,599 a month for up to $200,000 in monthly sales, with 0.8% on orders above that, and $3,999 a month at the next tier. It is built for teams with a dedicated engineering function and a large catalog, which is exactly who should pay that price.

Own only the storefront, and Shopify or BigCommerce will carry the rest. Own everything, and Medusa or Saleor give you a back end you can read. Already on WordPress, and WooCommerce is the obvious fit. Already have a storefront in Next.js or Astro and only need the money to move, and Whop is the shortest path. Whichever you pick, the checkout is where disputes start, so log what SitePoint's friendly fraud guide for developers recommends from day one.

#10549. 10 Funniest ‘Far Side’ Comics Parodying Disney Charcters

From 1980 until 1995, no one was safe from the dark wit of Gary Larson, not even Mickey Mouse. For 15 years, while he was publishing his beloved single-panel comic strip The Far Side, Larson satirized just about everything, including popular culture. And while Disney didn’t own Marvel, Star Wars, the Muppets, and so many other brands back then, the company was still a huge target that Larson took aim at several times.

Here are the 10 best Disney parodies featured in Gary Larson’s The Far Side.

Jiminy Cricket, Pinocchio's conscience in the 1940 Disney classic and the ghost of Christmas Past in 1983’s Mickey's Christmas Carol, finds himself in a glass jar in this comic strip. I assume this guy is an entomologist, or at least a bug collector.

As funny as this idea is on its own, there are a few dark details here that add to the macabre humor​​​​​​​. For one, the guy is singing the Pinocchio song “When You Wish Upon a Star,” which makes me wonder if he intentionally lured Jiminy to him. Also, if you look closely in the jar with Jiminy, he’s being kept in some sort of water or solution, with his hat floating on top of the liquid. Is Jiminy already dead?

Pinocchio's conscience isn’t the only one to feel the wrath of Gary Larson. So does the wooden boy himself in this hilarious 1992 comic panel which is all about the worst possible time for Pinocchio to become a real boy.

A year after the lion comic, Larson returned to Pinocchio and made him a victim of yet another feline​​​​​​​. Honestly, if I were a cat, I would definitely turn Pinocchio into a scratching post. He’s perfect for that.

This strip is a play on the word “Mousketeer” — at least, I think it is. This feels like one of those Far Side comics that is more of a rough idea than a perfectly defined joke. Still, the visual alone of this kid on a pirate ship is enough to get a laugh.

I realize that Peter Pan isn’t just a Disney property, but the 1953 animated film is the most famous version of the character, and I’m guessing that’s the version Larson had in mind in 1982 when he depicted Peter Pan having just flown into a window. To be fair, Peter Pan never struck me as the brightest Disney character, so I buy that he might fly himself into a window.

Larson poked fun at another Peter Pan character a decade later, when he showed Captain Hook with a career counselor who is helping him decide between a job in piracy or being a massage therapist. While it’s obvious that he becomes a pirate, I do wonder if Hook might have been happier had he gone the other way (although his patients might have suffered).

Yet another Peter Pan character is featured in this one. If I were the frog​​​​​​​ that managed to catch Tinkerbell, I’d forever have bragging rights at the pond. Good for this frog. He earned it.

The Incredible Journey is a 1963 Disney film based on a 1961 novel by Sheila Burnford. It’s about three lost pets — two dogs and a cat — who travel a long distance​​​​​​​ to reunite with their human family. Kids of the 1990s (like me) may be more familiar with the 1993 remake Homeward Bound: The Incredible Journey starring Don Ameche​​​​​​​ and Michael J. Fox as the dogs and Sally Field as the cat.

To make fun of this movie, all Larson had to do was team up three ridiculously mismatched animals: a cow, a squid, and a snake.​​​​​​​

If Goofy were my pilot, I would also be very worried​​​​​​​. Have you seen how bad skiing goes for that guy? It’s not good. I also kind of wonder how he even got a pilot’s license.

Larson was a master at finding the worst possible scenario to put a character into. Pinocchio on safari is a great example of this, as is Dumbo in a mid-air collision.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

#10547. A hidden limit of space and time could unlock electronics 100,000 times faster

Quantum physics places fundamental limits on what scientists can know about the behavior of particles. Werner Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle states that certain properties, such as a particle's position and momentum, cannot both be measured with unlimited precision. This restriction is not caused by flaws in scientific instruments. It is built into the laws of nature.

But position and time are different. Heisenberg's uncertainty principle does not impose the same restriction on these quantities. Now, researchers have experimentally demonstrated another fundamental limitation: an electron's location and its behavior over time cannot both be determined with arbitrary precision. Known as the "space-time limit," this newly observed relationship could have major implications for the future of quantum technology and ultrafast electronics.

The discovery comes from an international collaboration involving research groups at the Regensburg Center for Ultrafast Nanoscopy (RUN), led by Profs. Jascha Repp, Rupert Huber, Franz Giessibl, and Klaus Richter, along with a team at the Max Planck Institute in Hamburg led by Angel Rubio.

Watching Electrons Move at Incredible Speeds

Understanding how matter behaves at the smallest scales is essential for developing the next generation of technology. Advances in clean energy, quantum computing, and powerful electronics for artificial intelligence all depend on knowing how chemical reactions unfold, how light interacts with materials, and how electrons travel through electronic devices.

However, even the sharpest microscopic photographs cannot reveal the full picture. Scientists also need to observe how these processes change over time, effectively creating slow-motion movies of events occurring at the atomic scale.

At RUN, researchers are developing ultrafast microscopes capable of recording the movements of electrons, atoms, and molecules with extraordinary precision in both space and time. About ten years ago, scientists in Regensburg used ultrafast scanning tunneling microscopy to resolve the movement of a single molecule across space and time for the first time.

Tracking individual electrons presents an even greater challenge. At these microscopic distances, electrons can move roughly a thousand times faster than atoms and molecules, with important changes taking place over just attoseconds.

To appreciate how extreme these scales are, consider that an atom is approximately ten million times smaller than a millimeter. An attosecond is one-billionth of a billionth of a second. The difference between an attosecond and a second is comparable to the difference between one second and the entire age of the universe.

Making these measurements is especially difficult because electrons do not behave like ordinary objects. Their motion follows the unusual laws of quantum mechanics rather than the familiar rules of classical physics.

Ultrafast Lasers Reveal Quantum Tunneling

To observe electron behavior at these extraordinary speeds, the researchers built a new laser system that provides much finer time resolution than earlier experiments. The system allows them to control and study the quantum motion of individual electrons directly.

In the experiments, carefully timed laser pulses drive electrons from an extremely sharp metal tip toward a silver surface. The distance they travel is only a few atomic diameters.

The researchers detect this movement by measuring the resulting electrical current. To determine exactly when the electrons move, they use two pulses of light separated by a precisely controlled interval.

Simon Maier, the lead author of the paper, explains: "By varying the time interval between the two laser pulses, we can directly observe how the electrons respond."

This approach revealed clear evidence of electron motion occurring on attosecond timescales. It showed that the laser pulses could transfer electrons within these incredibly brief intervals while allowing scientists to observe the process as it happened.

What makes the result particularly remarkable is how the electrons travel. Instead of moving like conventional particles, they behave as quantum waves. This allows them to pass through an energy barrier separating the metal tip from the silver surface, even though classical physics predicts they should not have enough energy to cross it.

This phenomenon, known as quantum tunneling, is somewhat like an object passing through a solid wall without breaking or damaging it.

"Our measurement can be understood as a high-speed camera for the electron wave packets, since you can see at what point in time the tunneling process takes place," explains Katharina Glöckl, a doctoral student and co-author of the publication.

To understand the measurements more deeply, researchers working with Prof. Angel Rubio performed sophisticated quantum simulations of the electrons' behavior near the "space-time limit."

Their calculations closely reproduced the experimental findings and uncovered another remarkable detail. The electrons did not respond to the light field instantaneously. Instead, their motion lagged behind it by approximately 500 attoseconds.

At such extreme scales, several fundamental features of quantum physics become important simultaneously.

For example, the researchers found that the laser pulses could not be described entirely by treating light as a wave or entirely by treating it as individual particles called photons. Their effects displayed characteristics of both descriptions. This combination was essential for exploring electron behavior so close to the "space-time limit."

The team also discovered that controlling electron motion over extremely short intervals changes how the electrons are distributed in space. Quantum mechanics describes these electrons using wave packets, which represent how their wave properties extend across different locations.

The relationship between the timing of electron motion and the spatial extent of these wave packets revealed the central limitation behind the discovery.

Raffael Spachtholz, another co-author of the study, explains: "The more precisely we want to pin down the electron's position in time, the more energy we need to provide. And as a result, the electron wave packet spreads out more spatially."

In other words, improving the precision of an electron's timing comes with a cost. The more tightly researchers define when its motion occurs, the more broadly its quantum wave packet spreads through space.

To examine this relationship experimentally, the team placed a single atom on the surface to confine the electron wave packets to an extremely small region immediately before the laser pulses arrived.

This setup allowed the scientists to directly measure the connection between the electrons' spatial distribution and the timing of their motion.

Importantly, even when the electrons received substantial energy from the laser pulses, their wave packets remained sufficiently localized for microscopy with atomic spatial resolution and attosecond time resolution.

The findings provide experimental insight into a fundamental space-time constraint that scientists had previously only suspected. They also make it possible to systematically investigate how changes in electron motion over time affect the spatial structure of quantum wave functions.

Could Quantum Electronics Become Hundreds of Thousands of Times Faster?

Beyond revealing a new aspect of quantum behavior, the discovery could open the door to technologies that operate on timescales far beyond the capabilities of today's electronics.

One especially promising possibility involves using precisely controlled electron wave packets to influence chemical reactions.

Transferring a single electron to a molecule represents the smallest possible transfer of electric charge. But when that electron is concentrated within an exceptionally small region of space and time, the resulting local peak current density can reach as much as 1 trillion amperes per square centimeter.

Such extreme conditions could give scientists new ways to control chemical bonds and observe how molecules change during reactions.

"In the future, we want to use such wave packets to specifically trigger chemical reactions and observe, on the relevant length and time scales, how chemical bonds can be broken or altered," explains Prof. Jascha Repp enthusiastically.

The implications could extend well beyond chemistry. Greater control over electron motion may eventually help engineers develop electronic devices and quantum information systems that operate at speeds approaching the fundamental limits of electron dynamics.

"In the long term, the insights gained could also contribute to operating electronics and quantum information processing at the intrinsic speed limit of electron motion itself -- hundreds of thousands of times faster than the currently dominant CMOS technology," adds Prof. Rupert Huber.

That possibility remains a long term technological goal rather than an achievement demonstrated in the current experiments. Nevertheless, the findings provide a new foundation for exploring how electrons can be manipulated at the smallest distances and shortest timescales.

For Repp and Huber, the results suggest that the range of potential applications is extraordinarily broad. They believe that future advances in controlling electrons at the space-time limit may ultimately depend more on human creativity than on the constraints imposed by nature.

#10541. Inside this quantum computer, memory vibrates like a guitar string

A quantum computer might seem to have little in common with a guitar, but researchers at ETH Zurich have found a surprising connection. Physicist Yiwen Chu and her team have developed a system that uses microscopic mechanical vibrations to store and manipulate quantum information, much like the vibrating strings of a guitar produce musical notes.

Unlike the sounds produced by a musical instrument, these vibrations occur at frequencies far beyond human hearing. They take place within a tiny quantum chip, where they serve as a new form of computer memory.

By harnessing these vibrations, the researchers have created a way for quantum computers to process information efficiently while accessing a flexible working memory. "The interaction between the quantum processor and the quantum memory provides a crucial foundation with a view to establishing quantum computers as a powerful and reliable way to perform computations that are not feasible with conventional computers," says Yiwen Chu.

The breakthrough, described in the journal Science, introduces a different approach to quantum computer design. Instead of closely integrating information processing and storage, as many existing systems do, Chu's team has developed an architecture that gives these functions more distinct roles.

Quantum Memory Inspired by Conventional Computers

The researchers drew inspiration from the basic design of everyday digital computers. In a conventional machine, the central processing unit (CPU) carries out calculations while information is held separately in working memory, commonly called random access memory (RAM). The arrangement of these components plays an important role in determining how efficiently a computer operates.

Chu's quantum system follows a similar principle. A superconducting qubit acts as the processing and control unit, performing a role comparable to that of the central processing unit (CPU). Meanwhile, a separate quantum memory temporarily holds the information needed during calculations, allowing the processor to retrieve and modify it as necessary.

The major difference lies in how that information is stored.

"In our quantum working memory, however, information is not stored electromagnetically -- as is usually the case today -- but rather in the form of mechanical vibrations," explains Chu.

Quantum computers take advantage of unusual properties of quantum physics, including superposition, which allows quantum systems to exist in combinations of different states, and entanglement, which creates powerful connections between quantum states. These effects could eventually enable quantum machines to tackle certain extremely complicated calculations much faster than conventional computers, including some problems that are currently beyond their practical capabilities.

Why Vibrating Quantum Memory Could Be Better

One of the greatest challenges in quantum computing is maintaining precise control over quantum information. The states used for calculations are delicate, and even small disturbances can cause information to be lost. To perform calculations reliably, the processor and memory must interact closely enough to transfer and manipulate quantum states with high precision.

In the new system, microscopic structures called mechanical resonators hold information in particular patterns of vibration. When the superconducting qubit needs that information, it accesses the stored vibrational state, performs the required operation, and returns the modified information to memory.

Many existing quantum computers instead use electromagnetic memory alongside superconducting qubits. Researchers understand these technologies well, and they can be combined to provide extremely accurate control over quantum states, including reading, changing, and manipulating stored information.

However, electromagnetic memory systems have a significant disadvantage. They can occupy considerable space, making it difficult to expand experimental quantum devices into the larger, more capable computers that researchers and industries ultimately need.

Mechanical resonators offer several potential advantages. They are substantially smaller, allowing more compact designs. They can also support numerous different patterns of vibration, providing opportunities to store more information simultaneously than conventional electromagnetic memory.

Another important benefit is that mechanical vibrations can preserve quantum states for longer periods before the stored information begins to fade. Longer storage times could make it easier to carry out complicated calculations that require information to remain available throughout multiple processing steps.

The research published in Science provides the first experimental demonstration that mechanical resonators can work together with superconducting qubits to carry out quantum computations. The results establish that vibration-based memory is a feasible alternative to electromagnetic storage.

The achievement goes beyond simply storing information. Chu's team demonstrated that its architecture can handle both relatively straightforward calculations and more demanding quantum operations.

To evaluate the system, the researchers tested two important computational techniques: the quantum Fourier transform and period finding. Both are fundamental tools in quantum computing and require a computer to coordinate multiple quantum states with considerable precision.

"The Quantum Fourier Transform is a fundamental computational procedure required for many quantum algorithms. The period-finding algorithm we implemented served as a demonstration of how this procedure can be used," explains Igor Kladaric, doctoral student in Chu's team and co-author of the publication.

Successfully completing these tasks requires the system to store, control, and maintain coherent relationships among multiple quantum states at the same time. The experiments showed that the new architecture can meet these requirements, providing strong evidence that it can support genuine quantum computation rather than simply function as a memory device.

A Promising Step Toward Powerful Quantum Computers

One of the most significant findings is that the new system can, in principle, carry out all the fundamental operations needed for arbitrary quantum computations. This means the architecture has the essential capabilities required for a programmable, general-purpose quantum computer.

However, demonstrating those capabilities in an experimental chip is only the beginning. The next major challenge is scaling the technology to larger systems with greater processing power while preserving the precision and reliability demonstrated in the laboratory.

Chu and her colleagues are continuing to investigate how their approach can be developed further. Its long-term success will depend on whether mechanical quantum memory can remain effective as more components and increasingly complex computations are added.

Practical quantum computers powerful enough for widespread scientific and industrial applications are still some distance away. Nevertheless, by showing that tiny mechanical vibrations can serve as compact, capable quantum memory, the ETH Zurich team has demonstrated a promising new route toward making those machines a reality.

#10537. AI-powered bionic eye breakthrough could help the blind see again

Artificial intelligence could help overcome one of the biggest challenges in restoring vision to people who are blind: getting a brain implant to produce reliable visual experiences. Researchers from three institutions, including UC Santa Barbara, have demonstrated that AI can improve how precisely an experimental visual prosthesis stimulates the brain and predicts what a person perceives.

The team used a deep learning system to develop electrical stimulation patterns for electrodes temporarily implanted in the visual cortex, the region at the back of the brain responsible for processing visual information. By analyzing the brain's responses, the technology gave researchers greater control over neural activity and helped them better anticipate the visual sensations produced by the implant.

Published online in the journal Neuron, the proof-of-concept study represents progress toward more advanced visual prostheses, sometimes described as bionic eyes. These devices could eventually become more effective by learning how each person's brain responds to stimulation rather than relying on predetermined settings.

"Building a model in the abstract is one thing," said Michael Beyeler, an associate professor of computer science and psychological and brain sciences at UC Santa Barbara. "Seeing it shape an experiment with a person is something else entirely. That ability to go from theory to something that may one day help people is what drives much of the work in our lab."

How Bionic Vision Could Bypass Damaged Eyes

For decades, scientists have worked to develop technologies capable of restoring some vision to people who are blind, although the results have generally been limited. Some experimental devices stimulate the retina, the layer of tissue at the back of the eye that detects light. Others attempt to activate parts of the visual system farther along the pathway that carries visual information to the brain.

Visual cortical prostheses work differently. Instead of depending on the eyes or optic nerves, they deliver electrical signals directly to the visual cortex. This approach could offer a potential solution for people whose visual pathways have been damaged but whose visual cortex can still respond to stimulation. Possible candidates include individuals who lost their sight because of strokes, neurodegenerative diseases, or traumatic brain injuries.

The ability to regain even limited vision could be especially meaningful for people who remember what it was like to see.

Beyeler highlighted those "who have been able to see for part of their life, but then, because of an inherited eye disease or an accident, have lost their vision," adding, "For those people, the desire may be very strong to get some vision back."

The new findings build on earlier computational research supported by Beyeler's 2022 National Institutes of Health (NIH) Director's New Innovator Award. The five-year, $2 million grant has supported his efforts to make artificial vision systems more accurate, predictable, and effective.

Scientists Test AI With a Brain Implant in a Blind Man

An important opportunity to test the technology came in 2024, when researchers from Beyeler's Bionic Vision Lab traveled to Spain. Their goal was to determine whether their computer models could improve the performance of a brain implant already being used in an experimental study.

At Hospital IMED Elche, the team worked with a 27-year-old man who had become blind following a traumatic brain injury. He had received an implant containing 96 electrodes arranged in an array within his visual cortex. The device was intended to remain in place for six months before being removed.

When researchers activated the electrodes using electrical current, the participant experienced visual sensations known as phosphenes. These can appear as spots, flashes, or shapes of light, sometimes resembling stars or fireworks.

Producing these sensations is possible, but making them consistent and predictable is much more difficult. The same electrical stimulation does not always trigger an identical response, and activating particular electrodes does not guarantee that the person will perceive a specific visual pattern.

To address this problem, the researchers trained a deep neural network, a type of AI designed to recognize complex patterns, to predict how the visual cortex would respond to different electrical stimulation settings. The model also considered the brain's resting activity immediately before stimulation, giving it information about the participant's current neural state.

Using these predictions, the researchers identified combinations of electrical signals that were most likely to produce particular patterns of brain activity.

When the scientists tested the stimulation patterns selected by AI, the results were encouraging. The new approach generated the intended patterns of neural activity more accurately than the comparison methods while requiring less electrical current.

Another important discovery emerged when the team examined the relationship between brain activity and the participant's visual experiences. The patterns recorded directly from his brain were better predictors of what he perceived than the stimulation settings themselves.

This distinction matters because activating the brain is not the same as knowing what a person will see. Even when researchers can produce a desired neural response, they still need to understand how the brain transforms that activity into a conscious visual experience.

"Engineers naturally want to treat phosphenes like pixels: stimulate more electrodes, and you should get a more complete image," Beyeler said. "But the brain does not work that way. Electrodes interact, neural responses fluctuate, and what we put into the brain is not necessarily what the person perceives. The challenge is to learn more about that transformation."

The findings suggest that future visual prostheses may need to do more than simply deliver electrical signals. They may also need to monitor how the brain reacts and use that feedback to continually improve stimulation.

Teaching Brain Implants to Adapt to Each Person

A key advantage of the implant used in the experiment was its ability to perform two tasks. It could stimulate the visual cortex with electrical current while simultaneously recording the resulting neural activity.

This allowed researchers to train their AI model using the brain's actual responses rather than relying exclusively on information about which electrodes were activated and how much current they delivered.

The participant also provided feedback about what he experienced. He reported whether he saw a phosphene and, during certain experiments, described its appearance, including its shape, size, brightness, and color.

Comparing these reports with the recorded brain activity revealed that neural responses contained more useful information about the perceived visual sensations than the electrical stimulation parameters alone.

The AI system also used measurements of resting brain activity to account for changes in the participant's neural state before each stimulation. This could be particularly valuable because the brain's response to electrical signals can vary over time, including from one day to the next.

By adapting stimulation to these changing conditions, future prostheses might provide more consistent visual experiences without requiring users to adjust to a rigid set of signals.

"A useful visual prosthesis cannot rely on a fixed recipe," Beyeler said. "It has to learn how an individual brain responds and adapt the stimulation accordingly. Ultimately, the device should adapt to the person, not the other way around."

A Collaborative Step Toward Restoring Sight

The research brought together scientists from UC Santa Barbara, ETH Zurich, and Miguel Hernández University (MHU). Beyeler described the effort as "a true collaboration."

The project was led by co-first authors Pehuén Moure of ETH Zurich, Jacob Granley of UC Santa Barbara, and Fabrizio Grani of MHU. The supervising researchers were Beyeler, Shih-Chii Liu of ETH Zurich, and Eduardo Fernández of MHU. Their work forms part of a larger feasibility trial taking place in Spain.

Although the technology remains experimental and the participant's visual experiences were limited to flashes and shapes of light, the results demonstrate how AI could improve communication between artificial vision devices and the brain.

Rather than treating every brain as if it responds the same way, future bionic vision systems could learn from each individual and adjust their signals accordingly. That personalized approach may prove essential to developing implants that can eventually restore useful vision to people who have lost their sight.

#10535. CrowdStrike is buying Zscaler to create a cybersecurity giant built for the era of AI agents

Two cybersecurity companies are coming together in one of the industry’s biggest ever mergers.

On Monday, November 23, CrowdStrike announced that it will purchase Zscaler in an all-stock deal. The transaction values Zscaler at a whopping $43.9 billion, with the company’s shareholders getting 1.135 CrowdStrike common shares (Nasdaq: CRWD) for each Zscaler share (Nasdaq: ZS) they own.  

“Security used to be about keeping the adversary out. Today, safety is required to let AI in,” said George Kurtz, CEO and founder of CrowdStrike. “The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data.”

CrowdStrike stock falls, Zscaler rises

In response to the news, CrowdStrike’s shares fell more than 8% in premarket trading, while Zscaler shares rose over 17%. 

CrowdStrike expects the deal to expand its total addressable market by $185 billion, with the combined company bringing in over $10 billion in annual recurring revenue. 

Kurtz will stay on as CEO of the joint company, while Jay Chaudhry, founder and CEO of Zscaler, will lead Zscaler’s business and become a member of CrowdStrike’s board. 

The deal is expected to close in the second half of 2028, subject to regulatory approvals. It comes amid growing concerns over vulnerable tech infrastructure and a future of bot-fueled cyberattacks.

“With Zscaler, we’re building cybersecurity’s operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely,” Kurtz added. “We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen.”

Alongside the purchasing announcement, CrowdStrike and Zscaler both shared their quarterly earnings reports.

Kurtz called CrowdStrike’s most recent quarter the company’s best yet. CrowdStrike had record-high cash flow from operations with $665 million. Its revenue rose 28% year-over-year (YOY) to $1.58 billion. 

Similarly, Zscaler’s quarterly revenue rose 25% YOY to $898.2 million.

#10533. This is one of your kitchen’s hardest working tools. Here’s how to keep it food safe

The cutting board might be the hardest working tool in the kitchen yet one thought about least. It takes the raw chicken, then the onions, then sometimes the bread, and in between it typically gets a quick swipe with the nearest sponge.

If cutting boards are given a second thought, it’s usually in the debate over whether to use wood or plastic. But the more important issue is how to clean and care for the boards you already own.

“I think the real question is not which is safer, but are they both safe to use?” said Donald Schaffner, a professor of food science at Rutgers University who studies how bacteria move between surfaces.

Knife fight! Plastic vs. wood boards

For a long time, plastic was considered the safer choice, mostly because it seemed easier to clean. Then in the 1990s, Dean Cliver, a food safety researcher at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, put salmonella and E. coli on wood boards and found the bacteria were pulled down into the wood, where they didn’t multiply and eventually died off.

Plastic boards were easy to clean, but once they were all scratched up, bacteria-harboring residue like chicken fat settled into the knife grooves and was hard to scrub out by hand.

Schaffner said the research is “a bit muddied,” but Cliver’s work showed “wood was not as risky as we thought.” Wood also has natural antimicrobial compounds that seem to help, at least somewhat.

Nicole Papantoniou, senior director of the Kitchen & Cooking Lab at the Good Housekeeping Institute, doesn’t pick a side. She uses both.

Beyond wood and plastic cutting boards

Papantoniou is a fan of composite boards, made from wood or paper fibers bonded with resin. They’re light and they hold up.

One paper composite board made by Epicurean was a winner of Good Housekeeping’s 2027 Kitchen Awards. Epicurean CEO Dave Benson said knife cuts in composite stay open instead of closing back over bits of food the way they can on plastic, which makes the boards easier to clean.

Schaffner pointed out that there isn’t much independent research yet on how composite or bamboo boards stack up against wood and plastic.

Rubber boards, common in restaurant kitchens, are easy on knives but not many are made for home use, Papantoniou said.

Glass is nonporous, and she still wouldn’t recommend it: It slides, it can break and it dulls knives. And marble and slate look gorgeous under cheese and charcuterie, but don’t cut on them. “Your poor knife,” she said.

How to clean your cutting board

For everyday chopping, Papantoniou said washing with warm, soapy water and letting the board air dry is plenty.

But truly sanitizing takes an extra step. It should be done after a board has already been cleaned to kill bacteria the washing can leave behind.

Hot water from the tap isn’t going to get you there. “If the water were hot enough to actually kill bacteria, it would also burn our hands,” Schaffner said.

After raw meat or poultry, Schaffner sanitizes, either by carefully pouring boiling water over the clean board, running it through the dishwasher on the sanitize cycle or using bleach diluted according to the directions on the bottle. Vinegar and hydrogen peroxide “are not very effective, at least not nearly as effective as bleach,” he said.

Wood should be washed by hand, since the dishwasher or a long soak in the sink can warp or crack it. Plastic and composite boards can go in the dishwasher. It’s why Schaffner saves the plastic ones for raw meat and poultry.

Keeping separate boards for raw meat and produce is “a perfectly reasonable food safety precaution,” he said, but it’s not a must. Just remember that bacteria travel with water, so a wet chicken breast can spread them to the board, to your hands and to whatever you grab next (often the pepper grinder).

Extra care tips for wood boards

While they look great on a kitchen counter, wood does need more babying than plastic.

Papantoniou said wood boards should be oiled and kept conditioned. That’s less about food safety and more about keeping the board in good shape for years, and it’s a lot easier to keep up with if you start when it’s new. Wood boards should also dry standing on their edges so air can get to both sides.

John Boos & Co. has been making butcher block cutting boards in Effingham, Illinois, since 1887.

“Oil wood cutting boards once a month, and more often if you use them daily for meal prep,” said Lisa Bosio, vice president of group marketing at the company. When the wood looks lighter and a little dry, it’s due, she said. Boos recommends a food-safe mineral oil, applied in a light layer, left to soak in overnight and wiped off in the morning.

For stubborn stains, like those from tomatoes or beets, Noah Pinsonnault, home care and cleaning reviews analyst at the Good Housekeeping Institute, suggests 3% hydrogen peroxide left on for about five minutes and then rinsed off. Peroxide is a mild bleach and can lighten the wood, especially darker varieties like walnut, so try it on a small spot first.

If the board smells like last night’s garlic, scrub it with a paste of baking soda and water, rinse and let it dry completely, Pinsonnault said.

When to toss your cutting board

There’s no hard rule for when a board needs replacing. Schaffner gets rid of one when it becomes increasingly hard to get it visibly clean. Papantoniou tosses boards that warp, plastic boards with deep grooves or little curls that could end up in food, and wood boards that crack or develop dark spots that could be mold.

Katie Workman writes regularly about food for The Associated Press. She has written two cookbooks focused on family-friendly cooking, “Dinner Solved!” and “The Mom 100 Cookbook.” She blogs at https://themom100.com/. She can be reached at Katie@themom100.com.

—Katie Workman, Associated Press

#10531. Tiny flaws in diamonds could unlock a quantum computing revolution

A new computational technique could accelerate the search for diamond materials capable of powering future quantum computers and highly sensitive sensors. Developed by a researcher at the U.S. Department of Energy's (DOE) Princeton Plasma Physics Laboratory (PPPL), the method makes it much easier to identify promising materials for quantum technology, potentially opening the door to advances across many areas of science.

The approach has already revealed new candidates for quantum computing and sensing applications. The findings were recently published in ACS Central Science and have attracted attention from other researchers in the field.

One of the biggest challenges in developing quantum technology is finding materials that can maintain delicate quantum states under practical conditions. Many candidates require extremely low temperatures to function reliably. Diamond materials stand out because certain quantum properties can remain useful even at room temperature.

This capability could help advance technologies that are important to national research priorities and the development of next-generation computing. Quantum sensors promise levels of sensitivity and precision beyond those of conventional instruments, while quantum computers could eventually solve certain extraordinarily complex problems much faster than today's most powerful supercomputers.

Why Imperfect Diamonds Could Be the Key to Quantum Computing

Although diamonds are made of carbon atoms, a flawless diamond crystal is not enough to create a useful quantum computing system. The key lies in tiny irregularities within the crystal structure.

These imperfections, known as defects, can occur when a carbon atom is absent from its normal position, when another element replaces a carbon atom, or when both changes occur together. The atoms surrounding these defects can form qubits, the basic units used to store and process quantum information in quantum computers and sensors.

Finding the most useful defects, however, has been difficult. Accurately modeling their quantum behavior traditionally requires enormous computational resources, making it challenging to evaluate large numbers of possible candidates.

A Simpler Way to Discover Better Diamond Qubits

Mark Martirez, a staff research scientist at PPPL, developed a way to dramatically reduce the complexity of these calculations. Instead of simulating a much larger diamond structure, he isolates a small region containing just 16 to 30 atoms around a defect.

By "carving out" this tiny atomic cluster and treating it like a separate molecule, Martirez can use powerful computational methods that chemists have spent decades refining. These established techniques make it possible to analyze the quantum properties of diamond defects much more efficiently without sacrificing the high level of accuracy needed for the calculations.

"Because we're treating the qubits as molecules, the cost of calculating these defects is equivalent to the cost of just modeling a simple molecule," Martirez said. "This method really opened the floodgates" in terms of how qubits can be assessed.

The new technique allows researchers to calculate how different qubits respond to laser light and microwave pulses far more quickly than before. Using this approach, the team identified promising defects involving boron and oxygen that could serve as new types of qubits.

New Diamond Defects Reveal a Spectrum of Quantum Colors

Light plays an important role in how scientists interact with diamond qubits. Researchers use lasers and microwave pulses to manipulate their quantum states and retrieve stored information.

Much of the previous work in this area has centered on a defect known as the nitrogen-vacancy center. This structure forms when a nitrogen atom takes the place of a carbon atom in a diamond crystal, while an adjacent position that would normally contain another carbon atom remains empty.

Under laser illumination, this type of defect can emit red light when it occupies a particular quantum state.

The newly investigated defects offer different optical properties. Oxygen defects can produce blue light, while those involving boron emit ultraviolet light.

These differences could make it possible to combine several types of qubits within a single quantum device. Because each type responds differently to light, researchers might be able to interact with one group of qubits without disturbing another.

"One could imagine, then, combining different qubits of different responses, so you can basically probe one qubit but not the other," Martirez said.

Beyond identifying new candidates, the findings suggest a broader change in how researchers approach quantum technology. Diamond qubits have traditionally been studied primarily through physics, but understanding their underlying chemistry could provide additional ways to design them.

Martirez believes that researchers could eventually engineer qubits for particular functions using principles similar to those applied when chemists create specialized molecules, including compounds designed for new medications.

How Quantum Chemistry Could Transform Future Research

The potential benefits extend well beyond the development of quantum computers themselves. Martirez sees an intriguing possibility in which advances made possible by quantum chemistry eventually lead to quantum technologies that help improve the field of chemistry.

In other words, quantum chemistry could develop the very tools needed to "enrich itself" in the coming decades.

A practical quantum system containing multiple qubits could make molecular calculations substantially more accurate. That would allow scientists to study increasingly complex chemical structures, including large biological molecules and proteins, with a level of detail that is difficult to achieve using conventional methods.

"We could describe larger molecules, biomolecules, proteins and everything like that in a more accurate way, and that could improve all the chemical-adjacent industries, such as pharmaceutical science and materials science. Even our fundamental understanding of molecular systems could be enriched."

Such improvements could have far-reaching implications for pharmaceutical development, materials research, and the fundamental study of how molecules behave.

Martirez also hopes the research will encourage more chemists to explore solid-state physics. Although building advanced quantum computers is often viewed as a challenge primarily for physicists, he argues that chemistry offers valuable expertise that could help accelerate progress.

"At this moment in time, there's a great emphasis on building the first state-of-the-art qubit computer," he said. "But it's not just physicists who should be participating in this research. Chemists have decades of developed intuition about chemical systems that could be easily translated into understanding defects in solids as well."

By bringing these disciplines together, scientists may be able to develop more effective ways to discover, understand, and eventually engineer the quantum materials needed for future technologies.

The research was funded through PPPL's Laboratory Directed Research and Development program under prime contract number DE-AC02-09CH11466, with additional support from the DOE Office of Science, Office of Fusion Energy Sciences.
