#10530. Your SSL certificates can now last 200 days at most: The real risk isn’t renewal

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every SSL certificate comes with a limited lifespan. Following the latest update from the CA/Browser Forum, the maximum lifetime of a certificate has fallen from 398 days to 200, and it will drop to 100 days in March 2027 and 47 days in 2029. Renewal is no longer an annual chore. It’s an ongoing task that only stays manageable with automation.

So what does this mean if you already look after sites with SSL certificates, and will it bring drastic changes? For many setups, very little will change. For others, one missed renewal can mean an outage. This article explains what’s changing and what to do if you’re in the second group.

Today, an SSL certificate is valid for 200 days at most. The limit applies when a certificate is issued, so the ones you already hold keep their original expiration date.

Where you feel it depends on how your certificates renew. Sites on managed hosting platforms or behind a content delivery network (CDN) usually renew automatically already, often every 90 days, so the new limit changes little. The pressure lands on setups where someone buys a certificate once a year and installs it by hand. At 47 days, that becomes about 12 renewals a year.

A certificate is a promise from a certificate authority that a key really belongs to a domain, and that promise gets less reliable the longer it runs. If a private key leaks, or a certificate is issued by mistake, it stays usable until it expires. Cutting the lifetime shrinks that window, so a problem that once lingered for over a year is now fixed in weeks.

Shorter lifetimes also make revocation, the process for canceling a bad certificate, far less critical. Revocation has never worked perfectly, but a certificate that expires within weeks matters much less if a cancellation doesn’t reach every browser.

Another reason is about people. A task done once a year is easy to forget and hard to check from outside. Browser makers have pushed for years to move the industry onto automatic renewal, on the logic that a process that runs every few weeks gets tested constantly. If it breaks, you notice quickly instead of discovering it on the day the certificate expires.

For most people, the first effect is simply more work. A certificate that once needed attention every year will soon need it every few weeks. Plus, each renewal is a small chance for something to go wrong. Multiply that by dozens of sites, mail servers, and devices, and the chance of one slipping through grows quickly.

The safety nets are also thinner. If you relied on the renewal emails, nothing is watching your certificates now. Additionally, with a shorter life, there’s less time between this should have renewed and this has expired.

An expired certificate doesn’t make a site slow or dated. It makes it unreachable for most visitors. Chrome, Firefox, and Safari all show a full-page warning that the connection isn’t private, and most people leave instead of clicking through. If the site uses HSTS, a setting that tells browsers to connect only over secure links, there is often no way to click through at all, and visitors are blocked outright until the certificate is fixed.

The damage doesn’t stop at the home page. Checkouts, contact forms, and logins that depend on the secure connection fail along with it, often without an error message that points at the real cause. Other systems break, too, because apps and integrations that call your site over a secure link refuse to connect to an expired certificate.

Nothing about your site has to change for any of this to happen. A date passes, and a site that worked on Friday is down on Monday. In a 47-day lifetime, that date comes around about every six weeks.

Automatic renewal tools do the heavy lifting well. The failures happen around them:

Look at the five failures above and you’ll see what they share. Each one happens quietly. The renewal job ran, or looked like it did, and nobody checked what the server was actually serving afterwards. Automation can tell you it tried. It can’t tell you the result reached your visitors.

What closes that gap is a check that never relies on your server’s own account of events. Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring does exactly that. It reads the certificate installed on your web server from the cloud, the way a visitor’s browser would, and alerts you before it expires, using thresholds you choose. If a renewal fails quietly, you hear about it from the monitor and not from a customer.

For each certificate, it records the issuing authority, the owner, the issue date, the expiration date, and the days remaining, including on servers that host several certificates behind one address. That last number matters more as lifespans shrink. When a certificate lasts 47 days, the days left is the figure that tells you a renewal is overdue, and you can set your warning against it using the quarter-of-the-lifetime rule covered next.

Once a monitor is watching your certificates, the next question is when it should speak up.

Most expiration alerts are set to a fixed number of days: warn at 30 days left and escalate at seven. That works for a certificate that lasts a year and renews with a month to spare. It breaks when the whole certificate lasts for a limited number of days.

Here’s why: a healthy certificate renews with about a third of its life still left. If your alert appears earlier than that, it goes off on certificates that are doing nothing wrong, and soon everyone ignores it. As lifespans shrink, a fixed 30-day alert will start doing exactly that.

The fix is to set the alerts based on lateness, not on how close the expiry date is. Warn when a quarter of the lifetime is left, since a renewal is overdue by then, and escalate when a tenth is left. Because these are shares of the lifetime, they keep working, however short lifespans get.

The next cut lands on March 15, 2027, so a few checks now will save you from scrambling later:

Shorter lifespans won’t break your site, but a missed renewal will, so fix the quiet failures now while a mistake still costs an afternoon to fix, not a Monday morning outage affecting lots of customers.

If you’d like a second pair of eyes on your certificates, you can start a 30-day free trial of Site24x7’s SSL/TLS certificate monitoring.

#10528. Why Nvidia brought Jensen Huang back as CEO despite his scandals

It took just 11 days for Nvidia to reverse course on Jensen Huang, its totemic CEO, who is returning to his position as president and CEO, as well as a member of the board.

The decision also appears to have received the White House’s tacit approval, according to Nvidia’s surprise announcement of Huang’s return. That’s despite an ongoing independent investigation into whether Huang was targeted by Chinese intelligence through a woman with whom he has acknowledged having an affair. And the controversy extends beyond that relationship: Huang has also faced allegations of at least one, and possibly two, other affairs with subordinates. During his brief absence from the company, he also faced criticism over his reportedly toxic management style.

“I am honored to return to NVIDIA and lead one of the most consequential technology companies in the world at the beginning of a new industrial revolution,” Huang said in a statement.

His return to power echoes Sam Altman’s brief sojourn outside OpenAI—an episode that marked its three-year anniversary last week—and highlights just how important individual leaders have become to the success of companies like Nvidia and OpenAI.

“It’s a stunning return,” says Kristy Loke, a research fellow at MATS studying China’s place in the broader AI world—and Nvidia’s role in the global geopolitics of AI. “It doesn’t necessarily mark the end of the chip debates in D.C., but it speaks to the centrality of Jensen as the chief strategist for Nvidia and as the force that’s holding the many pieces of the AI ecosystem together.”

That centrality was evident in the groundswell of support for Huang’s reinstatement across Silicon Valley over the past week. A veritable who’s who of tech executives spoke up for him. Elon Musk was among those calling for Huang’s return on November 14, warning that many of Nvidia’s most important supply-chain deals rested on personal relationships with Huang rather than written contracts. Uber co-founder Travis Kalanick also backed him, arguing that his mistakes did not warrant removing him from the company he built. By November 16, a petition calling for Huang’s reinstatement had attracted more than 180,000 signatures, including those of Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, Replit CEO Amjad Masad, and more than 8,800 people identifying themselves as Nvidia employees.

Nvidia’s board appears to have reached a similar conclusion about Huang’s integral role in running the company, owing in large part to his hands-on management style. “This is a board that reversed itself because the alternative, a founder-less Nvidia at the start of the Rubin cycle with a CEO search running into next year, was the bigger risk to shareholders,” writes Dan Ives, principal analyst at Yorkville Ives & Co.

Nvidia is partway through launching its Rubin chip platform, with updates due later this year—a process Huang will likely want to oversee closely, given that the platform has already generated $20 billion in its first quarter of production. “It chose to pair the reversal with the governance architecture investors will want to see around him,” Ives adds.

Ives has attempted to put a price on Huang’s importance. In his estimation, Nvidia is worth $300 a share with Huang back in charge and its governance troubles resolved, versus $225 without him—a difference of a third. Even so, he has maintained his formal price target of $225 while the investigation continues.

Investors also appear confident in Huang’s return, with Nvidia shares surging nearly 30% in the first few minutes of trading following the announcement.

Huang’s 11 days in the wilderness were more than twice as long as Altman’s absence from OpenAI, but his grip on Nvidia appears no less significant. As Fast Company previously reported, the skills and responsibilities any permanent replacement would need to master make for a daunting list. They include balancing the competing interests of the White House and Beijing while managing a complex web of informal relationships with Taiwanese suppliers—relationships that have helped cement Nvidia’s seemingly unassailable lead in the AI chip race.

It’s Huang’s relationship with Washington that may have proved most important, reckons MATS’ Loke. “Trump’s presidency in his second term is very much an AI presidency,” she explains. “The same goes for the American stock market. Huang is the key node, through circular financing, support for the open model ecosystem, and also being a key supplier of AI hardware.”

“He is, it seems in hindsight to some,” Loke adds, “in fact irreplaceable.”

#10526. A 1992 Batmobile is going to auction, but don’t expect to see it out on the road

It was hijacked by the Penguin, careened through Gotham’s streets and lived beneath Wayne Manor. Now it can be yours — if you have a Bruce Wayne-sized bank account.

The Batmobile — one of three custom-made versions used in Tim Burton’s 1992 blockbuster “Batman Returns” — will hit the auction block Monday in Gardena, near Los Angeles, as the centerpiece of a Julien’s Auctions sale that features dozens of pieces of Batman and other superhero memorabilia.

With a preauction estimate of $5 million to $7 million, the vehicle could break the Batmobile auction record set in 2013, when the car driven by Adam West in the 1960s TV series sold for $4.6 million including fees.

Known as the “Number One Hero car” and measuring just under 20 feet (about 6 meters) long with two sweeping tail fins, the glossy black vehicle appeared in the majority of the film’s driving sequences, Julien’s said in auction notes on its website.

Since then it has spent years on display at the Marconi Automotive Museum in Tustin, California. It still has its original paint, Chevrolet V-8 engine and radio-controlled flaming exhaust system that Michael Keaton’s Batman used to torch one of the Penguin’s circus goons.

Citing the influence of Burton’s “darker aesthetic” on modern superhero movies, Julien’s called the car “one of the most recognized and important cinematic vehicles in Hollywood history.”

No matter who buys this Batmobile, don’t expect to see it on the road: Julien’s noted that the vehicle is “not street legal” and the engine and transmission have been tested a “limited number of times” since filming concluded.

The buyer must also adhere to various restrictions, according to an agreement with Warner Bros. Consumer Products Inc., which handles DC Comics’ intellectual property rights.

For example the buyer must secure approval before exhibiting it at special events, and the vehicle “must remain stationary when exhibited to the public,” the auction house said.

—R.J. Rico, Associated Press

#10524. Young Americans can’t afford to move out. These cities are seeing the biggest increases

A near-record share of young adults won’t be traveling home to visit family for the holidays—they’re already living there.

One in three U.S. adults between age 18 and 34 live with their parents, according to a new report from Realtor.com. That’s 25.2 million or 33% of young adults living at home in 2025, only a sliver less than the 33.6% during the pandemic-era peak in 2020. 

Living at home is one way that Americans who can’t afford to buy or rent on their own wrestle with the high cost of housing and other expenses. While the cost of housing is a nationwide problem, the share of adults living with their parents offers an interesting glimpse into regional affordability patterns around the country.

“The pattern of young adults at home has spread beyond the most expensive markets, with the share rising most in parts of the South and West, including some that have traditionally supported an earlier launch,” Realtor.com’s chief economist, Danielle Hale, wrote in the report. “As young people adapt to high housing costs, they are delaying homeownership and the wealth creation that comes with it.”

Two stories with the same ending

Predictably, at least one of the country’s priciest cities is among those with the largest share of adults living with their parents—but the full story is more complicated. New York leads the list, with 41.3% of adults between the ages of 18 and 24 staying in the nest. In Philadelphia, Miami, Baltimore, and Houston, more than 38% of adults in that age range lived with their parents. Across Los Angeles, Atlanta, and Chicago, the share of adults living at home was over 35%. 

Houston, Atlanta, and Baltimore aren’t known for their high cost of living, unlike costly urban hubs like New York and Los Angeles, but home prices have soared in recent years in those markets. That rapid change to local affordability is freezing many young adults out of a housing market they might have been able to afford during a previous era.

The share of adults living at home has grown the quickest in Las Vegas, San Francisco, and San Jose, but those metros tell different stories. In San Francisco and San Jose, two expensive cities close to the AI boom, housing prices have always been high, even as they grow higher still. In Las Vegas, once known as a relatively affordable market in the West, home prices have skyrocketed by more than 60% since 2019.

“Las Vegas and the Bay Area show two different paths to the same challenge,” wrote Hannah Jones, senior economist at Realtor.com. “In Las Vegas, the sharp rise in home prices has made it harder for young adults to buy. In San Francisco and San Jose, already-high housing costs and limited inventory leave many young adults facing steep barriers to living on their own.”

Living at home on the rise

Regionally, the Northeast still dominates with 36.9% of young adults living at home in 2025, though that number is trending down. The West is next with a 32.4% “co-residence” rate, followed by 32.3% in the South and 31.7% in the Midwest. 

That share is rising everywhere except the Northeast, demonstrating that the housing affordability crisis may look distinct in different corners of the country, but it’s something that a surprising share of Americans everywhere are struggling with.

“For young adults who hope to live independently or buy a home, elevated costs and limited entry-level supply can delay household formation and, for would-be buyers, the opportunity to build home equity,” Jones said. “Until affordability improves and entry-level supply expands, the report finds that pent-up demand is likely to continue accumulating.”

#10523. Quantum computers are getting too powerful to check. Scientists have a solution

As quantum computers grow more powerful, researchers face a troubling question: How can they know whether the results are correct when conventional computers can no longer verify them? Scientists have now developed a promising new method that could help overcome this challenge by measuring the accuracy of quantum simulations and identifying how much uncertainty their predictions contain.

The technique uses experimental measurements to reconstruct how a quantum system behaves, then calculates how uncertainties in that reconstruction affect the results. Researchers successfully demonstrated the approach using a quantum simulator with up to 51 qubits in Innsbruck, Austria.

Quantum systems are expected to become capable of solving increasingly complex problems that traditional computers struggle with or cannot handle at all. However, this progress creates a verification problem. When a simulation is small enough, scientists can compare its results with calculations performed on a conventional computer. As quantum systems grow beyond those limits, that direct comparison becomes impractical, making alternative ways to assess their reliability essential.

A New Way to Measure Quantum Computing Accuracy

The breakthrough comes from an international research team led by Tristan Kraft at the Technical University of Munich and Peter Zoller at the University of Innsbruck and the Institute for Quantum Optics and Quantum Information of the Austrian Academy of Sciences, working alongside Barbara Kraus of the Technical University of Munich. The researchers developed a method for experimentally determining how a quantum simulator operates and translating uncertainties in its behavior into measurable limits on the accuracy of its results.

Quantum simulators are specialized physical systems designed to reproduce the behavior of other quantum systems. They are especially useful for investigating situations involving large numbers of interacting particles, where the calculations required can quickly overwhelm even powerful conventional computers.

But quantum experiments are never entirely free from errors. "But no real experiment is perfect," says Tristan Kraft. "Interactions may turn out differently than expected, the system is influenced by its environment, and measurements are also subject to uncertainties."

Rather than assuming a quantum simulator behaves exactly as intended, the new approach examines experimental data to determine what is actually happening inside the system. This allows researchers to identify important interactions and account for disturbances such as fluctuations and noise.

"From this data, we determine the relevant interactions as well as key influences from fluctuations and noise. We then calculate how the uncertainties in this model affect the simulation results," explains Tristan Kraft. "The quantum simulator thus provides not just a single value, but a result with error margins that quantify its accuracy."

Successfully Tested on a 51 Qubit Quantum Simulator

To evaluate the method, the researchers began with a relatively small quantum system containing ten trapped ions. Because conventional computers can still calculate the behavior of a system of this size, the team could test its reconstructed models and calculated error limits against independent measurements.

After this initial validation, the researchers expanded their experiments to a chain of 51 trapped ions. The larger demonstration, carried out by a team led by Manoj Joshi and Christian Roos using an ion trap quantum simulator, showed that the technique could also work with substantially more complex quantum systems.

This represents an important step toward establishing how trustworthy quantum simulation results are, particularly as quantum devices advance into territory where direct verification with traditional computing methods becomes increasingly difficult.

Extending the Breakthrough to More Complex Quantum Systems

The next challenge is to bring the technique to two-dimensional quantum simulators. These systems can model more complicated interactions among particles, making them valuable for exploring quantum physics. At the same time, their growing complexity makes their results particularly difficult to confirm using conventional calculations.

"This is particularly important because classical calculations for such systems become significantly more difficult as the number of particles increases," explains quantum computing pioneer Peter Zoller. "This also makes independent verification of the results increasingly complex, making the question of experimentally determined error limits all the more important."

The researchers are now adapting their method for the newest generation of two-dimensional quantum simulators, which offer improved precision and can investigate systems containing larger numbers of particles.

Looking further ahead, the approach could help scientists establish a more meaningful way to measure quantum advantage, the ability of quantum systems to outperform conventional computers. Instead of comparing machines based only on their processing speed or the size of the problems they can handle, researchers could also examine how accurately each system produces its answers.

"After all, when a classical computer and a quantum simulator tackle the same problem, it's not just a matter of which one delivers a result faster. What's also crucial is which one can solve the problem with a smaller, verifiable margin of error," says Peter Zoller.

This shift could change how progress in quantum computing is evaluated. Future breakthroughs may be judged not simply by how many particles a simulator can model or how quickly it performs calculations, but by its ability to solve increasingly difficult problems with demonstrably reliable results.

The findings were published in Physical Review X. The research received support from the Austrian Science Fund (FWF), the German Ministry of Research, Technology and Space, the European Union, BMW, and other funding organizations.

Materials provided by University of Innsbruck. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10520. After Anthropic’s record-breaking debut on Wall Street, nonprofits prepare for a wave of AI philanthropy

It was philanthropy, the story goes, that first bonded two of the founders behind artificial intelligence giant Anthropic.

Jared Kaplan, the San Francisco company’s chief science officer, and CEO Dario Amodei were doctoral students carpooling to an event organized by an academic fellowship. As Kaplan recalls, he and Amodei spent the ride discussing “this idea of thoroughly investigating what kinds of charitable causes could do the most good.”

“We had so much largesse to, sort of, disperse,” Kaplan joked at a Hertz Foundation discussion in April 2025.

Those once-hypothetical philanthropic ambitions gained new steam as Anthropic debuted on the stock market to a record $1.5 trillion valuation. The Claude AI tool maker already plans to dedicate 5% of its shares for a new Anthropic Public Benefit Fund. Its seven co-founders say they’ll give away 80% of their equity — a $112 billion commitment based on its IPO price.

Now suddenly affluent employees, many of whom will become millionaires as they sell their vested stock, may wield enough capital to make transformative donations themselves — riches that are commanding the attention of nonprofits looking to direct some sliver toward causes from global health to animal welfare.

Silicon Valley has great expectations. The napkin math suggests leading U.S. AI firms’ valuations could add at least $37 billion annually in intended philanthropy, according to a widely circulated May blog post by Nan Ransohoff, head of public goods at the payments company Stripe.

“This is a once-in-a-lifetime kind of opportunity for people to really make an impact on extreme poverty,” said Peter Singer, the moral philosopher whose writings influence many tech donors.

Nonprofits aren’t counting on an immediate charitable windfall, though. Many Anthropic employees cannot sell or gift the bulk of their shares until next spring. Questions persist about the charitable priorities of these potential philanthropists whose worldviews have been shaped by the increasingly powerful AI models advancing at breakneck speed. The sector’s volatility — between high borrowing costs, recent turmoil at the chipmaker Nvidia and warnings about risks to humanity — leaves skeptics doubtful about the amounts actually available to redistribute.

“The challenge is that deploying philanthropic capital is hard,” said Renaissance Philanthropy president Kumar Garg, whose nonprofit matches funders with scientific initiatives. “That’s not an automatic step.”

AI philanthropists are expected to make large donations quickly

Will the artificial intelligence wave of wealth creation differ from previous ones that created new philanthropy?

There’s reason to believe so, according to Benjamin Soskis, a historian of philanthropy and senior research associate at the Urban Institute’s Center on Nonprofits and Philanthropy, even if he finds the number of tech executives who have “actually managed to empty the safe” is low.

The modern philanthropic foundation stemmed from the post-Civil War wave of industrial capitalism. Titans such as oil magnate John D. Rockefeller Sr. and steel tycoon Andrew Carnegie established bureaucratic institutions intended to address the root causes of social problems. Those were followed by the turn-of-the-century technology wealth that brought a more data-centric, risk-taking approach. The Gates Foundation launched to address pressing crises in global health and poverty.

AI wealth, according to Soskis, carries a sense of “extreme peril” and “really transformative promise” that should spur even more urgent action from this emerging class of donors.

At least one of Anthropic’s co-founders will donate in the coming weeks to the Trump Accounts children’s savings program, according to comments made Friday by U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent on CNBC.

Anthropic’s explosive growth is also fueling some anticipation that its 5,300 employees will want to rapidly scale their charitable impact, too. A generous internal giving program adds another incentive. Employee equity gifts are matched by the company with shares of Class N common stock. Anthropic also partners with donor-advised fund vendors such as Daffy, which offer a tax-efficient tool for grantmaking without the hassle of a more sophisticated charitable foundation.

Daffy CEO Adam Nash expects this younger generation to give more quickly.

“The people working in AI see the impact AI is having already,” Nash said. “And they are giving now, right?”

Tech-adjacent nonprofits prepare for influx

One organization in particular appears poised to move those rising donation volumes.

Coefficient Giving was formed by San Francisco-based billionaire Cari Tuna and her husband, Dustin Moskovitz, a Facebook co-founder and early Anthropic investor. A spokesperson told The New York Times the couple transferred their Anthropic stake into an undisclosed nonprofit last year to avoid personally profiting.

The philanthropic funder — which has directed more than $8 billion since 2014 in support of global health, animal rights, AI safety and the effective altruism community — has been scaling its pledges. In July, Coefficient Giving increased its commitment to the grantmaker GiveWell to $1 billion.

The move reflected expectations of “considerably more future funding for this kind of work,” according to the announcement.

“We might grow a lot, in part because we want to spend faster in response to these trends,” Coefficient Giving CEO Alexander Berger said during a late July public discussion. “But there is a great deal of room for other organizations and approaches, and I do not expect us to play an overwhelmingly dominant role.”

GiveDirectly has spent months identifying the biggest barriers to rapidly growing its antipoverty work, according to Nick Allardice, its president and CEO. The tech-focused nonprofit sends cash straight to poor households with no strings attached.

Recipients must have mobile phones to receive the digital transfers. GiveDirectly offers phones for about $15, usually subtracted from recipients’ first payment. So the nonprofit secured investments this year to build out a supply chain to support many more mobile phones going forward.

The group is betting that its preparations for a charitable boom will be worthwhile.

“I have this level of cautious optimism,” Allardice said. “But I can’t foresee the future, and I don’t know exactly what’s going to happen.”

Wild Animal Initiative executive director Cameron Meyer Shorb shares those tempered expectations.

His nonprofit funds wild animal welfare research. It’s the sort of work some observers predict will see the biggest upswings in support: nonprofits connected to effective altruism, a data-driven movement that prioritizes donations to causes with the greatest impact on the most living beings.

The movement’s reputation took a hit when Sam Bankman-Fried, one of its leading proponents and financial backers, got a 25-year prison sentence for cryptocurrency fraud. But the ideas are rebounding in the same tech circles that could see big payouts as more AI companies go public.

Effective altruists have learned not to rely too heavily on any one funder, according to Shorb. He expects “quite a bit” of fresh support for animal advocacy and sees an outsize role for midsize donors.

He said he has connected with a couple of Anthropic employees. Perhaps more important, he said, are relationships with the philanthropic advisers who help plan donors’ giving.

“As you can imagine, it’s a pretty tricky place for a soon-to-be billionaire to be to have all this potential power coming soon,” Shorb said. “As soon as anyone finds out about that, anyone who can possibly get their email or phone number will just be knocking down their door. So we’ve tried to be gentle and slow and respectful in our approach.”

Associated Press coverage of philanthropy and nonprofits receives support through the AP’s collaboration with The Conversation US, with funding from Lilly Endowment Inc. The AP is solely responsible for this content. For all of AP’s philanthropy coverage, visit https://apnews.com/hub/philanthropy.

—James Pollard, Associated Press

#10518. The free market isn’t so free. Just look at Jensen Huang

There’s plenty to scratch your head about in the speedy and surprising return of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO, just 11 days after he resigned following revelations of an affair with a woman who may have helped make him a target of Chinese intelligence. Never mind the fact that two other allegations of improper relationships with Nvidia employees have emerged, alongside a slew of claims about his reportedly toxic management style. Or that he’s still, technically, under investigation, according to Nvidia’s statement announcing his return. Or that six days earlier, the board told investors it “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

Perhaps most head-scratching of all is the White House’s claim to reporters that “President Trump was totally involved” in Huang’s return to the company, and reports that he dialed into a board meeting to argue for the return of the executive whose resignation the board had accepted less than two weeks earlier. Trump has since claimed in a call to CNBC following the announcement that Huang was “exonerated”—before adding, almost incongruously, that “he’s in the business of selling chips.” (That leap between one thought—the basis for which remains unclear, given the unfinished independent investigation—and another tells its own story.)

Senator Elizabeth Warren has called the decision “reckless.” There’s another word for it that might come to mind. But the most diplomatic way to say it is that it’s the end of a free market in the United States.

Leaders of countries can have their own opinions about who should run a major company operating in their country. But they shouldn’t be influencing boardroom discussions about the running of that company. Nvidia’s own announcement acknowledges that directors received “input from senior levels” of the U.S. government before concluding that Huang’s reinstatement posed no national security risk.

The massive losses in the stock market, with Nvidia a lynchpin of the AI boom, won’t have gone unnoticed at 1600 Pennsylvania Avenue. And for a man who professes to be the master of “the art of the deal,” a deal that boosted Nvidia’s market value by roughly $1 trillion in a matter of hours sounds like a pretty sweet deal.

But rising share prices don’t answer questions about Huang’s conduct, or his paramour’s potential ties to Chinese intelligence. Nor do they explain why a supposedly independent board tasked with holding executives accountable appears to have allowed a world leader to influence its deliberations and ultimately sway its decision. Trump, after all, explicitly said he “convinced them” to reinstate Huang.

And what about the Nvidia employees on the receiving end of Huang’s reported tirades? According to The New York Times, he has threatened to rip off employees’ arms and beat them to death with them, tear the tops of their heads off and “pee on your brain,” and told one employee that his mother should have aborted him. To be sure, many Nvidia employees have expressed support for Huang. But at least when Sam Altman was deposed as CEO by his board at OpenAI nearly three years ago to the day, the groundswell of opinion in favor of reinstating him represented a near-unanimous employee revolt.

The truth is that Jensen Huang may ultimately be cleared of the most serious allegations being made against him—that’s literally what investigations are for. But the independent investigation hasn’t concluded. And some of the most powerful people in America seem to have arrived at the verdict first, and decided the process can catch up later. That’s not a free market. But it is a big, beautiful trade.

#10516. Does culture change people’s personalities?

Personality is often misunderstood. For instance, people tend to think they are way more unpredictable and complex than they are according to others, to the point of mistakenly assuming that their “personality changes from one situation to the next”. In fact, everybody has a preferred pattern of reactions to different situations, and if you hang out with people long enough you will have no trouble predicting how they are likely to act in any given situation, or indeed understanding what makes them tick.

And yet, even if humans are predictable, that doesn’t mean that they are rigid or unable to flex according to the demands of the situation. One of the earliest findings in psychology is that behavior is always a function of both the person and the situation, which means that in order to predict how people will act you must take into account not just who they are but also what the situational requirements are.

Micro vs. Macro

At the micro-level, this explains why personality may be “suppressed” or inhibited in strong situations, that is contexts where there is a great deal of pressure to behave in a specific way or form: from a church (quiet, respectful, pensative) to a nightclub (loud, extraverted, energetic), to a lecture (focused, pensative, attentive), to a soccer game (depending on where you are, anywhere from excited and energized to aggressive and stressed). That is not to say that people stop being who they are when they are required to inhibit their personality; but rather, that personality becomes a weaker predictor of behavior when the strength of the situation temporarily eclipses it.

At the macro-level, this begs the question of how much our personality may change when we make big long-term changes in our chosen environments or contexts. Most notably, people changing jobs or employers will often switch from one organizational culture to another; and people switching cities or countries will also be faced with different cultural demands to adapt or adjust their behaviors. Let me use myself as an example: moving from Argentina to Britain first, then from Britain to the US, required shaping my main adaptations to meet the cultural demands of each environment: I first learned to become less excitable, emotional, and direct (all of which work far less in Britain than Argentina), and then learned to suppress the sarcasm, nuances and indirectness in my communication (as these are less effective in the US than Britain).

But, what does the research show about how culture, either at the societal or organizational level, impacts people’s behavior? And can this impact be significant or consequential enough to translate into actual personality changes?

Adaptations

Let’s start with the second question, which is actually simpler. Since personality can mostly be understood as “what you typically or habitually do,” enduring changes to how you behave—new adaptations—will indeed be perceived as personality changes. So, for instance, after living in NYC for over a decade the fast-paced nature of the city can make you more impatient, to a point that may not make you stand out in NYC (where type-A impatience is the norm) but will certainly stand out if you transfer that impactience to slower tempo cultures. As the famous line suggests, “The problem with city people in the countryside is that they expect even nature to meet deadlines.” Note, however, that city people often come from the countryside in the first place, but unless they adapt to their new fast environments and adopt the appropriate speed, they will not last very long.

As for the first question, there’s plenty of research evidence—especially recent scientific papers—showing that while the essence of personality may remain largely unchanged even after people switch cultures, there are still logical changes to their personality at the more granular level, which correspond to the adaptational requirements of their adoptive cultures. In a way, we could say that our personality evolves when we go outside our comfort zone and challenge ourselves with new contexts that require new adaptations and broadening the behavioral repertoire in our tool kit.

For example, a recent longitudinal study comparing 300 Japanese students studying in the United States with 108 students who remained in Japan found that international experiences were associated with changes in personality-related behaviors, particularly greater openness to new experiences and more socially adaptive behavior. Interestingly, the researchers also found that students who chose to study abroad already differed from those who stayed home, suggesting that personality not only influences how we adapt to new environments, but also which environments we choose to enter in the first place.

Likewise, a recent meta-analysis examining 25 studies, comprising 40 samples and over 5,700 participants, found that living and studying abroad was associated with measurable personality changes, including increased openness to experience, greater self-confidence and agency, and lower levels of neuroticism (that is, greater emotional stability). Although the effects were generally small, the findings suggest that personality is not simply something we carry with us wherever we go, but also something that is shaped by the environments and cultures we inhabit.

In another longitudinal study tracking 180 British university students, some of whom spent a year abroad across 32 different destinations, those living overseas became somewhat more agreeable, curious, and less anxious than their peers who remained at home. Interestingly, although the overall personality changes were relatively small, the researchers found that people’s everyday behavioral tendencies predicted longer-term personality change, suggesting that adapting how we behave in unfamiliar environments may eventually influence who we become.

Perhaps even more intriguingly, a series of four studies examining Spanish-English bilinguals found that people’s reported personality characteristics varied depending on the language they were using. Specifically, participants tended to report higher levels of extraversion, agreeableness, and conscientiousness when responding in English rather than Spanish, consistent with the cultural norms associated with each language. This suggests that our personality is not always expressed in the same way across situations, and that simply switching languages may activate different aspects of our identity, even without changing countries.

Workplace influence

Even career or employment switches can shape people’s personality, as a growing body of research suggests that our jobs are not just an expression of who we are, but can also influence who we become. For example, a longitudinal study tracking Australian workers over five years found that moving into more senior managerial and professional roles was associated with increases in openness to experience, while another study examining nearly 7,700 German workers found that occupational demands were associated with subsequent personality development, particularly among those who remained in their jobs long enough to be shaped by them. Likewise, a longitudinal study following people from age 18 to 26 found that work experiences and personality influenced each other, such that people tended to select jobs compatible with their existing traits, but those same jobs subsequently reinforced or modified their personalities. In other words, we may choose our careers based on our personalities, but our careers can also end up choosing the personalities we develop.

Of all these patterns of change, the most salient is the positive impact that environmental and cultural switches have on openness to experience, the academic term for intellectual and experiential curiosity. Interestingly, the effect is a form of “snowballing” whereby cultures operate in a way that accentuate who people are in the first place. In other words, our choices are a function of our inherent or a priori preferences and dispositions, and they contribute to augmenting them. This is why when people change, the most common outcome is that they become a more exaggerated version of their earlier selves.

Psychologists have long documented the phenomenon of niche-picking, whereby people gravitate towards environments, relationships, and experiences that are compatible with their existing personalities. Importantly, this is not just a matter of selecting environments that suit us, but also being shaped by them, as illustrated by a longitudinal study showing that the personality traits that predispose people to certain work experiences tend to be reinforced by those same experiences over time. In other words, curious people may seek intellectually stimulating environments that make them even more curious, while competitive individuals may gravitate towards demanding, high-pressure cultures that further amplify their competitiveness. Thus, we don’t just choose environments that reflect who we are; we also become more like the environments we choose. Paradoxically, then, the more we change, the more we become like ourselves.

#10514. Women’s AI leadership is bigger than the data shows

Every study on women in AI seems to say the same thing. Women are disproportionately impacted yet are hugely underrepresented in AI roles, not making the decisions that have such an impact on the world. The latest comes from LinkedIn, whose recent research found that women accounted for just 26% of U.S. AI hires in 2025, against 50% of hires into non-AI occupations. Across 27 countries, women hold only 13% of C-suite AI leadership roles at AI companies. Thus, they make up a ridiculously small percentage of the best-paid jobs in the economy. 

That’s very worrying. The people designing these tools and running these companies decide what the technology is for, whose tasks get automated, and how the value is captured. If women are left out at every stage, the products and the profits will reflect it. The gender gap will get bigger because of that. 

But after reading some of these studies, I’ve started to wonder if they don’t also have a few blind spots of their own. In denouncing women’s absence from AI, we may fall into the old habit of downplaying what women actually already contribute because it happens in places we don’t count and fail to see.

I interviewed Sarah Steinberg, who leads global public policy partnerships at LinkedIn, at the Reykjavík Global Forum this week. Steinberg told me LinkedIn’s research on AI leadership shows women are more likely to hold jobs exposed to AI and less likely to have AI skills. 

But do the numbers fully capture women’s AI contribution? She agreed we may be underestimating women in AI leadership. And we had just been in a session in which many women leaders from across the globe had talked about AI from another angle: regulating it, contesting it, limiting its costs for communities and the environment. These women show that many are involved in initiatives shaping the future of AI.

A narrow definition of AI work

The first blind spot is in the definition. LinkedIn counts someone as “AI talent” if they hold an AI-classified occupation or list two or more AI engineering skills. There may be a will to also count “implied skills”, i.e. for people in occupations that assume AI expertise even if they haven’t tagged it on their profile, but by and large the lens remains mainly technical. Women’s share of C-suite roles held by AI talent at AI companies is said to be 13%. But it leaves out government and civil society as well as leaders within the AI industry who don’t hold technical titles. 

Skills raise the same question. LinkedIn separates AI literacy, meaning the use of tools like ChatGPT or GitHub Copilot, from the engineering skills needed to build the technology. Asked where AI governance or policy expertise fits, Steinberg said she didn’t know whether it is counted at all, “because it’s not necessarily a technical engineering skill or a tool that you would be using.”

We have seen this before. The previous generation of tech jobs was measured with coding as the yardstick, and women who ran product, policy, or trust and safety teams barely showed up in the count.

Decisions made from the outside

If leadership means making decisions that change the course of AI, many of those decisions are now made outside AI companies. On Nov. 3, voters in 10 U.S. states weighed in on 41 local ballot measures related to data centers, nearly all of which aimed at limiting construction. Voters approved nearly all of them.

And women are prominent in these fights. A survey found that 55% of women strongly oppose data centers, compared with 43% of men. In Prince George’s County, Maryland, Taylor Frazier McCollum’s petition gathered more than 25,000 signatures and led to moratoriums that blocked a $5 billion project. In New York, State Senator Kristen Gonzalez sponsored a bill imposing a one-year moratorium on permits for data centers drawing more than 20 megawatts, which the legislature passed in June.

The revolt is likely to change the industry. It is “forcing a lot of AI leaders to have to reflect on what they have been doing that is clearly alienating the public,” Steinberg said. When companies arrive in towns and ask local politicians to sign nondisclosure agreements, on the assumption that residents would welcome a transformative technology, “what they’re finding is that the benefits that people are experiencing from this technology are not outweighing the negatives that having a data center in their community would present,” she added. Data centers do create some jobs, she added, but “whether that is at a level that is worth it relative to the downsides is up for communities to decide.”

Europe tells a similar story. Margrethe Vestager coordinated the EU’s work on the AI Act, and Lucilla Sioli, who led its drafting at the European Commission, now heads the EU AI Office. In 2024, Fortune reported that women headed more than 50 % of the 30 authorities enforcing EU data rules.

It’s true that some of the best-known critics of the industry, like Timnit Gebru, did not choose to stand outside the AI industry: they were pushed out. Women end up regulating and contesting AI because public-sector, legal, and community careers are more inclusive, while the best-paid technical jobs remain largely in the hands of tech bros who perpetuate a culture that actively filters women out.

The women we miss

Another blind spot is usage: women may use AI more than they admit. In an experiment reported in Harvard Business Review, 1,026 engineers reviewed the same piece of Python code. When told the author had used AI, they judged that person 9% less competent, even though they rated the code itself no differently. Women paid a heavier price: a 13% drop in perceived competence, compared with 6% for men. Because of this “hidden penalty of using AI”, any measure of AI skills based on self-reporting is likely to miss all the women who choose not to mention their use and their skills. 

Steinberg also pointed to the skills employers now want. Demand for AI literacy grew 70% year over year on LinkedIn, but the other most sought-after skills are interpersonal: communication, negotiation, conflict management. She is bullish on women’s strengths there, but she warned that “we haven’t really figured out the code” to train, evaluate, and promote people on those skills. Until we do, that contribution will remain mostly invisible too.

The case for counting differently

None of this is an argument for complacency. Women should be building AI tools and running AI companies, where decisions about capital and products are made. A 13% share of C-suite AI leadership roles is a disaster. And LinkedIn’s data can help better fight this fight. Because it tracks how people move from job to job, it shows where women drop off, and the answer varies by industry. In sectors where women are well represented, the biggest drop comes between vice president and the C-suite. Where they are scarce, it comes at the first manager level. In STEM, it happens in the first year after graduation, between the degree and the job. 

But a picture limited to engineers at AI firms makes women look absent and passive, even though many are shaping AI through ballots, legislation, regulation, and everyday use. If we could map the gap across industry, government, and civil society, we would have a better baseline, and it would probably show that women already have more say over AI than all the studies suggest.

→ Baca artikel asli

#10513. Logging AI Prompts and Responses in Java Without Leaking Sensitive Data

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Picture this. A customer writes to your support chatbot: "Hi, I'm Asha, my card ending 4111 was charged twice, call me on 98400 12345." Your Java service wraps that message in a prompt, sends it to a model, and logs the whole thing so you can debug it later. Congratulations, you now have a customer's name, phone number and card details sitting in a plain text log file that half the engineering team can open.

This happens more often than people admit. Logging AI calls is genuinely useful, but prompts and responses are some of the most personal text your application ever touches. Here's how to keep the usefulness and drop the risk.

A regular web request is easy to log: URL, status code, time taken. AI calls are messier.

The output changes from run to run. If a user says "your bot gave me a wrong answer yesterday", the only proof of what happened is what you sent and what came back at that moment. You can't just replay it and expect the same result.

Prompts are also built, not typed. The final text is usually a template, a few retrieved document chunks, some chat history and the user's message stitched together. When something looks off, the bug could be in any of those pieces, and only the assembled prompt shows you which one.

Then there's cost. You pay by the token, so without per-call records you can't say which feature is burning money. And quality drifts over time as models get upgraded and templates get edited. Old logs are your only baseline.

So yes, you need these logs. The real question is what goes into them.

Before writing any masking code, agree on what counts as sensitive. In most business apps that means:

Names, emails, phone numbers and home addresses

Card numbers, bank details and government ID numbers

Health, legal or job-related details people mention in free text

Internal material like contract wording, pricing rules or source code

The first three groups follow patterns, so they're easy to catch. The last two don't, and that's where most teams get caught out. Keep that in mind, because it shapes the design below.

The safest sensitive value is the one that never reaches the log. So split what you record into two buckets.

The first is metadata: a request ID, a hashed user or tenant ID, model name, prompt template name and version, token counts, latency, retry count, error type and estimated cost. None of this is personal, and it answers most day-to-day questions.

The second is content, meaning the actual prompt and response text. You only need this when you're investigating quality, and even then you rarely need all of it. A sensible default is to log metadata for every call and masked content for a small sample, maybe five percent, plus every failed call.

Here's a mistake that's easy to make: letting the log appender clean things up afterwards. By then the raw text may already be in a buffer, an async queue or a crash dump. Mask first, then log.

The cleanest way is one small component that every AI log call has to pass through:

public interface PiiMasker {     String mask(String input); }

Nothing else is allowed to write raw prompts. One chokepoint is much easier to review and test than masking sprinkled across fifty classes.

For structured data like emails, cards and tokens, regular expressions do the job well:

public class RegexPiiMasker implements PiiMasker {     private static final Map RULES = new LinkedHashMap<>();     static {         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("[A-Za-z0-9.%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}"), "[EMAIL]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\d[ -]?){13,19}\b"), "[CARD]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("\b(?:\+?\d{1,3}[ -]?)?\d{10}\b"), "[PHONE]");         RULES.put(Pattern.compile("(?i)bearer\s+[A-Za-z0-9.~+/=-]+"), "Bearer [TOKEN]");     }     @Override     public String mask(String input) {         if (input == null || input.isBlank()) return input;         String result = input;         for (var rule : RULES.entrySet()) {             result = rule.getKey().matcher(result).replaceAll(rule.getValue());         }         return result;     } }

A few small choices matter here. The patterns are compiled once, not on every call. The order is fixed, because a card number can otherwise get half eaten by the phone rule. And the replacement labels such as [EMAIL] keep the sentence readable, so whoever debugs it later can still follow what the user was asking.

Regex won't catch a name like "Asha Raman" or an address buried mid-sentence. For those you have a few options, and they work best together.

You can run a named-entity recognition model that tags people, places and organisations, then mask anything above a confidence threshold. You can do exact replacement of values you already know, such as the customer's name from the current session. Or, best of all, you can mask at the field level while building the prompt. If your code knows that customer.getEmail() is going into the template, swap in a placeholder right there instead of trying to spot it afterwards. Knowing beats guessing every time.

Plain redaction can remove too much. If the same email shows up five times in a conversation, five identical [EMAIL] tags hide the fact that it was one person.

The fix is consistent pseudonyms. Turn each real value into a short token using a keyed hash, so the same email always becomes something like EMAIL_7f3a. You can still follow the thread, but nobody can read the original from the log. Use an HMAC with a secret from your secrets manager. A plain unkeyed hash of a phone number can be brute-forced in minutes, because there just aren't that many phone numbers.

One warning: if you also store a lookup table to reverse the tokens, that table is now sensitive data too. Most teams are better off with one-way tokens.

Free-text log lines are hard to search and easy to leak through. Write one JSON record per AI call with clearly separate fields:

log.info("ai_call",     kv("requestId", requestId),     kv("model", model),     kv("promptVersion", "support-reply-v4"),     kv("inputTokens", usage.inputTokens()),     kv("outputTokens", usage.outputTokens()),     kv("latencyMs", elapsed),     kv("promptMasked", masker.mask(prompt)),     kv("responseMasked", masker.mask(response)));

Now dashboards for cost and latency never need to touch the text fields at all. You can also keep the two kinds of data for different lengths of time: metadata for a year, masked content for a couple of weeks. Put the request ID into your logging framework's MDC so every line belonging to one call, including retries and tool calls, carries the same ID.

Streaming trips people up. Tokens arrive in tiny pieces, and a card number can be split across two chunks, which means masking each chunk on its own will miss it. Collect the full response in a buffer, mask it once the stream ends, and log that. If the stream dies halfway, run the partial buffer through the same masker.

Tool calls are the other trap. When the model calls one of your Java methods, the arguments and return values are often the most sensitive thing in the whole exchange: order details, account balances, addresses. Treat them as part of the prompt and response. Log the tool name, duration and outcome by default, and log arguments only after masking.

Masking lowers the risk but never removes it, so surround it with some ordinary discipline:

Give content fields a short retention period. Old prompt archives are liabilities.

Limit who can read AI logs. Debugging a model doesn't mean someone needs to read customer messages.

Encrypt log storage and the pipeline that feeds it.

Log the hashed user ID so you can find and delete one person's records when they ask.

Allow verbose logging in development with fake data, and enforce strict settings in production through configuration, not through people remembering.

A masker that quietly stops working is worse than having none, because everyone assumes the logs are clean. Write tests for it:

@Test void masksEmailAndCardNumber() {     String raw = "Contact me at asha@example.com, card 4111 1111 1111 1111";     String safe = new RegexPiiMasker().mask(raw);     assertFalse(safe.contains("asha@example.com"));     assertFalse(safe.contains("4111"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[EMAIL]"));     assertTrue(safe.contains("[CARD]")); }

Build a small set of messy, realistic samples: numbers with odd spacing, mixed languages, addresses inside sentences, text pasted from spreadsheets. Run them in your CI pipeline, and add a new case every time a leak turns up. A scheduled job that scans stored logs for patterns that should never appear makes a good safety net too.

A few I'd watch for. Logging raw text at debug level and assuming production never enables it; debug flags get switched on during incidents, so mask at every level. Masking only the user's message while forgetting retrieved documents, system prompts and tool results. Letting SDK exceptions carry the full request body into the logs. Leaving wire-level HTTP logging on in production, which bypasses your masker completely. And treating masking as finished once it ships, when every new prompt template can bring new kinds of data.

Every AI call writes a structured metadata record with a request ID

All prompt and response text goes through a single masker first

Streams are masked after assembly, and tool arguments are masked too

Content logging is sampled, short-lived and access-controlled

Tests cover the masker, with a regression case for every past leak

Client libraries aren't dumping raw payloads into the logs

Good logs turn an AI feature from a black box into something you can actually debug, price and improve. The same logs can also expose the people who trusted you with their details. The way through is fairly plain: record metadata for every call, push all content through one well-tested masker, use pseudonyms when you need to follow a conversation, handle streams and tools carefully, and keep retention short. Set that up before launch, not after the first incident, and you get visibility without regret.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10511. How to keep AI from turning your company into the Borg

Like many who grew up in the late 1980s, I was a big fan of Star Trek: The Next Generation. While I enjoyed the stories, what really stuck with me in an enduring way was the ethos of the show, and especially what it had to say about how to lead when long-term principles and immediate goals pull in different directions. The death last month of Sir Patrick Stewart—the actor behind the inimitable Captain Picard—sent me back to watch some old episodes of the show. It is perhaps unsurprising that the episodes that stood out to me were those in which the captain and his crew confronted the Borg, a collective of formerly independent life forms who had become fused together into a single hive mind.

In the show’s lore, the Borg grew by absorbing other species and their technology. The hive’s drones carried the knowledge of every civilization the collective had absorbed but lacked anything in the way of individuality: they had no name, no will, and no ideas of their own. The first time I watched the episodes, I saw them as a thought-provoking and dystopian fantasy. Today, they strike me as delivering an essential warning about the dangers of AI.

The Borg chased perfection through uniformity. AI seems to be sending humans along a similar, worrying trajectory.

The homogenizing effect of AI

Language models are trained to predict the most likely next word in a sequence, then tuned to give the kinds of answers people rate highly. The result is output that is fluent, competent, and increasingly alike. And when millions of people and thousands of companies lean on the same few models, that sameness spreads.

In a 2025 study named after the Borg’s defining image of a hivemind, researchers tested more than 70 language models on real-world, open-ended questions. They found striking similarities both within models and between models—individual models repeated themselves, while groups of models collectively converged on the same responses. For example, when 25 models (from the US, China, and elsewhere) were asked 50 times to provide a metaphor about time, most of the responses were variants of time being either a river or a weaver. By contrast, when a reporter at the MIT Technology Review asked six of their (human) colleagues the same thing, they got six different answers with nary a mention of rivers or weavers.

This commonality reaches beyond the texts that LLMs output themselves. In September this year, Nature rounded up a fast-growing body of research suggesting that generative AI is homogenizing how humans write, and possibly how we think. In one study it cited, students who used ChatGPT on creative tasks for just five days were still giving more similar answers months later, even when they didn’t use AI. Business is showing early signs of the same phenomenon. A recent study of earnings-call transcripts found that companies’ prepared remarks have grown strikingly similar as AI writing tools have spread, and that more uniform language was associated with more negative initial market reactions.

The irony is striking. Each company is betting that AI will make it sharper. But collectively AI seems to be making business duller and more standardized. The leaders who win in this new world will be those who find ways to capture the benefits AI can offer to individuals without falling into the trap of becoming part of the collective. Picard’s encounters with the Borg offer three lessons in how to do that.

Don’t become a mouthpiece

In “The Best of Both Worlds,” the Borg capture Picard and turn him into a drone they call Locutus, their spokesman. Picard’s face and voice announce decisions that are no longer his, but that come instead from the collective hivemind.

Leaders may already be drifting in this direction. In IBM’s 2026 CEO study of 2,000 CEOs, 64% said they are “comfortable making major strategic decisions based on AI-generated input.” Further, they expect AI to make 48% of operational decisions without human intervention by 2030, up from 25% today.

Unchecked delegation to AI can end up being very expensive. In October 2025, Deloitte Australia agreed to partially refund the roughly $290,000 it was paid for a government report that contained apparent AI-generated errors such as imaginary research and made-up quotes from federal courts.

More insiduously, the skills that let leaders catch such errors can fade quickly. In a randomized trial published by Anthropic in January, developers who learned a new programming tool with AI assistance scored 17% lower on a quiz about the tool than those who had acquired their knowledge by themselves. Deloitte itself is a case in point. Last month, a year after that first refund, the government of Western Australia demanded an expedited refund from Deloitte for a study on merging three of the state’s universities. The firm had disclosed using generative AI to help gather research, summarize information, and proofread content, but the report nevertheless turned out to contain errors such as a simple miscalculation that reduced cost estimates by 260 million AUD, references to sources that did not exist, and using a name for one of the universities that had been “changed under an act of state parliament in 2017.”

The takeaway: Delegate the analysis, keep the judgment. Anyone who signs off on an AI recommendation should be able to explain why it is right—and this should extend to personal ownership of all the constituent details.

Protect the people who disagree

The Borg instantiated the promise of a certain type of harmony: no drone ever argued with the collective for the simple reason that there was nothing to argue about. All drones thought the same thoughts and held exactly the same beliefs. This made the Borg both formidably efficient and dangerously blind.

AI can build a version of that consensus into an organization. A recent Stanford-led study found that 11 leading AI systems affirmed users’ actions 49% more frequently than did other humans, even when those actions involved deception or harm. A single affirming response made people more convinced they were right and less willing to take responsibility for their decision. Worse, participants rated flattering and non-flattering responses as about equally objective. “The very feature that causes harm also drives engagement,” the authors wrote. An organization in which every manager has a tireless assistant telling them they’re right is an organization slowly losing its capacity to disagree.

On the show, the Borg’s uniformity is eventually broken by a single drone. Rescued by Picard’s crew, a drone they name Hugh develops a sense of self, and after he is sent home, this threat to the collective spreads to other drones.Yet left without support, the newly independent drones quickly fall under the control of a manipulative new leader. Independent thinking is both contagious and fragile.

The takeaway: If your AI tends to agree with you, you will need to turn to other people for disagreement. Treat dissent as infrastructure. Reward the person who pushes back, and back them when they are right. Individuality without support is easily undermined.

Rebuilding takes more than retraining

The crew rescues Picard from the Borg quickly but it takes him much longer to become himself again. After being rescued, he goes home to his family’s vineyard in France, unsure who he is and tempted to quit Starfleet. No debriefing or new assignment puts him back together. His brother does, in a quarrel that ends with Picard finally breaking down. It takes time, and it takes family.

When companies plan for what AI will do to their people, they tend to plan for skills: who needs retraining, and on which tools. Far less attention goes to the meaning and purpose that people find in work. A study published in March found that people who relied passively on AI to do their work found less meaning in it, and that the loss appeared to carry over into the work they later did on their own. And in an October survey by Elon University, 70% of U.S. workers said paid work is extremely or very important to their sense of meaning and purpose, and 65% of Americans agreed that if AI makes work less meaningful, people will lose their sense of purpose and dignity.

As with Picard, much of the rebuilding happens through people. In the same survey, workers said that if AI substantially affects their jobs, they would place more faith in the people close to them than in formal organizations. Yet AI is quietly thinning those relationships at work. A recent piece in Harvard Business Review that surveyed just over 1,000 U.S. workers reported that AI was reducing communication within teams and thereby significantly reducing team trust and coordination. So the bonds are fraying just as people need them more than ever before.

The takeaway: Budget for rebuilding purpose alongside retraining. Give people time to adjust, and protect the relationships they will lean on while they do. Track whether people still find meaning in their work as carefully as you track whether they can use the tools.

Resistance is the point

The Borg’s catchphrase was “Resistance is futile.” Patrick Stewart’s Picard proved them wrong, mostly by being stubbornly, unmistakably himself. And in that lies a crucial lesson for business leaders.

AI is a remarkably useful tool. But it nudges everyone who uses it toward the same safe answer, and a company that stops noticing will end up thinking, sounding, and deciding like everyone else. Pushing back against that pull is now part of a leader’s job. Nobody else is going to do it.

#10509. ‘Sex sells, fellas’: President Trump defends Jensen Huang, says he pushed for him to be reinstated as Nvidia CEO

President Donald Trump says he played a role in pushing Nvidia to reinstate company president and CEO Jensen Huang.

On Monday morning, Nvidia announced that Huang would resume his position just 11 days after the tech mogul’s resignation. “I am honored to return to Nvidia and lead one of the most consequential technology companies in the world at the beginning of a new industrial revolution,” Huang said in a statement on the latest development. 

The decision comes as multiple investigations—including those from the Justice Department and the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission—are still ongoing. And just last week, Nvidia chairman Harvey ​Jones had said that the company would not rehire Huang, given that his conduct did not meet the company’s code of conduct. Huang had stepped down as CEO after allegedly having an extramarital affair with a suspected Chinese spy, as well as an additional affair with a subordinate.

But after Monday’s stunning decision to reinstate Huang, Trump seemingly took some credit for the move, telling the media that he advocated for the briefly jobless boss to come back into power. 

Trump told CNBC that Huang “did nothing wrong,” and said that he spoke to Nvidia’s board “directly and indirectly” ahead of the move, making clear that he argued on behalf of the CEO.

The president also made clear that he told the company that Huang returning to his role was the best decision in terms of national security, and for winning the AI race with adversaries like China. 

“They did ask for assurance on the national security threat,” Trump told the outlet. He added: “People say, ‘Is it a threat to have him?’ No, it’s a threat to lose him.”

When asked about Huang’s extramarital activities, Trump even went so far as to say the ordeal would help, rather than hurt, the CEO’s success. 

“I think he’s going to start off at an almost higher level,” the president said. “He’s apologized for the indiscretions, which were unfortunate. Probably, that’s what brought it to such a level, because sex sells, fellas.” With this remark, Trump seemed to imply that the nature of the news story being tied to affairs is what brought it so much attention.

The president seemed to double down on the sentiment on Truth Social by posting an image of Huang waving a Photoshopped red bra in the air, referencing a famous photo from the 2024 assassination attempt on Trump’s life in Pennsylvania.

Trump may have been the most influential voice, but he wasn’t the only powerful person standing behind Huang. 

An open letter to Nvidia’s board titled “Stand With Jensen” had gained more than 206,000 signatures as of Monday morning. One of them was from Elon Musk, who took to X to share that he had signed it on November 13. “A petition is underway to reinstate Jensen, and I’m signing it too,” the Tesla and SpaceX CEO wrote. 

More than 10,000 of the signers said they were current Nvidia employees, while more than 108,000 said they were shareholders. Still, while the White House and tech moguls may be behind the decision, Huang’s critics aren’t staying quiet. 

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer wrote on X that Trump is “putting his own financial interests and the needs of his billionaire buddies before our national security.” Some Republicans even came out against Huang’s reinstatement, including Sen. John Cornyn of Texas. In response to Trump’s denial that the affair posed any risks, the outgoing GOP senator posted on X: “No national security risk? Based on what?”

However, in the wake of his reinstatement, experts are now saying it was almost inevitable, given just how irreplaceable the CEO seemed to be. 

“There were definitely a lot of people who wanted him back. The extent that the administration intervened, the extent that shareholders intervened—it ​will be impossible to know,” said Steve Sosnick, chief strategist at Interactive Brokers, per Reuters.

Sosnick added: “But it tells us to some extent that Jensen Huang was too big to be fired.”

Nvidia’s stocks were up 30% in the hours after Monday’s major announcement.
