#10505. Will AI take your job? 3 principles for making yourself harder to replace

The Navier-Stokes equations, first formulated in the early 19th century, are widely used today to help design airplanes, create computer graphics, and even predict the weather. Yet for almost two centuries, a central question about them remained unsolved. We knew the equations worked in practice, but no one had proven whether they always hold up mathematically without breaking down.

That all changed in September when OpenAI claimed to have solved the Navier-Stokes problem, followed on October 6 by a torrent of proposed breakthroughs across nearly 400 math problems that had baffled researchers for generations. As Cornell University mathematician Steven Strogatz explained in an Atlantic essay, the announcements have left the field reeling. How should people think about their work when a machine can do it so much better?

Strogatz is no ordinary mathematician. A pioneer in the field of nonlinear systems and network science, he is also an award-winning author and a sometime New York Times columnist. His livelihood is not in danger. Yet OpenAI has him asking, “What’s the point?” It’s a question we’re all going to have to answer. Here are three principles that will help you figure it out. 


Principle 1: The Infinite Monkey Theorem

The Argentine writer Jorge Luis Borges had a fascination with a concept known as the infinite monkey theorem. The idea is that if you had an infinite number of monkeys pecking away at an infinite number of typewriters, they would randomly create the collected works of Tolstoy and every other masterwork ever written (or that could be written).

Clearly, today we are living in our own version of the infinite monkey theorem, which is what Strogatz was getting at in his Atlantic piece. If you can have infinite agents attacking every aspect of an age-old problem, solving it over a period of hours, what is the value of a mathematician’s work? Is it even worth pursuing? 

Borges, in his story The Library of Babel, gave us—and mathematicians like Strogatz—some guidance about creating value in a world of infinite monkeys. First, there is the problem of curation. Yes, infinite monkeys could write War and Peace, but good luck finding it amongst all the flotsam and jetsam. We’re seeing the problem in real life today. While AI should theoretically be ramping up productivity, in reality, workers are drowning in workslop. 

The second problem is that of intent. War and Peace isn’t just meaningful for the words it contains, but because of what Leo Tolstoy intended when he wrote them. In much the same way, while it is true that artificial intelligence can run circles around human mathematicians, it is also true that humans chose the problems that OpenAI solved, and they chose them because humans found them meaningful and, possibly, useful. 

So one way that mathematicians—or anyone else—can find meaning and value in the age of AI is by acting as curators and as principal agents. Humans can choose the work that gets done according to what is meaningful to them and other humans. You could see the seeds of that idea in Strogatz’s piece. Even if all problems are solvable, we still need to choose which ones we want to solve. 

Principle 2: The economics of automation

Another way to think about how you find meaning and create value in the age of AI is to analyze the economics of automation. One effective framework involves examining three constituent forces: the displacement effect, the productivity effect, and the reinstatement effect. Together, they can help us determine where threats and opportunities might lie.

The displacement effect refers to jobs that are replaced by an automating technology—in this case, AI. The productivity effect refers to the increase in output per given unit of input due to the technological advance. The reinstatement effect refers to new jobs made possible by a technological advance.

To understand how the three effects work together, it’s helpful to look at the life of Henry Ford. Growing up on a family farm, young Henry would once have been expected to remain in the fields. But as agricultural productivity increased, he had more opportunities beyond the farm. He eventually went to work for Thomas Edison and later created an entirely new automobile industry, reinstating millions of jobs both directly and indirectly.

In the case of Henry Ford, the story was a happy one. The productivity and reinstatement effects dwarfed the displacement effect and everybody, for the most part, was better off. But the AI data is fuzzy. We are certainly seeing early signs of the displacement effect as people lose jobs, but so far we’ve seen few signs of a significant productivity or reinstatement effect. 

Still, understanding the economics of automation can help us find value and meaning. Clearly, with answers to tough problems so much more accessible, mathematicians—and other experts—can help the rest of us formulate good problems to apply to real-world challenges. Some value will likely shift from solving problems to finding good questions. 

3. Jevons Paradox and the O-Ring model

During the Industrial Revolution—which the AI age is often compared to—James Watt’s steam engine used coal much more efficiently than Thomas Newcomen’s version. So, many assumed that coal use would go down. But English economist William Stanley Jevons saw that just the opposite was happening. Coal use was actually increasing. 

Ever since, that phenomenon has been known as the Jevons paradox, the effect that occurs when technological improvements that increase the efficiency of a resource’s use lead to a rise, rather than a fall, in its use. If price elasticity is high enough, the decrease in costs encourages people to find more uses for that resource and demand increases. 

Another useful concept is the O-ring theory of economic development, which gets its name from the Challenger space shuttle disaster. The shuttle, for the most part, was in good working order, but a malfunction in an O-ring seal due to unusually cold weather led to a massive midair explosion. The point is that every job is made up of many interdependent tasks, and if even a single one fails, the entire thing doesn’t work. 

We’re seeing signs of both effects in the AI economy. While software developers can outsource many coding tasks to AI, they still need humans for others, such as communicating and collaborating with other experts on the team. Nevertheless, with code being produced so much more efficiently, job postings for software developers are on the rise. 

Strogatz may find something similar happening in mathematics. After all, there are many facets of his job that don’t involve developing proofs. He is a teacher, a writer, and an incredible translator of complex mathematical concepts for laypeople. Maybe more mathematicians can follow suit,  helping experts in other fields solve important problems with mathematical insights. 

The future of technology is always more human

Technological revolutions are always fraught, with plenty of optimism, anxiety, and visions of doom to go around. A breakthrough proves to be transformational, attracting investment that overestimates the opportunity. Boom leads to bust. Jobs are displaced, then new ones appear and, at some point, we look back and it all seems as if it were preordained. 

Yet when you’re in the midst of disruption, there is little comfort. Yes, things tend to work out in the aggregate, but many are often left worse off. In fact, despite all of the incredible technological advances of the last half-century, a 2018 Pew Research study found that Americans’ real incomes have remained essentially unchanged, with many households far worse off. 

Today, there is no shortage of opinions about where AI is taking us. Tech entrepreneurs claim that we are on the brink of such a productivity boom that it will lead to a new age of abundance—if, of course, it doesn’t kill us. Economists are much more muted, seeing meager gains amid massive overinvestment. Yet for most of us, those macro-trends aren’t nearly as important as how AI will affect us—and our well-being—directly.

The good news is that we have good tools to help us navigate our way through. The infinite monkey theorem highlights the importance of curation and intent. The economics of automation reveals the need for productivity growth to finance reinstatement. The Jevons paradox and the O-ring model encourage us to identify our place within increasingly complex ecosystems. 

AI can give us answers, but it can’t tell us what questions we want to ask. In the future, that’s where human value and meaning will increasingly lie.

#10503. Thanksgiving travelers brace for airline system failures and storms

Traveling for the Thanksgiving holiday is shaping up to be a bit chaotic, following a cascade of glitches that have upended airlines the past week, the threat of storms on both coasts, and soaring airfares.

No wonder Americans are feeling jittery going into the busiest travel week of the year.

On top of all that, there’s concern that a big storm brewing along the East Coast could ground the big balloons in the 100th Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade.

Flight delays and cancellations

The biggest unknown for travelers is whether airlines will be upended again by new system failures.

First there was the Federal Aviation Administration’s network malfunction setting off the longest nationwide ground stop in 25 years. Then last week, both United Airlines and Delta Air Lines saw their own outages, leading to hundreds of flight delays.

Delta said Monday that its operations were stable Monday, a day after over 1,000 of its flights were delayed after an automated system that calculates aircraft weight malfunctioned.

“Delta teams are focused on keeping the operation moving safely and caring for our customers,” the airline said.

As of Monday morning, the flight-tracking website FlightAware said 7% of Delta’s flights were delayed.

Round-trip domestic tickets for Thanksgiving are up more than one-third from the same time last year, according to estimates made in late October by the travel-booking company Hopper.

Wicked weather on both coasts

Holiday travelers can expect a rainy week on the East and West coasts and dry conditions in the nation’s midsection, said Joe Wegman, a meteorologist with the National Weather Service’s Weather Prediction Center.

Many eyes are on an East Coast system. Heavy rain and high winds are expected to start Wednesday night along the coast of the Carolinas, hit the New York City area on Thanksgiving Day, and reach Maine by Friday, he said.

The windy forecast was causing concerns about whether the floats will be able to fly during the 100th Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade in New York. City law prohibits Macy’s from flying full-size balloons if sustained winds exceed 23 mph (37 kph) or wind gusts are over 35 mph (56 kph).

The weather service said it expected the rain in New York to hold off until after the parade ends, but winds could be an issue.

“This is poor timing with the storm coming up on Thanksgiving Day,” Wegman said. “Perhaps better timing that it’s happening on Thanksgiving Day rather on any of the travel days before and after.”

Rainy and windy weather was also expected on the West Coast. Moderate to major coastal flooding was forecast from Washington state to Southern California because of possible record-breaking king tides.

Thanksgiving snow for some in the U.S.

Rain was forecast for the Pacific Northwest on Monday and expected to spread to northern and central California by Wednesday morning, with snow possible in higher elevations, the weather service said.

Snow was forecast Monday for parts of the Rockies, northern Michigan, western New York state, and northwestern Pennsylvania.

“Across the U.S., certainly check with your local airports and airlines for any possible delays,” Wegman said. “I think weather won’t be a significant factor except on Thanksgiving Day itself, and if there are any lingering impacts into Friday morning perhaps from delays that happen on Thanksgiving Day.”

—By John Seewer, Associated Press

Associated Press reporters Kathy McCormack and Dave Collins contributed to this story.

#10501. How fast-food workers are being ranked and reprimanded with AI

Corrin Kearney had been delivering pizzas for a Domino’s in North Texas for a few months when their store asked them to install a new app on their phone and keep it open during their shift. Soon, Kearney learned the app was tracking and scoring every delivery route they drove, with the results displayed on a leaderboard back at the store for the whole crew to see.

The app uses technology from Drivosity, a company that sells what it calls “cutting-edge GPS and AI” driver-monitoring to fast-food chains, including Domino’s and Papa John’s. Each trip is scored from 0 to 100 based on factors including acceleration, braking, cornering, and speed. According to Kearney, drivers at their store are expected to keep their scores above 85. Anyone below that score is written up by managers and eventually locked out of the Domino’s system. “Drivosity can effectively fire people,” Kearney said.

As a driver captain, Kearney has to write up other drivers, even though they have found the app doesn’t recognize the nuance of many real-world situations. Drivers can be penalized for sudden braking to avoid a collision or rapid acceleration to merge on a highway, forcing them to choose between risking an accident or getting points docked, according to Kearney. “It’s infuriating,” Kearney said. “The AI can tell you you haven’t driven well enough and you get fired.” 

Drivosity also sells coaching and analytics features, including HustleTime, which measures how fast employees walk from the store to their car, how long they take handing the pizza to a customer, and how long they take walking back. In Drivosity’s promotional videos, franchise owners and managers at Domino’s and Papa John’s praise HustleTime’s ability to track workers in real time, down to the second. Stores can “gamify” this, according to the company website, by having workers compete for better scores. 

Domino’s and Drivosity did not respond to requests for comment. 

Fast-food companies are increasingly using AI-powered technology to turn worker behavior—how fast you drive, how warmly you greet a customer, how long you spend in the bathroom—into data that can score, rank, discipline, and manage employees. To understand how these AI-driven tools are impacting fast-food workers, Capital & Main interviewed over a dozen workers, researchers, and labor advocates, who described error-prone technologies being deployed at a rapid pace, without the consent or awareness of staff, even as their pay and promotions are increasingly shaped by AI-enabled monitoring. 

“They basically use their population of workers as guinea pigs,” Alexandra Mateescu, a researcher at the nonprofit Data & Society, said of the way that fast-food companies are deploying these new tools.

* * *

In February, Burger King announced that an OpenAI-powered assistant named Patty was being rolled out at about 500 U.S. restaurants, with plans to reach all U.S. stores by the end of 2026. A few weeks later, the tool arrived at the Burger King in Niagara Falls, New York, where Dante Vasquez took drive-thru orders. 

“It started with a little AI voice in our headset,” said Vasquez.

Then the monitoring features started. Each drive-thru conversation was scored on friendliness and upselling, with the numbers displayed on a screen in front of Vasquez as he worked. Anyone could click on a transaction to hear a recording and review the AI-generated transcripts. Green boxes marked what he’d done well — a “Good morning!” or “You rule!” Red boxes marked what Patty counted against him: points off for saying “please hold,” or “um,” or not speaking clearly. 

Managers watched the scores closely, constantly reminding the crew to stay above the 70% friendliness target, Vasquez said. Workers who scored low were cut from the schedule, he added. 

Vasquez learned to speak the way that Patty wanted. He got so good at it that customers started asking if he was a bot. His co-workers nicknamed him “Gemini” [Google’s AI assistant].

“The system wants me to talk like a robot, so I’m talking like a robot,” Vasquez said. “It just gets so mentally degrading.”

Burger King has said publicly that the system was not designed to evaluate individual employees. But Capital & Main reviewed screenshots from a Burger King training module, and images of the dashboard in use, showing individual drive-thru conversations time-stamped and marked for tone and upselling. Vasquez, and other Burger King employees, some of whom spoke to Capital & Main on the condition of anonymity for fear of retaliation, said it was easy to identify and discipline individual employees for low scores. 

Later, Patty started scoring workers on “warmth,” a metric Vasquez couldn’t make sense of. When he asked his district manager how it was calculated, he recalled, she told him to just talk “like a warm blanket.” 

According to Vasquez, Patty seemed to penalize his Black co-workers more than others for what the system flagged as “improper tone.” Mayu Tobin-Miyaji, counsel at the Electronic Privacy Information Center, said that scoring workers on warmth or friendliness is “sort of pseudoscience” and that those tools risk penalizing workers for their accents or disabilities. 

Asked to respond to these concerns, Kristen Viersen, Burger King’s director of communications, emailed Capital & Main a company statement that said, “We have never before heard any example similar to the questions you raised about team members being penalized based on race, gender, ethnicity, or accent.” Burger King declined to answer when asked if the company had tested these features for bias. 

The new monitoring features were intended “to celebrate great examples of friendly service” and allow managers to coach their team, according to the statement, which also said that Burger King has since disabled both features “for further improvements.” Burger King would not say when the features were disabled, and workers said the monitoring was active through August of this year.

Co-workers at Vasquez’s restaurant joked that Vasquez would soon be gone, replaced by Patty entirely, a looming threat that was amplified by a sign in the store that reminded workers that Patty was being trained on every headset conversation. Eventually, the stress just wasn’t worth it. 

He quit in August.

 “If Patty wasn’t introduced,” Vasquez said, “I would still be working at Burger King.”

* * *

While large chains have struck high-profile deals with major AI companies—Yum Brands, which owns Taco Bell, KFC, and Habit Burger & Grill, announced last year that it was deploying Nvidia-powered computer vision for “back-of-house labor management through real-time analytics and alerts”—a crowd of smaller vendors sells the same kind of AI monitoring to individual franchisees, with far less visibility.

Among the more established vendors is Solink, an Ottawa-based company backed by Goldman Sachs that describes itself as “the leader in AI-driven video intelligence.” It works with franchisees of Domino’s, Burger King, McDonald’s, Jack in the Box, Five Guys, and other chains. 

Solink’s product documentation describes a video-surveillance system capable of flagging workers who arrive late or leave early, take too long on their breaks, fail to wear a hairnet or the correct uniform, use their phones while on the clock, or simply stand idle. The software can layer onto a restaurant’s existing security cameras, and it lets managers type in prompts to find footage of specific behaviors or set up automatic alerts.

In a statement, a Solink spokesperson, who declined to be named, said the company’s platform helps businesses “improve operations, sales, safety, and customer experience.” It also stressed that Solink “does not offer identification tools such as biometrics or facial recognition,” and that its terms require customers to comply with the law.

Data & Society’s Mateescu said these tools are rarely marketed as worker surveillance. Instead they are sold as “loss prevention” or “workflow management” tools. This language, she said, obscures what the technology actually does “both to employees and to consumers.”

* * *

While some companies use corporate euphemisms to obscure worker monitoring, others are more direct. MashMore, a new potato-themed fast-food restaurant in Albany, New York, is explicit about its AI use. Using commercially available AI models, its co-founders built an HR chatbot, named Mashy after the store’s mascot, that schedules, scores, and ranks all employees.

Mashy runs on a stream of data employees generate as they work. Workers clock into routines, complete assigned “quests,” and upload photos proving that tasks are completed. In exchange, they earn “Mashy points,” which set their level in the restaurant’s ranking system and can determine bonuses and promotions.

When co-founder and CEO Andy Gor let Capital & Main interact with the chatbot, it didn’t hesitate about who the employee of the month should be. “Shontia Thomas, and it genuinely isn’t a contest,” the chatbot wrote on a screen that Gor shared with a reporter. Gor said the system would never be used to fire an employee, but the chatbot had created rankings for all workers, including those with the lowest scores. 

Gor wants to make every routine, cleaning task and customer-service expectation tracked, scored, and eventually copied from one location to another, so he can check anything from anywhere. “I’m in China right now,” he said. “I can see that during rush hour they cleaned the table nicely. Today, they pass.”

MashMore currently has just one location, but Gor wants to franchise fast. His pitch to investors is that the AI tool can manage employees across dozens of locations. Mashy, Gor said, is “our boss. Basically I’m working for him, too.”

* * *

Efforts to rein in algorithmic management have mostly stalled at the federal level. In California, Gov. Gavin Newsom signed a package of first-in-the-nation workplace-AI laws on September 30. SB 947, the No Robo Bosses Act, bars employers from relying solely on AI to fire or discipline workers. AB 1883 prohibits tools that use AI to infer an employee’s emotional state or collect “neural data.” SB 951 requires employers to notify workers when AI is behind a mass layoff.

Tobin-Miyaji said in an email that the emotion-scoring ban would likely cover a tool like Patty—but that No Robo Bosses could be satisfied by “cursory” human oversight, leaving workers “constantly surveilled and quantified.” 

Unions could in theory negotiate stricter protections than legislation might afford. In 2023, workers in California won the creation of a Fast Food Council, a state body empowered by law to set wages and weigh in on working conditions across the industry. But despite worker efforts to apply pressure to Gov. Newsom, the council currently lacks a chairperson and has not met for over a year. A spokesperson for the council, Charles Hamilton, told Capital & Main that “AI-powered employee monitoring and surveillance has not been presented to the council as an agenda item.”

Some workers have tried to head off the technology before it arrives. Sergio Ceja has worked in the kitchen of an Oakland Burger King for four years. He first heard about Burger King’s AI from his wife, who works at a different location. After her store brought in an AI drive-thru ordering system, she told him, several workers were laid off. 

When Ceja raised his concerns about AI with his managers, he said they told him it was the owner’s call whether to bring in the new tech. Ceja and his co-workers agreed among themselves that if Patty ever came to their store, they would walk out. So far, it hasn’t. Management told them that they don’t plan to bring it in, but that in the future they may need to in order to lift sales and keep the store from closing.

Ceja’s worry wasn’t only about layoffs. It was about the kind of job that would be left if he stayed. “You’ll no longer have a co-worker,” he said through an interpreter. “You’ll have the machine.”

—By Z Waite

This piece was originally published by Capital & Main.

#10499. Abdul El-Sayed rebranded ‘Medicare for All’ to get Trump’s attention. It worked

Democratic Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed of Michigan campaigned on passing “Medicare for All,” a universal single-payer national health insurance system. He believes in the initiative so much that he’s even willing to rebrand it to an unlikely name.

In mid-November, El Sayed posted an image on social media of a gold card emblazoned with the name “TrumpCare.” The mock-up was a convincing depiction of what a TrumpCare insurance card might look like. Trump’s name and signature are in the bottom right-hand corner, and there’s a presidential-style seal as well as the tagline “Great Health Care for Every American.”

“Donald, this is on the table. Guarantee healthcare for every American, from cradle to grave,” El-Sayed wrote on X on November 12.

The card was designed to get President Donald Trump’s attention—and it did. On Saturday, Trump reshared a screenshot of the card on his social network, Truth Social, without adding a comment of his own. Vice President JD Vance’s office reached out to El-Sayed’s team to set up a meeting, NBC News reported Sunday.

Republicans and others learned long ago that the key to Trump’s attention and heart is flattery, especially when it comes to design and branding. There are already limited-edition Trump passports, the “Trump Gold Card” for visas, and “TrumpRx,” a prescription drug website. Proposed children’s investment accounts—originally called “Money Accounts for Growth and Advancement” (for “MAGA”)—were renamed “Trump Accounts” in order to secure a spot in last year’s One Big Beautiful Bill Act. Even architectural firms that submitted a proposal to Trump’s Department of Transportation for a new terminal at Dulles Airport just outside Washington, D.C., slapped Trump’s name on it in their renderings.

El-Sayed’s proposal to turn Medicare for All policy into pictures that Trump understands is part of a larger Democratic playbook. Recall when New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani brought a mock-up of a New York Daily News front page—headlined “Trump to City: Let’s Build”—to the Oval Office to pitch Trump on federal grants for housing construction in the city.

The senator-elect’s rebranded proposal shows that Democrats have picked up on how to win over Trump’s favor when it comes to passing their agenda: Meet him where he’s at. After floating the idea of “TrumpCare” in an Instagram video last week as a way for the president to rebound from low approval ratings, El-Sayed went on NBC’s Meet the Press on Sunday and called the plan “the ultimate Trump card.”

Medicare for All is popular, but winning over Trump and Republicans is key to actually getting a proposal passed. An October KFF poll found 66% of U.S. adults “strongly” or “somewhat support” a national health plan, but it’s far more popular among Democrats (84%) and independents (72%) than it is among Republicans (34%). Trump has previously said Medicare for All “doesn’t work” and called it “a socialist takeover of our healthcare system.”

Though Democrats will have control of the next Congress beginning in January, Trump will still have the power of the veto to block legislation. To override that veto, Democrats will need Republican support to reach a total of 290 votes in the House and 67 votes in the Senate. Or they could avoid a veto altogether by getting Trump on board. A shiny gold card could be all it takes.

#10496. Making 6 figures? You might still be turning to ‘buy now, pay later,’ according to new research

We are a nation united in our desire to pay tomorrow for what we’re buying today.

In the past decade, various deferred-payment options have grown in popularity, and new data from Block might buck the notion of who the “typical” customer is. Once favored by younger shoppers and those consumers with limited access to credit, these options are actually more likely to be used today by households earning more than $100,000 per year, according to the Oakland, California-based fintech company.

“Buy now, pay later” (BNPL), or “checkout financing,” is most popular among American adults ages 18 to 59, with 66% reporting they used a deferred-payment option in the past year, according to a survey by Block. The study also found that BNPL use climbs with income: 69% of households earning over $100,000 used it in the past 12 months, compared with 65% earning $50,000 to $100,000 and 61% earning less than $50,000.

But it’s not just traditional BNPL that’s popular among high-earning Americans. Block’s study found that four of seven different deferred-payment options were more commonly used by the highest earners, including post-purchase payment splitting and installment plans offered directly by a store, service provider, or lender.

Though Block has a dog in the race—it owns Afterpay and Cash App, both of which offer deferred-payment options—the company contends that the growing demand for flexible payments isn’t necessarily a sign that people are spending beyond their means. Rather, Block found that 72% of users financed at least one expense in the past year that they technically had the cash to cover, but would have felt like a strain to pay up front. Another 47% opted to spread out the payments to keep money set aside for savings, investments, or other priorities.

The report shows how people weigh the trade-offs of the cost of financing versus keeping cash in their accounts. “People don’t look at their checking balance and think of it all as money they ⁠can spend,” Juan Hernandez, head of credit and underwriting at Block, told Reuters. “Some of it is already set aside ​for rent, some is for the next bill, and some they want to keep in case something ​goes wrong.”

FINANCING SHOWS STRAIN

Even so, some of the Block data underscores the strain that many Americans are feeling amid the current affordability crisis that’s seen people struggle to pay for everyday expenses. That’s important, as consumer spending accounts for roughly two-thirds of gross domestic product. And while Americans have largely weathered the crisis, one measure of consumer confidence recently fell to its lowest level since 2014. 

The Block study found that people are using deferred-payment methods to finance everyday purchases, like buying groceries, filling up at the gas station, or paying utility bills. To cover these basics, cash advances, earned wage access, and short-term loans accounted for 58% of financed transactions.

Even though a vast majority of consumers—82% for essentials and 73% for bills—reported repayment timelines within six weeks, the data still shows that many people are struggling to keep pace with their day-to-day expenses.

Such data underscores the findings of a recent survey of 5,166 U.S. adults by the Pew Research Center: 27% said they can’t pay some of their monthly bills—or can only afford to make partial payments.

The so-called blue tsunami that saw Democrats take control of both the Senate and House of Representatives in the midterm elections also gave these politicians a mandate to address affordability. And New York Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, the current speaker-designate of the House, indicated last week that this will be among the priorities for the new Congress come January.

“We’re going to relentlessly fight for an affordable America,” Jeffries said in a press conference. “We’re going to work hard to drive down the high cost of groceries and gas and housing.”

#10494. Spotify Wrapped 2026 is almost here. How to make sure you’re ready

It’s that time of year again, when people flood social media with year-end reviews and music lovers start counting down to what may be the most anticipated of them all: Spotify Wrapped.

Spotify Wrapped 2026 reveals not just your top five songs (you know, those embarrassing tunes you’ve got on repeat), it also stacks your minutes and musical tastes up against the general public—for better or worse. (In past years, it also revealed listeners’ most-streamed songs, favorite artists, and podcasts.)

Last year, some 500 million users shared their stories on social media in the first 24 hours, an overall increase of 41% year over year from 2025.

When Does Spotify Wrapped drop?

While the company hasn’t officially announced the release date for Spotify Wrapped 2026, the streaming platform’s past history reveals some good clues about when to expect it—and that date is coming up fast.

Last year, Spotify Wrapped dropped soon after Thanksgiving, on December 3. In 2024, it came out one day later, on December 4. Before that, in 2023 and 2022, it came out November 29 and 30, respectively, near Thanksgiving weekend.

However, since 2021, Spotify Wrapped has released on a Wednesday, often right after Thanksgiving, which this year falls on December 2.

A look at online prediction market Kalshi shows traders giving Spotify Wrapped 2026 only a 21% chance of dropping before December 2, but forecasting an 88% chance before December 3, and a 90% chance before December 4.

How can I see Spotify Wrapped once it releases?

Spotify listeners can view their personalized recap in Spotify’s mobile app and share results on social media platforms such as Instagram and TikTok.

To get the updated app, scan the QR code to download Spotify.

#10493. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.

#10491. Everything that led to Jensen Huang being reinstated as Nvidia’s CEO

Jensen Huang is back as CEO of Nvidia, just 11 days after his closely watched exit from the company he cofounded—even as he remains mired in controversy. 

In an announcement this morning, the Nvidia board claimed to be “satisfied with respect to Mr. Huang’s candor and ability to lead Nvidia,” following a number of meetings with the newly reinstated CEO. The chairman said the board “believes Jensen is the right person to lead Nvidia,” and that his return atop the company is “in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers.” 

There was little mention of the circumstances that led to Huang’s departure, aside from an assurance that the board had conferred with senior government officials and are now “confident that Mr. Huang’s reinstatement poses no national security risk.” The announcement noted that an independent investigation is still underway to probe the fallout from Huang’s extramarital affair with an alleged Chinese spy, and that Huang intended to cooperate. 

Huang’s reinstatement is not entirely a surprise (though it was apparently a surprise to interim CEO Colette Kress, who was not consulted on the decision, per a Bloomberg report). By the board’s own admission, the decision seems to have been influenced by President Donald Trump, who has remained a steadfast supporter of Huang throughout the debacle. But Trump was more explicit about his role in a CNBC interview today, revealing that he spoke directly to the company’s board. “And I convinced them,” he said. “I made the deal.” Trump also noted, “We have to protect our geniuses.”

As Fast Company reported earlier today, Huang’s return underscores his influence as a key player in the AI boom (not to mention his cozy relationship with Trump). Since Huang resigned, there has been a steady drumbeat of concern from the tech community, with prominent figures like Elon Musk and former Uber CEO Travis Kalanick arguing that he should be reinstated. 

At the New York Times DealBook summit last week, Musk argued, “There’s no one that can really replace Jensen,” and likened his resignation to “kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak.” (Other leaders at the conference also spoke highly of Huang, from Sam Altman to Anduril’s Palmer Luckey.) In a recent appearance, Kalanick drew parallels to his own experience being ousted from Uber and said, “We gotta bring Jensen back.” Y Combinator cofounder Paul Graham boldly asserted, “I’ve never known the tech world to be more unanimous about anything than they are about Jensen Huang.”

Despite allegations of multiple inappropriate relationships with his own employees—including a direct report—apparently plenty of Nvidia staffers also expressed solidarity with Huang. 

A Wired report last week revealed that some employees immediately urged the board to bring Huang back in internal posts. A widely circulated petition to reinstate Huang as CEO drew more than 200,000 signatures, of which more than 10,000 were from current Nvidia employees—about a quarter of the company’s workforce. Musk, Kalanick, and a handful of other CEOs and business leaders signed the petition.

It didn’t take long for Huang to plead his own case. 

“I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from Nvidia’s employees, investors, business partners and customers for me to return as CEO,” he said last week, hours before the company’s first earnings call without him. “If the board of directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO.” He also acknowledged that he “did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at Nvidia” and had made “serious mistakes” in his personal life. At the time, the board continued to insist that there was no plan to bring him back.

But now that Huang is at the helm again, is it back to business as usual? There are all kinds of questions about what his return could mean for Nvidia, and not just in terms of the serious national security concerns raised by his indiscretions. 

The board is still conducting an investigation into the events that led to Huang’s exit, but it could be little more than a formality if he has already been reinstated. (Or perhaps the board will find itself in yet another bind when that investigation surfaces new information.) 

While the board now claims to be convinced of Huang’s candor, it was quite the opposite just two weeks ago, per a Wired report, when his “lack of candor” and “lying” apparently pushed the board to remove him. As Fast Company previously reported, boards usually have little tolerance for CEOs pursuing romantic relationships with their subordinates (and the stock price often takes a dip following such revelations). 

Nvidia also has an unusually flat organizational structure, in which Huang has 60 direct reports, an arrangement that seems to have left the company without a succession plan—while making its CEO even more powerful. 

A New York Times investigation last week raised further concerns over Huang’s leadership at Nvidia, which seems to have created a culture of fear at the company. While many employees remain loyal to Huang—and even seem to revere him—others claim to have experienced ritual humiliation under his rule. More than a dozen former and current employees said Huang frequently used insults and personal attacks to motivate his staff. 

According to The Times, Huang could be particularly creative with his insults, telling an executive that his mother should have aborted him, and in another instance threatening to rip off an employee’s head and “pee on your brain.” 

The report found that Huang’s management style had sparked regular HR complaints—which some employees said weren’t adequately addressed—and that a former HR leader had been fired for calling attention to Huang’s conduct, which he characterized as “the most verbally abusive, threatening and intimidating behavior” that he had seen in his career. (The former employee threatened to sue but ultimately reached a settlement with Nvidia for more than $1 million, The Times reported.)

At the moment, it’s not clear how or whether Nvidia plans to address these issues, along with Huang’s other transgressions. His statement today stops short of introspection or insight into what—if anything—could change upon his return. 

“I am honored to return to Nvidia and lead one of the most consequential technology companies in the world at the beginning of a new industrial revolution,” Huang said in his statement. “I take this responsibility with the greatest humility and renewed commitment.”

It remains to be seen whether Huang will approach the job any differently. In the meantime, since news of his return broke this morning, Nvidia stock is up over 30%.

#10489. Who gets to decide what ‘bad things’ we should accept about AI?

There has been intense debate about the threat AI poses to humanity and what should be done about it. The voices have piled on: a former Anthropic researcher who said AI companies are “gambling with our lives,” prominent tech executives including Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calling for an AI slowdown, politicians refusing to back down from the AI arms race with China, and investors dismissing it all as hysteria standing in the way of “the largest wealth-creation event of our lifetimes.” 

Then, Sam Altman said out loud what they’ve all been dancing around: “We believe the world should accept some bad things happening for the benefits of this technology.” 

Altman isn’t alone in this belief. The AI industry as a whole wants the world to trust that the benefits will be worth the “bad things” that happen, even as companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations despite their own warnings. Those decisions are being shaped largely by a small, powerful group of men. 

Our research shows the problem goes well beyond a few CEOs. Over the past several months, our team at the Shorenstein Center at Harvard Kennedy School studied how AI is used, experienced, and understood by men and women in the U.S. We found that women are underrepresented across many of the critical places where AI is developed, adopted, and discussed.

Start with coverage. In our analysis of more than 78,000 news articles about AI over the past year, men were named in AI headlines 14.6 times as often as women and cited as experts 4.3 times as often. Part of that reflects who leads AI companies, but the gap still holds after we removed executives, founders, officials, and investors from the analysis. The people arguing the loudest about AI and shaping which views get bolstered as credible are a narrow slice of the country, while the rest of us live with the consequences of what they decide.

Women, the ones more likely to be wary about AI, are largely missing from the debate over which risks to accept. Our research finds meaningful differences between women and men in how they use and view it. Our survey of more than 5,000 U.S. adults finds women consistently report they use AI less, feel less confident using AI, see less value in it, and pay less attention to developments in AI. At work, women are less likely to receive AI training, mentorship, and encouragement, even though each is linked to more use, confidence, and perceived value for both women and men.

Trust in AI is low across genders, and lower among women, who report more worry and anxiety about AI. Men report more curiosity, optimism, and excitement. Both genders say men’s perspectives and needs are better represented in AI development and in AI-generated content. 

Browsing data shows a similar pattern. The share of women and men who have never used AI has nearly converged, but among people who use AI, men are more likely to use chatbots on a daily basis. If AI companies are building products around their heaviest consumers, AI is being optimized for men. The men using AI may see and reap the benefits, but women will also have to share the risks, whether or not they choose to use the technology. 

Many women are already working on these problems, and have been for years. Joy Buolamwini and Timnit Gebru showed in 2018 that commercial facial analysis systems failed far more often on darker-skinned women. Gebru has since argued that the AI extinction warnings distract from harms AI is already causing. Fei-Fei Li has said she is tired of a debate stuck between doomsday and utopia. Mira Murati has built her company around the practical question of how to keep humans in the loop instead of automating them out.

Their questions are more specific than whether AI will save or end us, yet the coverage has centered on extinction versus opportunity. Who gets named and quoted may be part of the reason.

History shows who often pays for that trade-off when women are left out of the design and key decisions of new technologies: Crash test dummies modeled on male bodies put women at greater risk for serious injury, and clinical trials focused on men led to unsafe drug dosages for women. AI repeats those gaps because it learns from the same skewed data where women are left out. Amazon had to scrap a hiring tool that penalized résumés containing the word women, and AI tools for screening liver disease were twice as likely to miss the disease in women. 

Altman says the world should accept some “bad things” to reap the benefits of AI. Women—who trust AI less, have paid for its many failures, and have long worked to fix them—may have a different cost-benefit calculation. Nobody is asking them, and it’s time we start.

#10478. Securing FastAPI Applications: JWT Authentication and Rate Limiting with Redis

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a modern web API is only half the battle. Ensuring it is secure, resilient, and capable of handling malicious traffic is what separates a hobby project from a production-ready service. When building highly scalable applications, we typically lean on stateless architectures. However, features like token revocation and rate limiting require state.

In this guide, we will explore the architectural concepts behind building a secure API using FastAPI, Redis, JSON Web Tokens (JWT), and SlowAPI. We will break down the theory, the tech stack, and the flow of data—without getting bogged down in the code.

Before diving into the architecture, it is crucial to understand the role each tool plays in our security model:

Security starts with how you manage your secrets. The first step in our architectural journey is setting up a configuration file and a local environment file.

Hardcoding secret keys, database URLs, or algorithm types directly into your application is a severe security risk. Instead, we load these from environment variables. The application uses a settings manager to read variables like the Redis host, port, the JWT secret key, the algorithm (typically HS256), and the token expiration time. This ensures that sensitive data stays out of your version control system.

To handle rate limiting and token tracking, we need a connection to Redis. Because FastAPI is inherently asynchronous, we initialize an asynchronous Redis client.

Redis acts as the central state-holder for our API. By using Redis, we ensure that even if our API is running on five different servers behind a load balancer, the rate limits and the token blacklists remain globally synchronized.

Next, we establish the utility functions that handle the cryptographic heavy lifting. This step is divided into two main parts:

Here, we initialize the FastAPI application and attach slowapi.

slowapi needs a way to identify the client making the request. By default, it uses the client's IP address. We attach the limiter to the application state and register an exception handler. This ensures that if a user breaches the rate limit, the API gracefully returns a "429 Too Many Requests" error instead of crashing.

We also set up a dependency that acts as a security guard for our protected routes. This dependency intercepts incoming requests, extracts the token, and decodes it. It then checks Redis to see if the token has been blacklisted (for example, if the user logged out before the token expired). If the token is valid and not blacklisted, the request proceeds; otherwise, a "401 Unauthorized" error is thrown.

The login endpoint is where the magic happens. When a user submits their credentials, the API verifies the username and password. In a production scenario, this involves querying a database; in our example, we verify it against a hardcoded credential.

Once the credentials are validated, the API generates a JWT. To ensure we can invalidate this token later if needed, we store the token in Redis with a time-to-live (TTL) that matches the token's expiration time. Finally, the token is returned to the client, which will store it and send it in the Authorization header of future requests.

Finally, we create a route that requires both authentication and rate limiting.

Before the route's logic even executes, two things happen:

If the client passes both security checks, they are granted access to the highly secure data provided by the route.

To validate this system, you would conceptually walk through several test scenarios. First, you test the login flow with correct and incorrect credentials to ensure tokens are only issued to valid users.

Next, you test the rate limit by rapidly firing requests at the protected route to ensure the "429 Too Many Requests" response triggers. Finally, you test the security guard by trying to access the route with no token, an expired token, and a manually blacklisted token to ensure the API correctly denies access in all scenarios.

Building a secure API requires layering defenses. By combining FastAPI's speed, Redis's fast state management, JWT's stateless authentication, and slowapi's rate-limiting capabilities, you create a system that is both robust and scalable.

While this foundational text explains the core concepts, moving to production requires adding token refresh flows, implementing per-user rate limiting (rather than just IP-based), deploying Redis with high availability, and ensuring your secrets are managed by a secure vault. These practices will ensure your API remains secure as your user base grows.

#10476. Chess.com’s next move is a new hub for classic games, from mahjong to sudoku

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

AI changed the game at Chess.com, helping it expand to 11 other classics this year.﻿

AI changed the game at Chess.com, helping it expand to 11 other classics this year.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Chess.com is ready to go a lot bigger than chess. After launching Gambit, a gambling-free poker site, earlier this year, the company is now adding 10 more games: backgammon, checkers, go, hearts, mahjong, shogi, sudoku, solitaire, xiangqi, and a Scrabble alternative called word. They’re all part of Chess’s new Classic Games platform, which is launching its public beta on Saturday on the web and through a new app for iOS and Android.

“Our cofounders have had many people come up to them and say, ‘Hey, I run a mahjong night on Wednesdays’ or ‘I go to this local bar in Chicago with like five people, and we love playing go, but we lack a Chess.com,’” chief growth officer Albert Cheng says in an interview with The Verge. “The way that Chess.com has grown this community of almost 300 million members has not really happened for a lot of these other types of classic games.”

Classic Games is aimed at filling that gap. Some of the new games, like shoji or xiangqi, are strategy games similar to chess while others are further afield, like sudoku, solitaire, and word. The Classic team chose the new games through “a mix of community feedback, market research, and then just our own confidence in our ability to serve that game well,” Cheng says. It’s focused on classics that “have some sort of lifelong journey of learning and a community.”

Classic Games will feel familiar if you’ve ever played a match on Chess.com. Each game has lessons, leaderboards, and a rating system. Multiplayer games like checkers or mahjong have options for matches against other players or bots, and they use the same bot characters that Chess.com has. (Martin remains the lowest-rated bot across the board, whether it’s on Chess, Gambit, or Classic.) Not all of the games have the same play modes — word and mahjong have puzzles like chess, while sudoku has a set of daily grids and solitaire has daily deals.

The expansion hasn’t put Chess.com itself on the back burner, according to Cheng. If anything, it’s never been better. “Chess is more and more so in the public zeitgeist than I’ve ever seen it, frankly, during my time here,” Cheng says. “Our daily active users are higher than they’ve ever been during the company’s history.” He declined to share exactly how many daily users Chess.com currently has but said the site is “doing amazingly well.”

So, while the company is expanding beyond its domain name, chess remains its primary focus. “The amount of folks that are fully dedicated toward building new games is less than 1 percent of the company,” according to Cheng.

AI played a key role in helping Chess.com launch 11 new games in a matter of months with a team of only a few people. Earlier this year, Cheng told The Verge his team built Gambit with the help of AI, mainly for coding tasks. He says they used “almost entirely the same approach” for Classic, with AI helping to speed up the coding process again.

“Because these games are built on top of the Chess.com code base and design system and bot characters and rating systems, it’s a huge advantage and allows us to move very quickly alongside AI to bring these types of games to market with a very small team,” Cheng says. “As we create more and more games, the adjacent games that can be created to it just happen much faster because the infrastructure is there, as opposed to standing everything up from scratch.”

AI wasn’t used for Classic Games’ art and design, Cheng says. “The look and feel of it is very carefully crafted by humans, and we’re proud of that,” he says. “The truth is, it really is a blend. It’s a small team of really talented folks that have good product and design judgment, and they’re leveraging AI.”

“We are fortunate in some ways that AI and chess have been intermixed for 30-ish years.”

Chess.com’s comfort with AI may come from the fact that human chess players have now spent decades playing and learning alongside machines. “We are fortunate in some ways that AI and chess have been intermixed for 30-ish years,” Cheng says, calling back to Garry Kasparov’s 1996 chess match against IBM’s Deep Blue supercomputer. “Even before the company was founded 20 years ago, the idea that AI was super powerful at the game of chess was embedded in how the cofounders thought about the company.”

Elsewhere in the games industry, there’s been increasing tension over the use of AI. Cheng says that while Chess.com may be using AI to “accelerate” coding tasks, the creative side of things has become more important. “On the art and the design side, the ratios are shifting a decent amount, where we’re hiring more of those folks,” he says, adding that the company has “shipped a lot more major features in Chess.com than in previous years” using what they’ve learned from Classic Games’ faster development process.

Chess.com might not be done growing its library of games, either. Cheng said it will likely expand to “a lot more” games in the future.

Reddit user ShweetsStuff discovered the songs used during a Grand Theft Auto-themed section. They found several audio files with Suno metadata that pointed back to the tracks on Suno’s site.

Creator Edmund McMillen took to X to apologize and claimed he “had no idea” the in-game GTA parody used generative AI music. Adding to the drama, Valve requires developers to disclose how and when they use generative AI in the development process, and Nicalis, Inc., the studio behind Repentance Plus, doesn’t appear to have mentioned the AI music anywhere.

#10467. Google Wallet may soon be able to store your Social Security card

Google Wallet is preparing another form of digital ID storage, with support for your Social Security card apparently on the way.

You can already store your passport or select State driver’s licenses in Google Wallet in the US, and it seems Google isn’t stopping there. Wallet now appears to be preparing some level of support for storing a digital copy of your Social Security card, as code in the latest app updates suggest.

There’s zero reason to store your SS card like this. Unnecessary exposure.

The folks over at Android Authority spotted explicit references to “Social Security Number” in Google Play Services v26.46.30 in connection with Google Wallet.

While exact functionality is unclear at this time, this appears to include support for seeing your Social Security benefits, or at least logging those benefits, in a digital form. There are mentions of Retirement, Survivors, and/or Disability Insurance (RSDI), Supplemental Security Income, Medicare, and more, as well as qualifiers for deductions and effective dates.
